Tightness of approximations to the chemical distance metric for simple conformal loop ensembles
Jason Miller

Abstract
. Suppose that Γ \Gamma is a conformal loop ensemble ( CLE κ {\rm CLE}_{\kappa} ) with simple loops ( κ ∈ ( 8 / 3 , 4 ) \kappa\in(8/3,4) ) in a simply connected domain D ⊆ 𝐂 D\subseteq\mathbf{C} whose boundary is itself a type of CLE κ {\rm CLE}_{\kappa} loop. Let Υ \Upsilon be the carpet of Γ \Gamma , i.e., the set of points in D D not surrounded by a loop of Γ \Gamma . We prove that certain approximations to the chemical distance metric in Υ \Upsilon are tight. More precisely, for each path ω : [ 0 , 1 ] → Υ \omega\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon and ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 we let 𝔑 ϵ ​ ( ω ) {\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega) be the Lebesgue measure of the ϵ \epsilon -neighborhood of ω \omega . For z , w ∈ Υ z,w\in\Upsilon we let 𝔡 ϵ ​ ( z , w , Γ ) = inf ω 𝔑 ϵ ​ ( ω ) \mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)=\inf_{\omega}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega) where the infimum is over all paths ω : [ 0 , 1 ] → Υ \omega\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon with ω ⁡ ( 0 ) = z \omega(0)=z , ω ⁡ ( 1 ) = w \omega(1)=w and let 𝔪 ϵ {\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon} be the median of sup z , w ∈ ∂ D 𝔡 ϵ ​ ( z , w , Γ ) \sup_{z,w\in\partial D}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma) . We prove that ( z , w ) ↦ 𝔪 ϵ − 1 ​ 𝔡 ϵ ​ ( z , w , Γ ) (z,w)\mapsto{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma) is tight and that any subsequential limit defines a geodesic metric on Υ \Upsilon which is Hölder continuous with respect to the Euclidean metric. We conjecture that the subsequential limit is unique, conformally covariant, and describes the scaling limit of the chemical distance metric for discrete loop models which converge to CLE κ {\rm CLE}_{\kappa} for κ ∈ ( 8 / 3 , 4 ) \kappa\in(8/3,4) such as the critical Ising model.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Overview

The conformal loop ensemble
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}

[
40
,
42
]
is the canonical conformally invariant measure on loops in a simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
. It is the loop version of the Schramm-Loewner evolution (
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
)
[
39
]
. It consists of a countable collection of loops in
D
each of which looks locally like an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve where
\kappa\in(8/3,8)
. Just like for
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
, the loops are simple and do not intersect each other or the domain boundary for
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
while for
\kappa\in(4,8)
the loops are self-intersecting and intersect the domain boundary. Its importance is that it describes (or is conjectured to describe) the scaling limit of all of the interfaces for a number of discrete models from statistical mechanics in two dimensions. For example, on deterministic planar lattices,
{\rm CLE}_{3}
was shown to be the scaling limit of the interfaces in the Ising model
[
7
,
3
]
,
{\rm CLE}_{16/3}
the interfaces in the FK-Ising model
[
16
,
44
]
,
{\rm CLE}_{6}
the interfaces in the percolation model
[
43
,
5
]
, and
{\rm CLE}_{8}
the Peano curve associated with the uniform spanning tree (UST)
[
20
]
.

The purpose of this article is to initiate the program of constructing a conformally covariant metric associated with a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
. We will focus on the case that
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
so that the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loops are simple and do not intersect each other. Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain and let
\Gamma
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
. Let
\Upsilon
be the carpet of
\Gamma
, i.e., the set of
z\in D
which are not surrounded by any loop of
\Gamma
. For each path
\omega\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon
and
\epsilon>0
, we let
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
be the Lebesgue measure of the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\omega
. For
z,w\in\Upsilon
, we let
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)=\inf_{\omega}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
where the infimum is over all paths
\omega\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon
with
\omega(0)=z
,
\omega(1)=w
. Finally, we let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}
be the median of the random variable
\sup_{z,w\in\partial D}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
. Our aim is to show that
(z,w)\mapsto{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
is tight and that any subsequential limit defines a geodesic metric on
\Upsilon
which is Hölder continuous with respect to the Euclidean metric.

We note that for
z,w\in\Upsilon
which are away from
\partial D
, it is natural to expect that the “shortest” path in
\Upsilon
from
z
to
w
is a fractal curve and that there exists
\alpha>1
so that the median of
\sup_{z,w\in\Upsilon}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
is
\epsilon^{2-\alpha+o(1)}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. If
\partial D
is smooth and
z,w\in\partial D
, then one could take the path which follows
\partial D
from
z
to
w
hence in this case
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{1+o(1)}
. This means that if
\partial D
is smooth then
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}
is the wrong normalization to get a finite metric on
\Upsilon
. For this reason, we will state our main result for a domain with rough boundary. In particular, we will take
\partial D
to be itself a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop.

1.2.
Main result

The main result of this paper is the tightness of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdotp;\Gamma)
, which we note is a function defined on a random set. Let us now specify with respect to which topology we will prove tightness.

We let
\mathbf{K}_{n}
be the set of pairs consisting of a compact subset
K\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{n}
and a function
f\colon K\to\mathbf{R}
. We define a metric on
\mathbf{K}_{n}
as follows. Let
d_{\mathrm{H}}
denote the Hausdorff distance on compact subsets of
\mathbf{R}^{n}
. For
(f,K),(g,L)\in\mathbf{K}_{n}
we let

{\mathbf{d}}_{0,n}((f,K),(g,L))=\sup\{|f(z)-g(w)|:z\in K,w\in L,\ |z-w|\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(K,L)\}.

We note that
{\mathbf{d}}_{0,n}
defines a pseudometric on
\mathbf{K}_{n}
but not a metric. Indeed, if
K
,
L
are distinct compact subsets of
\mathbf{R}^{n}
and
f,g
are the zero function then
{\mathbf{d}}_{0,n}((f,K),(g,L))=0
. To define a metric, we thus set

{\mathbf{d}}_{n}((f,K),(g,L))={\mathbf{d}}_{0,n}((f,K),(g,L))+d_{\mathrm{H}}(K,L).

Fix
R,S>0
. Suppose that
\mathbf{F}
is a family in
\mathbf{K}_{n}
so that:

(i)

for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
so that for every
(f,K)\in\mathbf{F}
,
z,w\in K
, and
|z-w|<\delta
we have that
|f(z)-f(w)|<\epsilon
and

(ii)

for every
(f,K)\in\mathbf{F}
we have that
K\subseteq B(0,R)
and
\sup_{z\in K}|f(z)|\leq S
.

Then the closure of
\mathbf{F}
is compact in
(\mathbf{K}_{n},{\mathbf{d}}_{n})
. Indeed, suppose that
((f_{n},K_{n}))
is a sequence in
\mathbf{F}
. Then there exists a subsequence
((f_{n_{j}},K_{n_{j}}))
so that
(K_{n_{j}})
converges with respect to
d_{\mathrm{H}}
. The usual proof of the Arzela-Ascoli theorem then implies that, by taking a further subsequence if necessary, there exists
(f,K)\in\mathbf{F}
so that
{\mathbf{d}}_{0,n}((f_{n_{j}},K_{n_{j}}),(f,K))\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.

The main argument used in this paper works whenever
\partial D
locally looks like an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve, though we will state our main result with a particular choice for the sake of concreteness (see Figure
1.1
for a numerical simulation of the setup).

Figure 1.1.

Top left:
A
{\rm CLE}_{3}
on the unit disk.
Top right:
The loop which surrounds the origin from the top left (red) with an independent
{\rm CLE}_{3}
in the domain surrounded by it.
Bottom:
The top right with the
{\rm CLE}_{3}
carpet colored according to the chemical distance from a uniformly chosen point in the carpet; the closest points are red and the farthest points are magenta.

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
. Suppose that
\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in the unit disk
\mathbf{D}
and let
D
be the set of points surrounded by the loop
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
which surrounds
0
. Given
\mathcal{L}
, let
\Gamma
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
. Let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}
be the median of
\sup_{z,w\in\partial D}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
. Then the law of the map
(z,w)\mapsto{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
is tight in the space
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
. Any subsequential limit is a.s. a geodesic metric on
\Upsilon
which is Hölder continuous with respect to the Euclidean metric.

We conjecture that the limit in Theorem
1.1
exists and is characterized by a certain list of axioms, as in the case of the Liouville quantum gravity metric
[
23
,
13
]
. We plan to address this in future work. We also conjecture that the limit describes the scaling limit of the chemical distance metric for discrete models which converge to
{\rm CLE}
, such as the critical Ising model
[
3
]
(
\kappa=3
).

1.3.
Outline and proof strategy

Fix
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
. In proving Theorem
1.1
, we will often work in an infinite volume setup which describes the local behavior of
\mathcal{L}
near a “typical” point in
\mathcal{L}
. That is, we will replace
\mathcal{L}
by a so-called two-sided whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
process
\eta
and we let
\Gamma_{+}
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in the component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta
which is to the left of
\eta
. Rather than normalize using the median and quantiles associated with the setup described in Theorem
1.1
, we will instead use the median and quantiles associated with this infinite volume setup and then at the very end of the proof show that they are comparable. In order to distinguish between these two situations, we will use the superscript HP to denote a quantity associated with the infinite volume (half-planar) setup and will not use a superscript when referring to a quantity associated with the setup described in the statement of Theorem
1.1
.

The basic strategy to prove Theorem
1.1
is the following. For each
\epsilon>0
and
p\in(0,1)
we let
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
denote the
p
th quantile of
\sup_{z,w\in\eta([0,1])}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma_{+})
and let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}={{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(1/2)
be the median. Note that for any
M_{0}\geq 1
there exists
M_{1}\geq 1
so that

(1.1)

\frac{1}{M_{1}}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{M_{0}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)\leq M_{1}{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon/M_{0}}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p).

That is, replacing
\epsilon
with a fixed multiple of
\epsilon
changes the quantiles by at most a factor which does not depend on
\epsilon
. (See also Lemma
5.4
for a more quantitative version of (
1.1
).)

The proof is divided into two main steps:

•

Establish the comparability of the quantiles
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
uniformly in
\epsilon>0
and prove the tightness of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
in
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
. We emphasize here that the tightness result will be proved in the finite volume setting but using the normalization from the median defined in the infinite volume setting.

•

Show that every subsequential limit is a.s. positive definite hence a metric.

The geodesic property of a subsequential limit and the comparability of the infinite volume and finite volume quantiles will not be difficult to establish once we have accomplished both of the steps above.

Let
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\kappa\in(4,6)
. In
[
31
]
, it was shown how one can view an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
process
\eta^{\prime}
as a
conformal percolation interface
(CPI) inside of the carpet
\Upsilon
of
\Gamma
which always keeps the loops of
\Gamma
which it hits on its right side. Moreover, the process obtained by following the loops of
\Gamma
which are hit by
\eta^{\prime}
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
process
\eta
. More generally, if we fix
\rho^{\prime}\in[\kappa^{\prime}-6,0]
then there exists a value of
\beta\in[-1,1]
so that we can couple an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime};\kappa-6-\rho^{\prime})
process with
\Upsilon
so that it keeps each loop of
\Gamma
that it hits on its left (resp. right) side with probability
(1-\beta)/2
(resp.
(1+\beta)/2
) and the process obtained by following the loops which are hit by
\eta^{\prime}
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{\beta}(\kappa-6)
process
\eta
. We will often abuse notation and refer to
\eta^{\prime}
together with the loops of
\Gamma
that it hits (i.e.,
\eta
) also as a CPI.

CPIs give us a way to “explore” the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
carpet in a Markovian way so that the conditional law of the loops of
\Gamma
which are in the components of
D\setminus\eta([0,t])
not surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
are independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
’s. This means that we can explore further into each such component using a CPI which can also start from any point on the component boundary. This type of exploration will be the basis for a spatial decomposition of the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
into “chunks”. Since the
shape
of a domain strongly affects the chemical distance metric (e.g., as we explained earlier if the domain boundary is smooth then it is natural to expect that points along the boundary will be close to each other), knowing the shape of the successive chunks of the exploration will restrict the form of the chemical distance metric in the unexplored region. We will thus want to consider a type of exploration which avoids revealing the exact shape of the explored region as much as possible. For this reason, we will explore the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
using CPIs in a “quantum” manner using the relationship between
{\rm SLE}
and Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) developed in
[
41
,
9
]
as well as the relationship between
{\rm CLE}
and LQG developed in
[
33
]
. The reason for this is that exploring a
{\rm CLE}
in a quantum manner is analogous to exploring a random planar map using a peeling process and in such an exploration the regions which are cut out depend only on the exploration through their boundary length. In particular, conditioning on chunks which have been explored this way means that we do not have to condition on their actual shape – only on how they are glued together.

We will call a chunk “good” if it is possible to traverse it with a path
\omega
with
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
and “bad” if it is not. We will prove that by making the chunk size sufficiently small (but with
p\in(0,1)
fixed), we can make the probability that a chunk is good as close to
1
as we wish uniformly in
\epsilon\to 0
. We will explain these estimates in Section
3
. We emphasize that this is the particular point in the proof where we make use of the normalization by
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}
instead of making a choice such as by the median length of a crossing
\omega
which minimizes
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
among those which connect two disjoint boundary arcs. The reason is that we will show that the probability that the diameter of the set of points
z\in\Upsilon_{+}
,
\Upsilon_{+}
the carpet of
\Gamma_{+}
, with
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(0,z;\Gamma_{+})\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
is at least
\delta
tends to
1
as
\delta\to 0
provided
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small relative to
\delta
. The reason that this is the case is that
\eta([0,1])
enjoys certain self-similarity properties. In particular, if there was a positive chance that the aforementioned set was arbitrarily small with positive probability then that would have to be very likely to be true somewhere along
\eta([0,1])
which in turn would contradict the definition of
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
.

We will show that the good vs bad chunks can be viewed as a type of strongly supercritical percolation Section
4
, though in a more general framework. In particular, we will show that it is likely that two points on the domain boundary are connected by a collection of such chunks. The initial estimate that we will obtain here will not have sufficiently good concentration in order to deduce the tightness of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
because the exploration that we will consider has the possibility of hitting obstacles such as large
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loops. In order to obtain stronger concentration, we will consider an adaptive version which circumvents these obstacles.

Using the definition of good vs bad chunks from Section
3
and the adaptive percolation exploration from Section
4
, we will show in Section
5
that it is possible to string together the crossings between good chunks in order to deduce that with high probability we can connect two boundary points by a path
\omega
with
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
at most a constant times
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
. The adaptive version of the exploration will give sufficiently good concentration so that we can establish the tightness of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
on
\partial D
and deduce the comparability of the quantiles
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
.

In Section
6
we then extend the tightness of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
from the boundary to the interior. The first step is to define collections of points
P_{j}
in
\Upsilon
which consist of i.i.d. samples from the natural LQG measure on
\Upsilon
constructed in
[
33
]
. We will show in Appendix
B
that there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}>0
so that if
|P_{j}|=2^{\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}j}
then with overwhelming probability
P_{j}
forms a
2^{-j}
-net of
\Upsilon
(with respect to the Euclidean metric). We then aim to show that there exists
\beta>0
so that if
z,w\in P_{j}
have Euclidean distance at most
2^{-j}
then
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
is very likely to be at most
2^{-\beta j}
. Proving this statement will require a number of estimates. For example, we will have to rule out the possibility that such points are separated from each other by a loop of
\Gamma
with diameter significantly larger than
2^{-j}
. Upon proving this, the remainder of the proof of tightness follows along the proof of the Kolmogorov-Centsov continuity criterion where the role of the dyadic decomposition in the latter is played by the sets
P_{j}
in the former.

In the statement of Theorem
1.1
, it is important to note that the normalization is by the median of
\sup_{z,w\in\eta([0,1])}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma_{+})
as opposed to the median length of a crossing which connects two disjoint boundary arcs. In particular, due to this choice it is not obvious that a subsequential limit is positive definite (the other properties of being a metric will at this point be obvious due to how
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is defined). Establishing the positive definiteness will be the focus of Section
7
. The first observation is that a subsequential limit
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
cannot be completely degenerate (i.e., vanish) because this would contradict the comparability of the quantiles
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
uniformly in
\epsilon>0
which we have discussed earlier. In particular, this implies that if we take two disjoint arcs
I,J
on
\partial D
then it is a positive probability event that the
\mathfrak{d}(I,J;\Gamma)>0
. On this event, let
K=\{z\in D:\mathfrak{d}(z,I;\Gamma)=0\}
and let
U
be the component of
D\setminus K^{*}
with
J
on its boundary. Then
\partial U\setminus\partial D
has the property that there cannot exist
z\in U
and
w\in K
with
\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
(for otherwise
z\in K
). Thus
D\cap\partial U
can be thought of as a type of “shield” through which a path with “
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
-length zero” cannot pass. The thrust of the argument in Section
7
is to use that such shields exist with positive probability and independence to show that there a.s. exists a family of such shields which separate
I
from
J
and therefore
\mathfrak{d}(I,J;\Gamma)>0
a.s. Since
I
,
J
were arbitrary boundary intervals, this will imply that
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is positive definite on
\partial D
. Finally, using a CPI exploration, we will argue that if it is a positive probability event that exist
z,w\in\Upsilon\setminus\partial D
distinct with
\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
then it is also a positive probability event that there exists
z,w\in\partial D
distinct with
\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
. Altogether, this gives the positive definiteness of
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
everywhere. At the end of Section
7
, we will explain why the subsequential limit
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is a.s. geodesic and deduce the comparability of the quantiles defined in infinite volume and finite volume settings.

In Appendix
A
, we will give an upper bound for the modulus of continuity of the space-filling version of
{\rm SLE}
from
[
26
]
when drawn on top of an independent quantum disk and parameterized by quantum area. In Appendix
B
we will collect some estimates for the natural measure on the carpet of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
which comes from LQG constructed in
[
33
]
. Finally, in Appendix
C
, we will collect some estimates for Lévy processes.

1.4.
Relationship with other work

Let us make some comments on the relationship between this work and some recent works focused on the construction of the LQG metric. Recall that LQG refers to the random two-dimensional Riemannian manifold whose metric tensor is (formally) given by
e^{\gamma h(z)}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})
where
h
is an instance of (some form of) the Gaussian free field (GFF) on a planar domain
D
. Since
h
is a distribution and not a function, this expression does not make literal sense and requires interpretation. The rigorous construction of the two-point distance function associated with LQG was first accomplished in the case that
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
in
[
28
,
29
,
30
]
and then subsequently (using a different method) in
[
8
,
13
]
for all
\gamma\in(0,2)
. In the program of constructing a metric in the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
carpet for
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
, the present work plays the role of
[
8
]
in the program of constructing the LQG metric for
\gamma\in(0,2)
.

The argument that we will employ to establish tightness here will be rather different to that given in
[
8
]
, however. Let us briefly highlight a few of the main differences. First of all, the heart of
[
8
]
is to consider a certain mollification
h_{\epsilon}
of the GFF
h
and then to prove the (uniform in
\epsilon
) comparability of quantiles for lengths of side-to-side crossings in a box measured using the metric
e^{\xi h_{\epsilon}}
where
\xi>0
is determined by
\gamma
. Establishing this comparability in
[
8
]
is based on decomposing the larger box into smaller boxes and then using the Efron-Stein inequality to get a recursive relationship in the quantiles for different values of
\epsilon>0
. Once the comparability of side-to-side quantiles is accomplished, a standard sort of Kolmogorov-Centsov argument makes it possible to string together paths with length comparable to the median length crossing and deduce the tightness of the diameter and indeed the whole metric. Moreover, the positive definiteness of the subsequential limit is immediate because if there were two points with zero distance in the limit then there would have to be some box across which the quantiles are not all comparable.

In this work, we will instead make the choice of looking at quantiles for the diameter of the whole boundary associated with our approximations. We will then get comparability of the quantiles of the boundary diameter while at the same time proving that the subsequential limit is continuous with respect to the Euclidean topology. The argument that we will employ does not use the Efron-Stein inequality or a recursion in order to compare the approximations for different values of
\epsilon
. Moreover, stringing together short paths in
[
8
]
follows the usual dyadic subdivision as in the proof of the Kolmogorov-Centsov continuity criterion. In the present work, stringing together short paths is rather delicate because of the presence of obstacles such as loops. Finally, because we are normalizing our approximations by the median boundary diameter, it is not immediate that the subsequential limit is positive definite.

Next, we mention that the
{\rm CLE}_{8}
metric was constructed in
[
14
]
, though the nature of the problem is rather different than for
\kappa\in(8/3,8)
. Recall that
{\rm CLE}_{8}
is the scaling limit of the UST
[
20
]
so defining the metric in this case corresponds to properly assigning a length to the continuous analog of the UST branches. These branches are
{\rm SLE}_{2}
-type curves
[
24
,
26
]
and the appropriate notion of length to consider is the so-called natural parameterization of
{\rm SLE}

[
21
,
19
,
2
]
.

Finally, we mention the work
[
22
]
which studies random planar maps where the face sizes have law which is in the domain of attraction of an
\alpha
-stable distribution and it is shown that the resulting metric space properly renormalized is tight in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology. The subsequentially limiting metric constructed in
[
22
]
should correspond to a quantum version (i.e., where the lengths of paths in the approximations are defined using a quantity from LQG rather than a Euclidean quantity such as Lebesgue measure) of our main result, but we will not focus on that in the present paper.

1.5.
Notation

We will make use of the following notation in this article. We will write
\mathbf{D}
for the unit disk and
\mathbf{H}
for the upper half-plane. If
f,g
are two functions, then we will write
f\lesssim g
(resp.
f\gtrsim g
) if there exists a constant
c>0
so that
f\leq cg
(resp.
f\geq cg
). For a simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
and prime ends
a,b\in\partial D
, we let
[a,b]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
(resp.
[a,b]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
) denote the counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) arc of prime ends in
\partial D
from
a
to
b
. For a set
A
, we let
\mathrm{int}(A)
(resp.
\mathrm{cl}(A)
) denote the interior (resp. closure) of
A
.

2.
Preliminaries

The purpose of this section is to collect a number of preliminaries. We will start by reviewing some facts about
{\rm SLE}
in Section
2.1
. We will then review
{\rm CLE}
in Section
2.2
. Next we will review some relevant results from imaginary geometry
[
24
,
26
]
in Section
2.3
. Finally, we will review some basics of Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) and its relationship to
{\rm SLE}
in Section
2.4
. We assume that the reader has some familiarity with these objects and we will provide references for the various facts that we will use.

2.1.
Schramm-Loewner evolution

The Schramm-Loewner evolution (
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
) is a random fractal curve in a simply connected domain and was introduced by Schramm in
[
39
]
. The chordal version corresponds to a curve which connects two boundary points. In the case that the domain is
\mathbf{H}
and the curve goes from
0
to
\infty
, it is defined by solving the chordal Loewner equation

(2.1)

\partial_{t}g_{t}(z)=\frac{2}{g_{t}(z)-W_{t}},\quad g_{0}(z)=z

where
W_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}
and
B
is a standard Brownian motion. Let
\mathbf{H}_{t}
be the domain of
g_{t}
and
K_{t}=\mathbf{H}\setminus\mathbf{H}_{t}
. In the case that
\kappa\neq 8
, it was shown by Rohde-Schramm
[
38
]
that there a.s. exists a curve
\eta
so that
\mathbf{H}_{t}
is equal to the unbounded component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
. The continuity in the case that
\kappa=8
follows from the convergence of the uniform spanning tree Peano curve to
{\rm SLE}_{8}
established in
[
20
]
. If
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is another simply connected domain (i.e., not
\mathbf{H}
) and
x,y\in\partial D
are distinct, then an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
from
x
to
y
is defined as
\varphi(\eta)
where
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
and
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{D}
is a conformal map which takes
0
(resp.
\infty
) to
x
(resp.
y
).

The so-called
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
processes are an important variant of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in which one has extra marked points, say at
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, associated with weights
\underline{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n})\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
. The
x_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq n
are sometimes called force points and the associated weight
\rho_{j}
determines how the curve interacts with
x_{j}
. In particular, if
\rho_{j}
is positive (resp. negative) then the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
is pushed away from (resp. pulled towards)
x_{j}
. These processes were first introduced in
[
17
]
and are defined by solving (
2.1
) where
W
is taken to be the solution to the SDE

\displaystyle dW_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}dB_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\rho_{i}}{W_{t}-V_{t}^{i}}dt,\quad dV_{t}^{i}=\frac{2}{V_{t}^{i}-W_{t}}dt,\quad V_{0}^{i}=x_{i}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,n.

Up until the first time that
W
collides with one of the
V^{i}
, the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
hence corresponds to a continuous curve. More generally, the standard
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
processes are defined up to the so-called continuation threshold
[
24
]
: the first time
t
that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}{\bf 1}_{W_{t}=V_{t}^{i}}\leq-2
. The continuity of the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
processes up to the continuation threshold was proved in
[
24
]
. One can also consider
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
processes with
-2-\kappa/2<\rho<-2
, though in this case their behavior is rather than different. In particular, an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
process with
-2-\kappa/2<\rho<-2
is self-intersecting for
\kappa\in(0,4]
while standard
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
and
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
curves are simple. The continuity in the case
-2-\kappa/2<\rho<-2
was proved in
[
31
,
27
]
in the presence of a single force point. We will sometimes use the notation
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho}_{L};\underline{\rho}_{R})
to indicate an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
process where
\underline{\rho}_{L}
(resp.
\underline{\rho}_{R}
) indicates the weights of the of the force points which are to the left (resp. right) of
0
. An
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
connecting two boundary points in a simply connected domain is defined as the conformal image of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
.

Let us mention some of the special ranges of
\rho
values. Suppose that
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho}_{L};\underline{\rho}_{R})
with force points located at
(\underline{x}_{L};\underline{x}_{R})
.

•

If
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,R}\geq\kappa/2-2
then
\eta
a.s. does not hit
[x_{i,R},x_{i+1,R})
(and likewise with
L
in place of
R
).

•

If
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,R}\in(-2,\kappa/2-2)
then
\eta
can hit
[x_{i,R},x_{i+1,R})
(and likewise with
L
in place of
R
).

•

In the case
\kappa^{\prime}>4
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,R}\in(-2,\kappa^{\prime}/2-4]
then with positive probability
\eta^{\prime}
fills an interval of the form
[x_{i,R},b)
for
b\in(x_{i,R},x_{i+1,R})
(and likewise with
L
in place of
R
).

We can also consider whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
processes, which are defined by solving the equation

\partial_{t}g_{t}(z)=g_{t}(z)\frac{W_{t}+g_{t}(z)}{W_{t}-g_{t}(z)}.

Here we take
W_{t}=e^{i\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}}
where
B_{t}
is a two-sided (i.e., defined for
t\in\mathbf{R}
) Brownian motion. For each time
t
,
g_{t}
is the unique conformal transformation from the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta([0,t])
to
\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathrm{cl}(\mathbf{D})
with positive derivative at
\infty
. The whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
processes also correspond to a continuous curve
[
38
,
20
]
meaning there exists a continuous curve
\eta\colon\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{C}
so that for each
t\in\mathbf{R}
the domain of
g_{t}
is equal to the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta((-\infty,t])
. There are also the whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
processes where
\rho>-2
which are constructed by replacing
W_{t}
with the solution to the SDE (defined for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
)

\displaystyle dW_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\rho}{2}\widetilde{\Psi}(O_{t},W_{t})-\frac{\kappa}{2}W_{t}\right)dt+i\sqrt{\kappa}W_{t}dB_{t},\quad dO_{t}=\Psi(W_{t},O_{t})dt

where

\Psi(w,z)=-z\frac{z+w}{z-w}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\Psi}(z,w)=\frac{\Psi(z,w)+\Psi(1/\overline{z},w)}{2}.

The continuity of the whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
processes with
\rho>-2
was proved in
[
26
]
.

2.2.
Conformal loop ensembles

We are now going to give a brief review of the conformal loop ensembles (
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
)
[
40
,
42
]
. We will focus on their construction using the so-called boundary conformal loop ensembles (
\mathrm{BCLE}_{\kappa}
) which is developed in
[
31
]
since this is the construction which will be most relevant for this work. We will primarily focus on the case of the simple
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
(i.e.,
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
).

Fix
\kappa\in(2,4)
and
\rho\in(-2,\kappa-4)
. A
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
(so that the loops have a clockwise orientation) is defined as follows. Let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho;\kappa-6-\rho)
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
with force points at
0^{-}
,
0^{+}
. Then the law of
\eta
is
target invariant
. This means that if
\varphi
is a conformal transformation
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
which fixes
0
then
\varphi(\eta)
and
\eta
, viewed modulo time parameterization, have the same law up until first disconnecting
\infty
and
\varphi(\infty)
. Let
(x_{n})
be a countable dense set in
\mathbf{R}
and, for each
n\in\mathbf{N}
, let
\eta_{n}
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho;\kappa-6-\rho)
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
x
with force points at
0^{-}
,
0^{+}
. The target invariance implies that we can couple the
\eta_{n}
together so that any finite collection of them agree up until their target points are disconnected and then afterwards evolve independently. The family of paths
(\eta_{n})
should be thought of as branches of a tree which is rooted at
0
. As we will explain shortly, the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
in
\mathbf{H}
is defined from the
(\eta_{n})
in a certain way;
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
in domains other than
\mathbf{H}
are defined by applying a conformal transformation.

The way that the loops are defined from the family
(\eta_{n})
is as follows. Suppose that
\eta_{n}
makes an excursion away from
[0,x_{n}]_{\partial\mathbf{H}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
in the time-interval
[s,t]
. Then
\eta_{n}|_{[s,t]}
corresponds to part of a loop. If
x_{m}\in(\eta_{n}(s),\eta_{n}(t))
then there exists
t^{\prime}>t
so that
\eta_{m}
makes an excursion away from
[0,x_{m}]_{\partial\mathbf{H}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
in
[s,t^{\prime}]
and
\eta_{n}|_{[s,t]}=\eta_{m}|_{[s,t]}
. Thus
\eta_{m}|_{[s,t^{\prime}]}
corresponds to a larger portion of the same loop. The whole loop is obtained by by taking a sequence
(x_{m_{j}})
in
[\eta_{n}(s),\eta_{n}(t)]_{\partial\mathbf{H}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
which decreases to
\eta_{n}(s)
and considering the excursion of
\eta_{m_{j}}
which contains
\eta_{n}|_{[s,t]}
. It was shown in
[
31
]
that the loops are simple continuous curves and that the law of the collection of loops does not depend on the root point (i.e., the origin). By considering the intervals
[0,x_{n}]_{\partial\mathbf{H}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
in place of
[0,x_{n}]_{\partial\mathbf{H}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
one can define the “false” loops of
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
and note that these are surrounded counterclockwise.
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
is defined in the same way except we take
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6-\rho;\rho)
in place of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho;\kappa-6-\rho)
and the loops of a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
correspond to the complementary components which are surrounded counterclockwise.

There is a natural exploration path of a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
process
\Gamma
on a Jordan domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
. Namely, let
\eta_{0}
be a path which traverse
\partial D
counterclockwise. We then let
\eta
be the path which follows
\eta_{0}
except whenever it hits a loop of
\Gamma
for the first time, it follows the loop in its entirety in the clockwise direction, and then continues following
\eta_{0}
. The continuity of
\eta
was proved in
[
31
]
as a consequence of the continuity of space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
established in
[
26
]
. If we take
\eta
and target it at a fixed boundary point (i.e., we parameterize
\eta
by capacity as viewed from the given boundary point), then we recover the branch of the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho;\kappa-6-\rho)
tree used to build the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
. We can similarly define an exploration path of a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(\rho)
.

For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
and
\rho\in(\kappa^{\prime}/2-4,\kappa^{\prime}/2-2)
a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime})
is defined in an analogous manner to the case
\kappa\in(2,4)
. Moreover, one can associate with a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime})
an exploration path which is defined in the same way as the case
\kappa\in(2,4)
. When
\rho^{\prime}=0
we will simply write
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. It turns out that the loops of a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
have the same law as the boundary touching loops of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
.

Fix
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
and let
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\kappa\in(4,6)
. The way that the iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
construction works is as follows. Suppose that we first sample a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
process
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Then in each of the complementary components which are surrounded clockwise we sample a conditionally independent
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
. This procedure is then repeated in each of the components which are surrounded by a false (i.e., counterclockwise) loop of the original
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
or by a false (i.e., clockwise) loop of one of the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
. The resulting collection of
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
loops (from all of the iterations) gives a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
process
\Gamma
.

Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the exploration path associated with the
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Let
\eta
be defined to be the path which follows
\eta^{\prime}
except whenever it hits a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
loop for the first time then it follows that loop counterclockwise. It is shown in
[
31
, Theorem 7.4]
that if we target
\eta
at any fixed boundary point then we get an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
process and recall that we know that
\eta^{\prime}
targeted at any fixed boundary point is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
process. We call
\eta
a
conformal percolation interface
(CPI) in
\Gamma
and
\eta^{\prime}
its trunk. If we draw
\eta
up to any stopping time in which it is not drawing a loop of
\Gamma
, then the conditional law of the loops of
\Gamma
in the complementary components are conditionally independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
’s. In order to emphasize that all of the loops that
\eta
hits are to its right, we will sometimes write
\eta\sim{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
. By replacing the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
with
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
we can similarly construct an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{-1}(\kappa-6)
process meaning that the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loops which it hits are always to its left.

What we have described above is a
totally asymmetric
CPI because all of the loops which are hit by
\eta
lie to its right. We can also consider the symmetric version which is constructed as follows. We first sample a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}/2-3)
process
\Gamma^{\prime}
. In each complementary component which is surrounded clockwise we sample a conditionally independent
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(\kappa/4-2)
and in each component which is surrounded counterclockwise we sample a conditionally independent
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\kappa/4-2)
. This procedure is then iterated in each of the false loops of the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(\kappa/4-2)
and
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\kappa/4-2)
. The resulting ensemble of loops is again a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
(see
[
31
, Theorem 7.4, Corollary 7.5]
). Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the exploration path associated with
\Gamma^{\prime}
and then let
\eta
be the path which is obtained by following
\eta^{\prime}
and except it traverses each loop of a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(\kappa/4-2)
(resp.
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa}(\kappa/4-2)
) that it hits counterclockwise (resp. clockwise). Then
\eta
targeted at any fixed boundary point has the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
process. Each time that
\eta
encounters a new loop of the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
, it goes to its left or right based on the toss of a fair coin flip.

We will not need to consider even more general versions of these explorations, but we remark that one can get
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{\beta}(\kappa-6)
for
\beta\in[-1,1]
using other combinations of
\rho
values in the above construction properly (see
[
31
, Theorem 7.4]
). The formula which relates
\beta
and
\rho
was computed in
[
33
]
. (The corresponding formula with the roles of
\kappa
and
\kappa^{\prime}
reversed was computed in
[
34
]
.)

2.3.
Imaginary geometry

We are now going to collect some background from the theory of imaginary geometry
[
24
,
26
]
which will be relevant for the present work. Throughout, we take the convention that
\kappa\in(0,4)
and
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\kappa>4
. We also let

\chi=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa}}-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{2},\quad\lambda=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa}},\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{\prime}=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa^{\prime}}}.

Suppose that we have weights
(\underline{\rho}_{L};\underline{\rho}_{R})
and force point locations
(\underline{x}_{L};\underline{x}_{R})
. Let
h
be a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by

-\lambda\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,L}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad(x_{k+1,L},x_{k,L}]\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,R}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad[x_{k,R},x_{k+1,R}).

It is shown in
[
24
]
that an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho}_{L};\underline{\rho}_{R})
process with force points located at
(\underline{x}_{L};\underline{x}_{R})
can be coupled with
h
as a flow line of the formal vector field
e^{ih/\chi}
. This means that if
(f_{t})
is the centered Loewner flow for
\eta
(i.e.,
f_{t}=g_{t}-W_{t}
where
(g_{t})
is the Loewner flow) and
\tau
is a stopping time for
\eta
then
h\circ f_{\tau}^{-1}-\chi\arg(f_{\tau}^{-1})^{\prime}
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by

-\lambda\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,L}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad(f_{\tau}(x_{k+1,L}),f_{\tau}(x_{k,L})]\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,R}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad[f_{\tau}(x_{k,R}),f_{\tau}(x_{k+1,R})).

Moreover, the boundary conditions on the image of the left (resp. right) side of
\eta|_{[0,\tau]}
are given by
-\lambda
(resp.
\lambda
). Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain,
\varphi\colon\mathbf{H}\to D
is a conformal transformation, and
\widetilde{h}=h\circ\varphi^{-1}-\chi\arg(\varphi^{-1})^{\prime}
. Then we can similarly view
\varphi(\eta)
as the flow line of
e^{i\widetilde{h}/\chi}
. In the coupling of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho}_{L};\underline{\rho}_{R})
as a flow line of a GFF, we also have that the former is a.s. determined by the latter. Finally, we can also define the flow line of a GFF
h
with angle
\theta
by considering the flow line of
h+\theta\chi
.

We now turn to the case of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
processes. Suppose that we have weights
(\underline{\rho}_{L};\underline{\rho}_{R})
and force point locations
(\underline{x}_{L};\underline{x}_{R})
. Let
h
be a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions

\lambda^{\prime}\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,L}^{\prime}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad(x_{k+1,L},x_{k,L}]\quad\text{and}\quad-\lambda^{\prime}\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,R}^{\prime}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad[x_{k,R},x_{k+1,R}).

Then we can view an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\underline{\rho}_{L}^{\prime};\underline{\rho}_{R}^{\prime})
process
\eta^{\prime}
as a counterflow line of
h
from
0
to
\infty
. This means that if
(f_{t})
is the centered Loewner flow for
\eta^{\prime}
and
\tau
is a stopping time for
\eta^{\prime}
then we have that
h\circ f_{\tau}^{-1}-\chi\arg(f_{\tau}^{-1})^{\prime}
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by

\lambda^{\prime}\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,L}^{\prime}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad(f_{\tau}(x_{k+1,L}),f_{\tau}(x_{k,L})]\quad\text{and}\quad-\lambda^{\prime}\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i,R}^{\prime}\right)\quad\text{in}\quad[f_{\tau}(x_{k,R}),f_{\tau}(x_{k+1,R})).

Moreover, the boundary conditions on the image of the left (resp. right) side of
\eta|_{[0,\tau]}
are given by
\lambda^{\prime}
(resp.
-\lambda^{\prime}
). As in the case of GFF flow lines, counterflow lines are a.s. determined by the GFF and counterflow lines for GFFs defined on domains other than
\mathbf{H}
are defined by applying the same change of coordinates formula as in the case of flow lines.

The discussion above implies that the iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
exploration is naturally coupled with the GFF. We will explain this point in further detail in Appendix
A
.

We will also have occasion to consider GFF flow lines which start from an interior point, as developed in
[
26
]
. If we fix
\alpha>-\chi
and we have a whole-plane GFF with values modulo
2\pi(\chi+\alpha)
, then its flow line starting from
0
is a whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
process with
\rho=2-\kappa+2\pi\alpha/\lambda
. We can also consider flow lines of different angles starting from the origin and talk about their conditional laws.

Lastly, let us also mention the space-filling version of
{\rm SLE}
which is considered in
[
26
]
. It is constructed by fixing a countable dense set of points, starting a flow line from each one, and then ordering the points according to how the flow lines merge. It is shown in
[
26
]
that there is a continuous path which visits these points in this order and this is space-filling
{\rm SLE}
.

2.4.
Liouville quantum gravity

A Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) surface is the random surface whose Riemannian metric admits the formal expression:

e^{\gamma h(z)}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})

where
h
is an instance of some form of the GFF and
\gamma\in(0,2)
is a parameter. This expression does not make literal sense because
h
is a distribution and not a function. The volume form was constructed in
[
10
]
(see also the references therein). If
h
is (some form of) the GFF with free boundary conditions, then one can also make sense of a boundary length measure by considering
e^{\gamma h(x)/2}dx
. The metric (i.e., two-point distance function) for LQG was constructed first for
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
in
[
28
,
29
,
30
]
and subsequently for all
\gamma\in(0,2)
in
[
8
,
13
]
.

If
\varphi\colon D\to\widetilde{D}
is a conformal transformation and we let

(2.2)

\widetilde{h}=h\circ\varphi+Q\log|\varphi^{\prime}|\quad\text{where}\quad Q=\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma}{2}

then we have that
\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(\varphi(A))=\mu_{h}(A)
for all Borel sets
A\subseteq D
. We say that two fields
h
,
\widetilde{h}
are equivalent as quantum surfaces if they are related as in (
2.2
). A quantum surface is defined as an equivalence class with respect to this equivalence relation. More generally, we can consider a quantum surface with marked points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in D
. Two quantum surfaces with marked points are considered to be equivalent if the fields are related as in (
2.2
) where the conformal map
\varphi
takes the marked points for one surface to the marked points for the other. We remark that it is not always obvious that two fields define equivalent quantum surfaces.

We will always assume that the parameters are matched by
\kappa=\gamma^{2}\in(0,4)
and
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\kappa>4
. There is a way to define the quantum length of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
on a
\gamma
-LQG surface which comes as a consequence of the main results of
[
41
]
. The main results of
[
9
]
provide a way to define the quantum length (so-called quantum natural time) of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
process on a
\gamma
-LQG surface.

There are a number of important types of LQG surfaces: quantum wedges, cones, disks, and spheres. We will not give a precise definition of these objects here because their exact definition will not matter for what follows, with the exception of the technical estimates which are proved in Appendices
B
and
A
. Briefly, a quantum wedge is an infinite volume surface which comes with two marked points (the “origin” and “infinity”). We will denote the law of a quantum wedge of weight
W>0
as
\mathsf{QWedge}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=W}
. Quantum wedges are homeomorphic to
\mathbf{H}
when
W\geq\tfrac{\gamma^{2}}{2}
and in this case we will use the notation
(\mathbf{H},h,0,\infty)
to indicate a quantum wedge which is parameterized by
\mathbf{H}
with the origin point at
0
and the infinity point at
\infty
.

Following
[
33
]
, we will refer to a sample from the law
\mathsf{QWedge}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=2}
as a
quantum half-plane
. The reason for this terminology is that the quantum half-plane is the LQG analog of the so-called Brownian half-plane
[
12
]
. It possesses the following special property
[
41
]
which distinguishes it from other quantum wedges. If
(\mathbf{H},h,0,\infty)
is a quantum half-plane,
r>0
is fixed, and
x\in[0,\infty)
is such that
\nu_{h}([0,x])=r
, then
(\mathbf{H},h,x,\infty)
is a quantum half-plane.

A quantum cone is an infinite volume surface which is homeomorphic to
\mathbf{C}
and also has two marked points (the “origin” and “infinity”). We will denote the law of a quantum cone of weight
W>0
as
\mathsf{QCone}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=W}
. We will use the notation
(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)
to indicate a quantum cone parameterized by
\mathbf{C}
where the origin point is at
0
and the infinity point is at
\infty
. A quantum disk is a finite volume surface which is homeomorphic to
\mathbf{D}
and its definition naturally equips it with two marked boundary points. We will also denote the law of a quantum disk with boundary length
\ell
using
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
and use the notation
(D,h,x,y)
to denote a quantum disk where the marked boundary points are at
x,y
.

The following theorem is proved in
[
41
]
in the case of
\mathsf{QWedge}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=4}
and an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
process and then generalized in
[
9
]
to the case of
\mathsf{QWedge}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=W}
for
W>0
and
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho_{1};\rho_{2})
for
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}>-2
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Fix
W\geq\tfrac{\gamma^{2}}{2}
and suppose that
W_{1},W_{2}>0
are such that
W=W_{1}+W_{2}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{W}=(\mathbf{H},h,0,\infty)
has law
\mathsf{QWedge}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=W}
. Let
\eta
be an independent
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(W_{1}-2;W_{2}-2)
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{2}
) be the quantum surface parameterized by the component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta
which are to the left (resp. right) of
\eta
. Then
\mathcal{W}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{2}
) has law
\mathsf{QWedge}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=W_{1}}
(resp.
\mathsf{QWedge}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=W_{2}}
) and
\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2}
are independent.

This is proved in
[
9
]
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Fix
W>0
and suppose that
\mathcal{C}=(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)
has law
\mathsf{QCone}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=W}
. Let
\eta
be an independent whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(W-2)
process in
\mathbf{C}
from
0
to
\infty
. Then the quantum surface parameterized by
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta
is a quantum wedge of weight
W
.

Remark 2.3
.

As a consequence of Theorems
2.1
,
2.2
we have the following fact. Suppose that
\mathcal{C}=(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)
has law
\mathsf{QCone}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=4}
. Let
\eta_{-}
be an independent whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(2)
in
\mathbf{C}
from
0
to
\infty
and, given
\eta_{-}
, let
\eta_{+}
be a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta_{-}
from
0
to
\infty
. Let
H_{+},H_{-}
be the two components of
\mathbf{C}\setminus(\eta_{-}\cup\eta_{+})
and let
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}=(H_{\pm},h,0,\infty)
be the quantum surfaces parameterized by
H_{\pm}
. Then
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}
are independent quantum half-planes. We note that if one considers the concatenation of the time-reversal of
\eta_{-}
with
\eta_{+}
then one obtains a curve in the Riemann sphere from
\infty
to
\infty
which in the literature is sometimes referred to as a two-sided whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
from
\infty
to
\infty
through
0
. One can also construct the law
\eta_{\pm}
using the whole-plane GFF using the machinery from
[
26
]
. Namely, let
h^{w}
be a whole-plane GFF with values modulo
2\pi(\chi+\sqrt{\kappa}/2)=4\pi/\sqrt{\kappa}
and let
h^{\mathrm{IG}}=h^{w}-\tfrac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{2}\arg(\cdot)
, also with values defined modulo
4\pi/\sqrt{\kappa}
. Then the joint law of the pair
(\eta_{-},\eta_{+})
is equal to that of the flow lines of
h^{\mathrm{IG}}
from
0
to
\infty
with angles
2\pi/(2-\kappa/2)
,
0
, respectively.

Using the invariance of the law of the quantum-plane under the operation of shifting its origin point a given amount of quantum length to the left or right, one also has the following. Suppose that
\eta_{+}
is parameterized according to quantum length and
r>0
. Then
(H_{+},h|_{H_{+}},\eta_{+}(r),\infty)
,
(H_{-},h|_{H_{-}},\eta_{+}(r),\infty)
are independent quantum half-planes. Therefore the quantum surface
(\mathbf{C},h,\eta_{+}(r),\infty)
also has law
\mathsf{QCone}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=4}
. Moreover, up to a translation and rescaling the law of the paths
\eta_{+}|_{[r,\infty)}
and the concatenation of the time-reversal of
\eta_{+}|_{[0,r]}
with
\eta_{-}
is the same as the law of
(\eta_{-},\eta_{+})
.

The following is proved in
[
33
]
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}=(\mathbf{H},h,0,\infty)
is a quantum half-plane. Fix
\beta\in[-1,1]
. Let
\eta
be an independent
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{\beta}(\kappa-6)
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
which is subsequently parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk. Then for each time
t
, the quantum surface
\mathcal{H}_{t}
parameterized by the unbounded component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
is a quantum half-plane which is independent of the quantum surface
{\mathcal{K}}_{t}
parameterized by its complement in
\mathbf{H}
and decorated by
\eta|_{[0,t]}
. This holds more generally if we replace
t
by a stopping time of the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma({\mathcal{K}}_{s}:s\leq t)
. Finally, let
L_{t}
denote the quantum length of the part of
\mathbf{H}\cap\partial{\mathcal{K}}_{t}
which is to the left of
\eta(t)
minus the quantum length of the part of
\partial\mathbf{H}\cap\partial{\mathcal{K}}_{t}
which is to the left of
0
. Define
R_{t}
analogously with right in place of left. Then the pair
(L_{t},R_{t})
evolve as independent
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy processes.

In the statement of Theorem
2.4
, the positivity parameter (which determines the relative intensity of upward vs downward jumps) and relative overall intensity of jumps for
L_{t}
and
R_{t}
depend on
\beta
and were determined in
[
33
]
. We will have some further discussion of this in Appendix
C
.

3.
Crossing estimates

The purpose of this section is to start to set up the percolation argument that will be used in order to establish Theorem
1.1
. In Section
3.1
, we will describe the setup that we will use throughout this section as well as give the main two statements. The first main statement (Lemma
3.1
) gives that it is very likely that a quantum surface
\mathcal{N}
cut out of a quantum half-plane by an independent
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
process run until the first time it hits the boundary after time
\delta^{4/\kappa}
contains paths
\omega
in the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
carpet with
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
(as defined in Section
1.3
; we will also recall the definition of
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
just below) which connect the bottom right corner of
\mathcal{N}
to any sufficiently large interval on the top of
\mathcal{N}
which intersects the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
carpet and otherwise stays away from
\partial\mathcal{N}
, provided
\delta>0
is sufficiently small. The second main statement (Lemma
3.2
) is of a similar flavor except with two chunks of quantum surface cut out by successive
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
processes. In order to read the rest of this article, one only needs these two main statements, so the rest of the section can be skipped on a first reading. In Section
3.2
, we will show that a CPI in a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
carpet
\Upsilon
has positive conditional probability given
\Upsilon
of being close to any fixed path in
\Upsilon
. The focus of Section
3.3
is to prove an intermediate result for the likelihood of such a crossing
\omega
in
\Upsilon
as above but in a slightly different setting. In Section
3.4
we will use this intermediate result in order to complete the proofs of the two main statements.

3.1.
Setup and main statements

Throughout this section, we assume that we have the following setup. We assume that
\mathcal{C}=(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)
has law
\mathsf{QCone}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=4}
with the circle average embedding. We let
h^{w}
be a whole-plane GFF with values modulo
2\pi(\chi+\sqrt{\kappa}/2)=4\pi/\sqrt{\kappa}
and set
h^{\mathrm{IG}}=h^{w}-\tfrac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{2}\arg(\cdot)
, also with values modulo
4\pi/\sqrt{\kappa}
. We assume that
h^{\mathrm{IG}}
is independent of
\mathcal{C}
. We let
\eta_{-}
,
\eta_{+}
be the flow lines of
h^{\mathrm{IG}}
from
0
to
\infty
with angles
2\pi/(2-\kappa/2)
,
0
, respectively. Then
\eta_{\pm}
are each whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(2)
processes in
\mathbf{C}
from
0
to
\infty

[
26
, Theorem 1.4]
. Moreover, the conditional law of
\eta_{-}
(resp.
\eta_{+}
) given
\eta_{+}
(resp.
\eta_{-}
) is that of a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
process in
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta_{+}
(resp.
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta_{-}
) from
0
to
\infty
. That is, the concatenation of the time-reversal of
\eta_{-}
together with
\eta_{+}
is a two-sided whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
process from
\infty
to
\infty
through
0
and the same is true with the roles of
\eta_{\pm}
swapped. We assume that
\eta_{\pm}
are parameterized according to quantum length according to
h
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{+}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{-}
) be the quantum surface parameterized by the component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus(\eta_{-}\cup\eta_{+})
part of whose boundary consists of the left side of
\eta_{+}
. By Theorems
2.1
,
2.2
we have that
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}
are independent quantum half-planes, which we assume to be marked by
0
and
\infty
. Let
\Gamma_{+}
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathcal{H}_{+}
and let
\Upsilon_{+}
be the carpet of
\Gamma_{+}
. As in Section
1.3
, for each
\epsilon>0
and
p\in(0,1)
we let
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
denote the
p
th quantile of
\sup_{z,w\in\eta_{+}([0,1])}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma_{+})
and let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}={{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(1/2)

Figure 3.1.
Illustration of the setup and statement of Lemma
3.1
(left) and Lemma
3.2
(right). Not shown are the loops of
\Gamma
, which are in particular dense in
\mathcal{H}_{+}\cap\partial\mathcal{N}
,
\mathcal{H}_{+}\cap\partial\mathcal{N}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{+}\cap\partial\mathcal{N}_{1}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For each
p_{0},\zeta_{0},p\in(0,1)
there exists
\xi_{0},\delta_{0}>0
so that for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the following is true. Suppose that
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
in
\mathcal{H}_{+}
from
0
to
\infty
which is coupled with
\Gamma_{+}
as a CPI and parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk. Let
\sigma=\inf\{t\geq\delta^{4/\kappa}:\eta(t)\in\partial\mathcal{H}_{+}\}
and let
\mathcal{N}
be the quantum surface disconnected from
\infty
by
\eta|_{[0,\sigma]}
. Let
d_{0}=\mathrm{diam}(\eta([0,\sigma]))
and let
z_{R}
be the rightmost point on the bottom of
\mathcal{N}
. Let
G
be the event that for each interval
I
on the top of
\partial\mathcal{N}
with quantum length at least
\zeta_{0}\delta
and which intersects
\Upsilon_{+}
there is a path
\omega
in
\Upsilon_{+}\cap\mathcal{N}
which

(i)

connects
\partial B(z_{R},\zeta_{0}d_{0})
to
\widetilde{I}
where
\widetilde{I}
is the interval on the top of
\partial\mathcal{N}
with the same center as
I
but with twice the quantum length,

(ii)

\mathrm{dist}(\omega,\partial\mathcal{N}\setminus\widetilde{I})\geq\xi_{0}d_{0}
, and

(iii)

satisfies
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
.

Then
\mathbf{P}[G]\geq p_{0}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For each
p_{0},\zeta_{0},p\in(0,1)
there exists
\xi_{0},\delta_{0}>0
so that for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the following is true. Suppose that
\eta_{0}
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
in
\mathcal{H}_{+}
from
0
to
\infty
which is coupled with
\Gamma_{+}
as a CPI and parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk. Let
\sigma_{0}=\inf\{t\geq\delta^{4/\kappa}:\eta_{0}(t)\in\partial\mathcal{H}_{+}\}
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{0}
be the quantum surface disconnected from
\infty
by
\eta_{0}|_{[0,\sigma_{0}]}
. Let
\eta_{1}
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
process in
\mathcal{H}_{+,1}=\mathcal{H}_{+}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{0}
starting from a uniformly random point chosen from the set of points on the top
\mathcal{H}_{+}\cap\partial\mathcal{N}_{0}
of
\partial\mathcal{N}_{0}
whose clockwise boundary length distance to
\partial\mathcal{H}_{+}
is an integer multiple of
\zeta_{0}\delta
and are within boundary length distance
\zeta_{0}\delta
of a point in
\Upsilon_{+}\cap\partial\mathcal{N}_{0}
and coupled with the loops of
\Gamma_{+}
contained in
\mathcal{H}_{+,1}
as a CPI. We assume that
\eta_{1}
is parameterized according to the quantum natural time of its trunk. Let
\sigma_{1}=\inf\{t\geq\delta^{4/\kappa}:\eta_{1}(t)\in\partial\mathcal{H}_{+,1}\}
. Let
d_{0}=\mathrm{diam}(\eta_{0}([0,\sigma_{0}]))
and let
z_{R}
be the rightmost point on the bottom of
\mathcal{N}_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{N}
be the quantum surface parameterized by
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}_{1}))
. Let
G
be the event that for each interval
I
on the top of
\partial\mathcal{N}_{1}
with quantum length at least
\zeta_{0}\delta
and which intersects
\Upsilon_{+}
there is a path
\omega
in
\Upsilon_{+}\cap\mathcal{N}
which

(i)

connects
\partial B(z_{R},\zeta_{0}d_{0})
to
\widetilde{I}
where
\widetilde{I}
is the interval on the top of
\partial\mathcal{N}
with the same center as
I
but with twice the quantum length,

(ii)

\mathrm{dist}(\omega,\partial\mathcal{N}\setminus\widetilde{I})\geq\xi_{0}d_{0}
, and

(iii)

satisfies
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
.

Then
\mathbf{P}[G]\geq p_{0}
.

Let us now explain the main steps used to prove Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
.

Step 1.

Show that a CPI in
\Gamma_{+}
has a positive chance given
\Upsilon_{+}
of being close to any fixed path in
\Upsilon_{+}
(Proposition
3.3
). This result will be used several other times in this article. Its proof does not need to be read in order to understand the proofs of Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
or the rest of the article.

Step 2.

We will construct a domain in
\mathcal{H}_{+}
using a pair of CPIs of
\Gamma_{+}
whose boundary has positive distance from
\partial\mathcal{H}_{+}
. We will argue that if we make this domain sufficiently small then it is likely to contain a crossing
\omega
in
\Upsilon_{+}
satisfying
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
. As we mentioned in Section
1.3
, the key point in the proof where we define the quantiles using
\sup_{z,w\in\eta_{+}([0,1])}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma_{+})
(rather than using the length of a crossing in
\Upsilon_{+}
connecting two disjoint boundary arcs) will be contained in this step and it is used to show that the probability that the (Euclidean) diameter of the set of points
z\in\Upsilon_{+}
with
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(0,z;\Gamma_{+})\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
is at least
\delta>0
tends to
1
as
\delta\to 0
provided
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small relative to
\delta
.

Step 3.

Complete the proofs of Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
. In the case of Lemma
3.1
, we will use Proposition
3.3
to argue that given
\mathcal{N}
there is a positive chance that some additional CPIs construct a domain as in Step 2 which contains a crossing
\omega
in
\Upsilon_{+}
satisfying
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
where the shape of the auxiliary domain is such that the crossing is forced to connect
\partial B(z_{R},\zeta_{0}d_{0})
to
\widetilde{I}
while staying at distance at least
\xi_{0}d_{0}
from
\partial\mathcal{N}\setminus\widetilde{I}
. The proof of Lemma
3.2
is the same except we want the crossing to traverse
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}_{1}))
instead of a single
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
chunk so we will not explain the argument in this case in detail.

3.2.
A CPI has a positive chance of being close to any path in the carpet

In
[
32
]
it was proved that a CPI inside of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
carpet
\Upsilon
is a.s. not determined by
\Upsilon
. That is, the conditional law of a CPI in
\Upsilon
given
\Upsilon
is a.s. non-atomic. In what follows, we will upgrade this statement to show that if
\omega
is any continuous path in
\Upsilon
connecting distinct boundary points then a CPI connecting the same boundary points has positive conditional probability given
\Upsilon
of staying in the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\omega
. We will phrase the following statement for a deterministic path
\omega
but by considering the finite collection of piecewise polygonal paths which connect the vertices in
(\epsilon\mathbf{Z}^{2})\cap\mathbf{D}
the same statement a.s. holds for all
\omega
simultaneously.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\omega\colon[0,1]\to\mathbf{D}
be a continuous curve with
\omega(0)=-i
and
\omega(1)=i
. Fix
\epsilon>0
, let
\omega^{\epsilon}
be the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\omega
, and let
K=\mathbf{D}\setminus\omega^{\epsilon}
. Suppose that
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
\mathbf{D}
,
\Upsilon
its carpet, and let
K^{*}
be the closure of the union of
K
and the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect
K
. Let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
in
\mathbf{D}
from
-i
to
i
coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI and let
\eta^{\prime}
be its trunk. Let
E
be the event that
K^{*}
does not disconnect
-i
from
i
in
\omega^{\epsilon}
. Then

\mathbf{P}[\eta^{\prime}\subseteq\omega^{\epsilon}\,|\,\Upsilon]>0\quad\text{a.s.\ on}\quad E.

In order to complete the proof of Proposition
3.3
we need to recall a few things from
[
32
]
. Suppose that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
on
\mathbf{D}
. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the branch of the exploration tree of
\Gamma^{\prime}
from
-i
to
i
and let
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}
be its time-reversal. Suppose that
t>0
. On the event that
\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,t]}
has not yet reached
i
, we have that
\eta^{\prime}
is a.s. exploring some loop
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
at the time
t
. Let
t_{0}
be such that
[t_{0},t]
is the interval of time in which
\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,t]}
is exploring
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
. Suppose that
\overline{t}>0
and we are on the event that
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}|_{[0,\overline{t}]}
has not hit
\eta^{\prime}([0,t])
. Then
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}
is a.s. exploring some loop
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
at the time
\overline{t}
. Let
\overline{t}_{0}
be such that
[\overline{t}_{0},\overline{t}]
is the interval of time in which
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}|_{[0,\overline{t}]}
is exploring
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
. Let
D_{t,\overline{t}}
be the component of
\mathbf{D}\setminus(\eta^{\prime}([0,t])\cup\overline{\eta}^{\prime}([0,\overline{t}]))
with
\eta^{\prime}(t)
,
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}(\overline{t})
on its boundary. Then it is shown in
[
32
, Lemma 3.1]
that the conditional law of the part of
\Gamma^{\prime}
contained in
D_{t,\overline{t}}
is a conformally invariant function of
D_{t,\overline{t}}
and the marked points
\eta^{\prime}(t)
,
\eta^{\prime}(t_{0})
,
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}(\overline{t})
,
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}(\overline{t}_{0})
. Moreover, let
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
be equal to
\eta^{\prime}
starting from
\eta^{\prime}(t)
,
\eta_{1}^{\prime}
the time-reversal of
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
starting from
\eta^{\prime}(t_{0})
,
\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}
be equal to
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}
starting from
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}(\overline{t})
, and
\overline{\eta}_{1}^{\prime}
the time-reversal of
\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}
starting from
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}(\overline{t}_{0})
. Then
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
can terminate at either
\eta^{\prime}(t_{0})
, in which case
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\neq\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
, or at
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}(\overline{t})
, in which case
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
. (The probability of the two ways of the paths hooking up was subsequently computed explicitly in
[
35
]
.) Then it is shown in
[
32
, Section 4.1]
that conditionally on the ranges of
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
,
\eta_{1}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{1}^{\prime}
and the event
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, either possibility has positive probability.

We recall that there is a natural exploration path
\Lambda
associated with the iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
construction of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}

[
31
]
which we will now describe. For simplicity, we will assume that the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
is in
\mathbf{D}
. We first let
\Lambda_{0}
be given by the path which follows
\partial\mathbf{D}
counterclockwise starting from
-i
. Let
\Lambda_{1}
be given by
\Lambda_{0}
except whenever
\Lambda_{0}
hits a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(0)
loop then it follows it clockwise in its entirety before continuing along
\Lambda_{0}
. We then let
\Lambda_{2}
be given by
\Lambda_{1}
except whenever it hits a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
loop it follows it counterclockwise in its entirety before continuing along
\Lambda_{1}
. We then continue iterating this construction in the false loops of the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(0)
and of the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
’s to obtain a path which follows the entire iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
construction of the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
instance. It is explained in
[
31
]
that this a.s. defines a continuous path, which we call
\Lambda
. We note that if we take
\Lambda
and target it at any point in
\partial\mathbf{D}
then we obtain a CPI in
\Gamma
starting from
-i
(and which has the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
process).

Finally, let us recall that a trunk
\eta^{\prime}
of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
process in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
process where the force point is at
0^{+}
. Moreover, by
[
24
, Theorem 1.4]
(see also
[
36
, Figure 2.5]
) the law of the left (resp. right) boundary of the trunk is that of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-4;2-\kappa)
(resp.
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa/2-2;-\kappa/2)
) process. In particular, the right boundary a.s. does not hit
(-\infty,0)
since
\kappa/2-2
is the critical value at or above which an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
process does not hit the boundary. On the other hand, since
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
we have that
2-\kappa<\kappa/2-2
so that the left boundary a.s. does hit
(0,\infty)
. Finally,
\eta^{\prime}
hits the points in its left (resp. right) boundary in order so can be decomposed into a collection of it excursions from the left (resp. right) boundary.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

We consider the following Markovian resampling operation which preserves the law of
\Lambda
. Let
z\in\mathbf{D}
and
U\in[0,1]
be independently sampled from Lebesgue measure and independently of
\Lambda
. For each
t\geq 0
, we let
D_{t}
be the component of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\Lambda([0,t])
which contains
z
. We let
\tau_{0}
be the first time
t
that the conformal radius
{\rm CR}(z,D_{t})
of
D_{t}
as seen from
z
is at most
U
or
\partial D_{t}
is a loop of
\Gamma
. In the latter case we set
\tau=\tau_{0}
. In the former case, if
\Lambda
is not drawing a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop at time
\tau_{0}
then we also set
\tau=\tau_{0}
. If
\Lambda
is drawing a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop at time
\tau_{0}
, we let
\tau
be the time after
\tau_{0}
that
\Lambda
completes the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop. Suppose that we are working on the event that
\partial D_{\tau}
is not a loop of
\Gamma
. Let
\varphi\colon D_{\tau}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal map with
\varphi(z)=0
and
\varphi(\Lambda(\tau))=-i
. Let
\sigma>\tau
be such that
[\tau,\sigma]
is the interval of time in which
\Lambda
is in
D_{\tau}
. By the definition of
\Lambda
and the iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
construction, modulo time parameterization we have that
\varphi(\Lambda|_{[\tau,\sigma]})
has the same law as
\Lambda
. Let
\overline{x}\in\partial\mathbf{D}
be sampled from Lebesgue measure and
t,\overline{t}\sim\exp(1)
independently of each other and everything else. Let
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
,
\eta_{1}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{1}^{\prime}
be the
4
-tuple of paths as described above defined in terms of the branch of the exploration tree of the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in
\mathbf{D}
from
-i
to
\overline{x}
described by
\varphi(\Lambda|_{[\tau,\sigma]})
and the times
t
,
\overline{t}
. We then resample
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
,
\eta_{1}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{1}^{\prime}
from their conditional law given their range. As we have explained above, by
[
32
, Section 4.1]
we have that on the event that the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loops described by
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
,
\eta_{1}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{1}^{\prime}
intersect it is a positive conditional probability event that after resampling
\eta_{0}^{\prime}
,
\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}
describe part of the same
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loop or a different
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loop.

Suppose that
N
is sampled from the geometric distribution with parameter
1/2
independently of everything else. In the remainder of the proof, we will show that if we run this Markov resampling step
N
times then on the event
E
from the statement it is a positive probability event given
\Upsilon
that a CPI in
\Upsilon
from
-i
to
i
reaches
i
before leaving
\omega^{\epsilon}
. Upon showing this, the proof of the lemma will be complete.

We assume that we are working on
E
. Let
V
be the component of
\mathbf{D}\setminus K^{*}
with
-i,i
on its boundary,
\Gamma_{V}
the loops of
\Gamma
contained in
V
, and let
I_{-}=[a_{-},b_{-}]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
(resp.
I_{+}=[a_{+},b_{+}]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
) be the arc of
\partial\mathbf{D}\cap\partial V
which contains
-i
(resp.
i
). Fix
x_{-}
(resp.
x_{+}
) in the interior of
I_{-}
(resp.
I_{+}
). Let
\Gamma_{V}^{\prime}
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in
V
coupled with
\Gamma_{V}
as in the
\mathrm{BCLE}
construction of
{\rm CLE}
. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the branch of the exploration tree of
\Gamma_{V}^{\prime}
from
x_{-}
to
x_{+}
. Then
\eta^{\prime}
is the trunk of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
process from
x_{-}
to
x_{+}
in
V
coupled with
\Gamma_{V}
as a CPI. As explained above, the right boundary of
\eta^{\prime}
a.s. does not hit
(x_{-},x_{+})_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
. Let
x_{-}^{\prime}\in(a_{-},x_{-})_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
x_{+}^{\prime}\in(a_{+},x_{+})_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
. Let
n
be the number of excursions that
\eta^{\prime}
makes from its right boundary which intersect
[x_{-}^{\prime},x_{+}^{\prime}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
and note that
n
is a.s. finite by the continuity of
\eta^{\prime}
.

More generally, let
\eta_{j}
,
\eta_{j}^{\prime}
be the paths after performing the resampling step
j
times and let
n_{j}
be the number of excursions that
\eta_{j}^{\prime}
makes from its right boundary which intersect
[x_{-}^{\prime},x_{+}^{\prime}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
. Let us assume that
n\geq 1
and let
\eta_{0,1}^{\prime},\ldots,\eta_{0,n}^{\prime}
be the
n
excursions that
\eta^{\prime}=\eta_{0}^{\prime}
makes from its right boundary which hit
[x_{-}^{\prime},x_{+}^{\prime}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
ordered chronologically. We assume that
\eta_{0,1}^{\prime}
starts from where
\eta^{\prime}
first starts drawing it and let
\overline{\eta}_{0,1}^{\prime}
be its time-reversal. Let
\tau_{0,1}
(resp.
\overline{\tau}_{0,1}
) be the first time that
\eta_{0,1}^{\prime}
(resp.
\overline{\eta}_{0,1}^{\prime}
) hits
[x_{-}^{\prime},x_{+}^{\prime}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
and let
W
be a component of
V\setminus(\eta_{0,1}^{\prime}([0,\tau_{0,1}])\cup\overline{\eta}_{0,1}^{\prime}([0,\overline{\tau}_{0,1}]))
whose boundary is contained in and has non-empty intersection with both
\eta_{0,1}^{\prime}([0,\tau_{0,1}])
and in
\overline{\eta}_{0,1}^{\prime}([0,\overline{\tau}_{0,1}])
. We note that such a component a.s. exists.

Given
\eta
and
\Upsilon
, it is a positive probability event that
z\in W
and
U
is such that
\Lambda|_{[0,\tau]}
is in
\partial W\setminus\eta_{0,1}^{\prime}
and has not drawn all of
\partial W\cap\eta_{0,1}^{\prime}
. Given this, there is a positive chance that
\overline{x}
,
t
,
\overline{t}
are such that
\varphi^{-1}(\eta_{0}^{\prime}\cup\eta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\overline{\eta}_{0}^{\prime}\cup\overline{\eta}_{1}^{\prime})
and the loops of
\Gamma
it intersects contains
\partial W
. On this event, as we have explained above, the Markovian resampling step has positive chance of making it so that
n_{1}=n-1
. By repeating this
n-1
further times, we see that there is a positive chance that
\eta_{n}^{\prime}
does not hit
[x_{-}^{\prime},x_{+}^{\prime}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
.

The above implies that on
E
it is a positive conditional probability event given
\Upsilon
that
\eta^{\prime}
does not hit
[x_{-}^{\prime},x_{+}^{\prime}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
. We note that by definition
\eta^{\prime}
is given by targeting at
x_{+}
the path which visits the loops of
\Gamma_{V}^{\prime}
ordered according to when they are hit by
[x_{-},x_{+}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Thus what we have shown is that on
E
it is a positive conditional probability event given
\Upsilon
that the loops of
\Gamma_{V}^{\prime}
which hit
[x_{-},x_{+}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
do not hit
[x_{-}^{\prime},x_{+}^{\prime}]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
