\mathrm{diam}(A)\geq d
then the probability that
\eta^{\prime}
hits
A
is at least
1-O(d^{-a})
for a constant
a>0
. Arguing as above, this implies that
v\to\infty
as
\mathrm{diam}(A)\to\infty
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

We are going to construct the family of quantum surfaces described in the lemma statement using the exploration that we have defined in Section
4.1
and by taking an appropriate subsequential limit. The first step is to control the change in the boundary length process (Step 1), then we will obtain a diameter bound for the approximations (Step 2), then deduce the existence of the subsequential limit (Step 3), and then finally describe the form of the conditional law given the exploration (Step 4).

Fix
K\geq J
. Suppose that we perform the exploration as in Section
4.1
with
J\leq M\leq K
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\downarrow}
) be the quantum surface which consists of all of the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
chunks for the exploration up to when the increasing (resp. decreasing) part with chunks with quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
has been completed.

Step 1. Change in boundary length.

Let
F
be the event that the exploration fails so that
F^{c}
is the event that it does not fail. For each
J\leq M\leq K
, let
T_{M}^{\uparrow}
denote the quantum length of
\partial\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}\setminus\partial\mathcal{H}
. For each
J\leq M
we also let
T_{M}^{\downarrow}
denote the quantum length of
\partial\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\downarrow}\setminus\partial\mathcal{H}
. We are now going to show that for each
\epsilon>0
there exists a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
so that

\mathbf{P}\!\left[\left(\left\{\max_{J\leq M\leq K}T_{M}^{\uparrow}\geq C\right\}\cup\left\{\sup_{J\leq M}T_{M}^{\downarrow}\geq C\right\}\right)\cap F^{c}\right]\leq\epsilon.

Consider the exploration when we have parameterized it by the quantum natural time of the individual
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
chunks. Let
X_{t}
be the overall boundary length process. That is,
X_{t}
is equal to the quantum length of the top boundary (part in
\mathbf{H}
) of the exploration minus the quantum length of the bottom boundary (part in
\partial\mathbf{H}
) of the exploration when
t
units of quantum natural time has elapsed. Note that the quantum length of the top boundary at time
t
is equal to
X_{t}
plus the quantum length of the bottom at time
t
. On the event that the exploration does not fail, i.e.,
F^{c}
occurs, we have that the bottom is contained in
[x_{-1},x_{1}]
. As
\nu_{h}([x_{-1},x_{1}])=2
, the quantum length of the bottom boundary is at most
2
on
F^{c}
. Therefore to give an upper bound to the quantum length of the top boundary on this event it suffices to give an upper bound to
X_{t}
.

Recall that
X_{t}
evolves as a
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy process. On
F^{c}
, we have that
n_{M}^{\uparrow}\leq c_{F}2^{(1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)M}
for each
J\leq M\leq K
,
n_{M}^{\downarrow}\leq c_{F}2^{(1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)M}
for each
M\geq J+1
, and
n_{J}^{\downarrow}\leq c_{F}2^{(\kappa/4)J}
. At the
M
th step of the upward or downward exploration (i.e., when exploring chunks with quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
), on
F^{c}
the amount of quantum natural time involved is thus at most
c_{F}2^{((1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)-1)M}
for
J\leq M\leq K
(upward exploration) or
M\geq J+1
(downward exploration) or at most
c_{F}2^{(\kappa/4-1)J}
(first part of the downward exploration). Note that
(1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)-1<0
as
\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}}\in(0,1)
and
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
and also that
\kappa/4-1<0
. By summing over
M\geq J
, we thus see that the total amount of quantum natural time involved in the exploration on
F^{c}
is at most

(4.10)

T=c_{F}2^{(\kappa/4-1)J}+2c_{F}\sum_{M=J}^{\infty}2^{((1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)-1)M}.

We emphasize here that
T
is be taken to be uniform in
K
. By
[
4
, Chapter VIII, Proposition 4]
, we have that
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}X_{t}
is a.s. finite. Altogether, what we have shown implies that the law of the top boundary length of the exploration is tight as
K\to\infty
on
F^{c}
.

Step 2. Diameter bound.
Fix
M\geq J
. We are going to show that the diameter of
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
is tight on
F^{c}
as
K\to\infty
. Let
x_{-2}<0
be so that
\nu_{h}([x_{-2},0])=2
. Let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
process in
\mathbf{H}
from
x_{-2}
to
\infty
coupled as a CPI in
\Gamma
conditionally independently of the rest of the exploration. Then we know that the conditional law of
\eta
given
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
is that of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
process in
\mathbf{H}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
from
x_{-2}
to
\infty
. Note that we can write
\partial\mathbf{H}\cap\partial\mathcal{E}_{I,J,K}^{\uparrow}
as
[a,b]
. Let
u
(resp.
v
) be the quantum length of
[x_{-2},a]
(resp.
[x_{-2},a]
and
\mathbf{H}\cap\partial\mathcal{E}_{I,J,K}^{\uparrow}
, i.e., the top of
\mathcal{E}_{I,J,K}^{\uparrow}
). As the quantum surface parameterized by
\mathbf{H}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{I,J,K}^{\uparrow}
is a quantum half-plane, it follows that the conditional probability given
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
that
\eta
hits
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
is equal to the probability that the running infimum of a
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy process with both upward and downward jumps starting from
0
hits
[2-v,2-u]
. As explained above, the quantum length
v-u
of the top of
\mathcal{E}_{I,J,K}^{\uparrow}
is tight on
F^{c}
as
K\to\infty
. Therefore the probability that
\eta
hits
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
is bounded away from
1
on
F^{c}
as
K\to\infty
. By Lemma
4.3
, this implies that the law of the diameter of
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
is tight on
F^{c}
as
K\to\infty
.

Step 3. Subsequential limit and law of unexplored region.
Let
g_{M,J,K}
be the unique conformal map from
\mathbf{H}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
to
\mathbf{H}
with
g_{I,J,K}(z)-z\to 0
as
z\to\infty
. Then it follows that the law of
g_{I,J,K}
(with the Caratheodory topology) on
F^{c}
is tight as
K\to\infty
since the diameter of
\mathcal{E}_{M,J,K}^{\uparrow}
on
F^{c}
is tight as
K\to\infty
. Indeed, this follows from
[
18
]
. Suppose that we pass to a sequence
(K_{n})
so that the joint law of any finite collection of the
g_{I,J,K_{n}}
together with
h
converges weakly as
n\to\infty
. Let
g_{I,J}
denote the limit as
n\to\infty
. We abuse notation and write
h
for the instance of
h
which is coupled with
g_{I,J}
as well as with
g_{I,J,K_{n}}
for each
n
. As the quantum surface described by
h\circ g_{I,J,K_{n}}^{-1}+Q\log|(g_{I,J,K_{n}}^{-1})^{\prime}|
is a quantum half-plane for each
n
, it follows that
h\circ g_{I,J}^{-1}+Q\log|(g_{I,J}^{-1})^{\prime}|
is a quantum half-plane as well. This proves that (
ii
) holds.

Step 4. Form of the conditional law.
By the construction of the subsequential limit, it follows that (
iii
) holds. That the final points hold is clear from the construction.
∎

We now deduce the analog of Lemma
4.2
in the case of the quantum disk. We note that the exploration that we have defined in Section
4.1
makes sense on a doubly marked quantum disk, although the estimates we have previously established for its basic properties are for the exploration in the case of the quantum half-plane. The purpose of the following lemma is to deduce these properties when we work instead on the disk.

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
\ell\geq 2
and suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathbf{D},h)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
. Suppose that
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
is sampled from
\nu_{h}
and
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
is such that
\nu_{h}([x,y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
. Let
z\in[x,y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
be such that
\nu_{h}([x,z]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=\nu_{h}([z,y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=\nu_{h}([x,y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})/2=(\ell-1)/2
. For each
J\in\mathbf{N}
, there is a family of quantum surfaces
\mathcal{E}_{I,J}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathcal{E}_{I,J}^{\downarrow}
so that all of the properties of Lemma
4.2
hold but with the quantum half-plane replaced by
\mathcal{D}
and the marked points at
0
,
x_{1}
,
\infty
replaced by
x
,
y
, and
z
, respectively.

In order to deduce Lemma
4.4
from Lemma
4.2
, we first record the following Radon-Nikodym derivative (
[
33
, Proposition 5.1]
) which serves to compare
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
explorations on quantum disks and on quantum half-planes.

Lemma 4.5
.

Fix
\ell>0
and suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathbf{D},h)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
. Suppose that
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
is sampled from
\nu_{h}
, let
L_{0},R_{0}>0
be such that
\ell=L_{0}+R_{0}
, and let
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
be such that
\nu_{h}([x,y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=L_{0}
and
\nu_{h}([x,y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=R_{0}
. Suppose that
\eta
is an independent
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
in
\mathcal{D}
from
x
to
y
which is parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk. For each
t\geq 0
, we let
\mathcal{D}_{t}
denote the component of
\mathcal{D}\setminus\eta([0,t])
with
y
on its boundary and let
L_{t}
(resp.
R_{t}
) denote the quantum length of
[\eta(t),y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
(resp.
[\eta(t),y]_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
). Let
E_{t}
be the event that
\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}L_{s}>0
and
\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}R_{s}>0
. On
E_{t}
, the Radon-Nikodym derivative between the law of
(L,R)|_{[0,t]}
and a pair of independent
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy processes starting from
(L_{0},R_{0})
on
[0,t]
is given by
(\Delta_{0}/\Delta_{t})^{4/\kappa+1}
where
\Delta_{t}=L_{t}+R_{t}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
,
x
,
y
,
z
are as in the statement of the lemma. Then we can consider the same approximation scheme using the exploration from Section
4.1
as in the proof of Lemma
4.2
. Indeed, even though we defined the exploration in Section
4.1
in the case of the half-plane, it still makes sense to consider it in the case of the quantum disk. Let
\Delta_{0}=\ell
be the boundary length of
\partial\mathcal{D}
. For each
J\leq M
, we let
\Delta_{M}^{\downarrow}
be the boundary length of the complementary component with
z
on its boundary after we have completed the part of the exploration where the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
chunk sizes are in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
. Note that the event that the exploration has not failed up until this point, the analog of the event
E_{t}
from Lemma
4.5
up to this time holds. Therefore on this event, Lemma
4.5
implies that the Radon-Nikodym derivative between the half-planar and disk explorations is of the form
(\Delta_{0}/\Delta_{M}^{\downarrow})^{4/\kappa+1}
. Due to the definition of the exploration failing, on the event that has not failed we have that
\Delta_{M}^{\downarrow}
is bounded from below. This completes the proof of the result.
∎

5.
Tightness on the boundary

The purpose of this section is to prove the tightness of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
restricted to the domain boundary. We are going to prove the result in a more general setting than in the statement of Theorem
1.1
in the sense that we will not restrict to the case that the domain boundary is given by a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop. Instead, we will make an assumption on the behavior of the quantum area measure when we consider a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
parameterized by the domain. (We note that this is effectively an assumption about the roughness of
\partial D
.) In what follows, when we write “quantum length metric on
\partial D
” for
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
simply connected we mean the metric on (the prime ends of)
\partial D
where the distance between
x,y
is given by the minimum of the quantum lengths of
[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
and
[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>2
and fix
\beta\in(0,2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}})
. Suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(D,h,x,y)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Fix
c_{0},\epsilon_{0}>0
and let
E
be the event that for every
z\in D
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
so that
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. Let
\Gamma
be an independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
and let
\Upsilon
be its carpet. Let
X_{\epsilon,\beta}
be the
\beta
-Hölder norm of
(z,w)\mapsto({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
with respect to the quantum length metric on
\partial D
. For every
p>0
we have that

(5.1)

\mathbf{P}[E,\ X_{\epsilon,\beta}\geq A]=O(A^{-p})\quad\text{as}\quad A\to\infty

where the implicit constants depend on
p,\epsilon_{0},c_{0},\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}},\beta
.

Moreover, let
Y_{\beta}
be the
\beta
-Hölder norm of the Euclidean metric with respect to the quantum length metric on
\partial D
. Then we have that

(5.2)

\mathbf{P}[E,\ Y_{\beta}\geq A]=O(A^{-p})\quad\text{as}\quad A\to\infty

where the implicit constants depend on
p,\epsilon_{0},c_{0},\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}},\beta
.

In the setting of Proposition
5.1
, truncating on the event that the quantum area measure is bounded from below serves to give a lower bound on the size of the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
chunks which make up the exploration described in Lemma
4.4
. The reason that this is important is that our definition of the good event for a chunk which is given just below will involve the minimal “
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}
-length” of various crossings when one has parameterized the chunk by
\mathbf{D}
. Embedding the chunk into
D
involves applying a conformal transformation which can lead to an effective change in the size of the neighborhood used to approximate the “length” of a path. As we will see in the proof of Proposition
5.1
, the lower bound on the quantum area measure will serve to give an upper bound on the size of an embedded chunk hence an upper bound on the “length” of an embedded path.

Let us now explain the main steps used to prove Proposition
5.1
.

Step 1.

We will first give the events for the good chunks in Definition
5.2
and then show in Lemma
5.3
that we can adjust the parameters in the definition to make the probability that they occur sufficiently close to
1
so that the results of Section
4
apply.

Step 2.

The definition of a good chunk in Definition
5.2
will involve quantities of the form
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
for paths
\omega
which arise after we have parameterized the chunk by
\mathbf{D}
. We then need to control the Lebesgue measure of the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of these paths after embedding them and this will be the usage of Lemma
5.4
.

Step 3.

We complete the proof of Proposition
5.1
using Lemma
4.4
. The idea is that we will start with two boundary points
x
,
y
and perform the exploration as in Lemma
4.4
. If the exploration succeeds, then our previous estimates and the definition of a good chunk will allow us to construct a path
\omega
in
\Upsilon
with control on
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
. If the exploration fails, then we will repeat the exploration as in Lemma
4.4
in the remaining domains. The exploration will be repeated either once or twice depending on the type of failure. We will refer to these new explorations as the “children” of the failed exploration so that we get a tree structure on such explorations. We will show that by properly adjusting the parameters we can dominate the number of times the exploration has to be repeated by the size of a subcritical Galton-Watson tree, which will complete the proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}=(\mathbf{H},h,0,\infty)
be a quantum half-plane. Suppose that
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
curve on
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
which is taken to be independent of
h
and then parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk. For the quantum surface
\mathcal{N}_{t}
disconnected from
\infty
by
\eta|_{[0,t]}
, we let
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{t})
(resp.
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{t})
) denote the leftmost (resp. rightmost) point on the bottom of
\mathcal{N}_{t}
. We also let
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{t})=\eta(0)
.

Figure 5.1.

Top:
Illustration of part (
ii
) of Definition
5.2
. Shown in blue is the exploration up until
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
is discovered and the images of
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
under
\varphi
are respectively given by
-1
,
-i
, and
1
, respectively.
Bottom:
Illustration of part (
iv
) of Definition
5.2
. The chunk
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
is shown in light yellow even though it is “good” in order to differentiate it from
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
. The images of
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
under
\psi
are
-1
,
-i
, and
1
, respectively.

Definition 5.2
.

We suppose that we have the setup described in Section
4.1
. Fix
R_{1},R_{2},S_{1},S_{2}\geq 1
. For each
i,M
, we define
E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
to be the event for
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
and
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
that
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}(\sigma_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\in\partial\mathbf{H}
and the following hold.

(i)

The quantum lengths of the top, left side of the bottom, and the right side of the bottom of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
are in
[R_{1}^{-1}2^{-(\kappa/4)M},R_{1}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}]
.

(ii)

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal transformation which takes
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
to
-1
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
to
-i
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
to
1
. There is a path
\omega
connecting
B(-1,R_{2}^{-1})
to
B(1,R_{2}^{-1})
in
\varphi(\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
which has distance at least
R_{1}^{-1}
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
and satisfies
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq R_{1}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}
.

(iii)

\mu_{h}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\leq R_{1}2^{-M}
.

(iv)

Let
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
be the chunk whose top contains
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}(0)
(so that the left side of the bottom of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
is glued to the top of
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
) if it exists, otherwise we set
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}=\emptyset
. If
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}\neq\emptyset
, let
\psi
be the unique conformal map which takes
\mathcal{N}=\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}\cup\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))
to
\mathbf{D}
so that
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
is taken to
-1
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
is taken to
-i
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
is taken to
1
. The harmonic measure of each of
[-1,\psi(z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
,
[\psi(z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})),\psi(z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
, and
[\psi(z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})),1]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
as seen from
0
is at least
R_{2}^{-1}
. Let
w
be the midpoint of
[\psi(z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})),\psi(z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
. Then there moreover is a path
\omega
in
\psi(\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{N})
which connects
B(-i,S_{2}^{-1})
to
B(w,S_{2}^{-1})
which has distance at least
S_{1}^{-1}
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
and satisfies
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq S_{1}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}
.

(v)

If
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
is another
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
chunk which starts from a point on the top of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
with clockwise boundary length distance from
\partial\mathcal{H}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
given by an integer multiple of
a_{0}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}
and with boundary length distance from a point in
\Upsilon
at most
a_{0}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}
and is run until the first time it hits the domain boundary after
\delta_{0}2^{-J}
,
J\in\{M-1,M,M+1\}
, then the conditional probability given
\mathcal{F}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
that the previous item holds for the pair
(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow},\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})
in place of
(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
is at least
1-R_{2}^{-1}
.

We define
E_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
analogously.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose that we have the setup described in Section
4.1
and the events
E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
,
E_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
are as in Definition
5.2
. For every
p_{0}\in(0,1)
there exists
R_{1}\geq R_{2}\geq 1
and
S_{2}\geq S_{1}\geq 1
so that

(i)

On the event that the exploration has not failed before exploring
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
) we have that
\mathbf{P}[E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i-1,M}^{\uparrow}]\geq p_{0}
(resp.
\mathbf{P}[E_{i,M}^{\downarrow}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i-1,M}^{\downarrow}]\geq p_{0}
) for all
i,M\in\mathbf{N}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
is as in part (
iv
) of Definition
5.2
,
\omega_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
(resp.
\omega_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
) is any path for
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
) satisfying part (
ii
) of Definition
5.2
. Then any path as in part (
iv
) of Definition
5.2
crosses both
\omega_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
and
\omega_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
.

Proof.

It is clear from the scaling properties of
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy process that (
i
) holds with probability tending to
1
as
R_{1}\to\infty
. Lemma
3.1
implies that with any choice of
R_{2}\geq 1
we have that (
ii
) holds with probability tending to
1
as
R_{1}\to\infty
. We have that
\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i-1,M}^{\uparrow}]=\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})]=O(2^{-M})
as
\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})]
is equal to a constant times the sum of the squares of the jumps made by the associated Lévy process (which are run for time at most
2^{-M}
). Indeed, the range of the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
itself has zero quantum area so all of the quantum area is in the quantum disks that it disconnects from
\infty
and the expected quantum area associated with a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
is equal to a constant times
\ell^{2}
. Therefore Markov’s inequality implies that (
iii
) holds with probability tending to
1
as
R_{1}\to\infty
. That (
iv
) and (
v
) hold with probability tending to
1
as
R_{1},S_{1}\to\infty
for any fixed choice of
R_{2},S_{2}
follows from Lemma
3.2
. Finally, for any values
R_{1}\geq R_{2}\geq 1
it is clear that the second assertion of the lemma holds by making
S_{2}\geq 1
sufficiently large.
∎

Before we proceed to the proof of Proposition
5.1
, we need to collect the following elementary lemma. As in the case of paths, for
K\subseteq\mathbf{C}
and
\epsilon>0
we let
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(K)
denote the Lebesgue measure of the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
K
.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
so that the following is true for all
\epsilon>0
and
\delta\in(0,1/2)
. Suppose that
K\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is connected and compact. Then we have that

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\delta\epsilon}(K)\geq c_{0}\delta{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(K).

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
and
\delta\in(0,1/2)
. Let
(z_{k})
be a countable, dense subset of
K
. By the compactness of
K
, there exists
z_{j_{1}},\ldots,z_{j_{n}}
so that
K\subseteq\cup_{i=1}^{n}B(z_{j_{i}},\epsilon)
. By the Vitali covering lemma, there exists
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\subseteq\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\}
so that the balls
B(z_{i_{j}},\epsilon)
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
are pairwise disjoint and
K\subseteq\cup_{j=1}^{\ell}B(z_{i_{j}},3\epsilon)
. Note that

\pi\epsilon^{2}\ell\leq{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(K)\leq 9\pi\epsilon^{2}\ell.

Since
K
is connected, for each
1\leq j\leq\ell
, we can find at least
\lfloor\delta^{-1}/2\rfloor
disjoint balls with centers in
K\cap B(z_{i_{j}},\epsilon/2)
and radius
\epsilon\delta
. It therefore follows that

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon\delta}(K)\geq\pi(\epsilon\delta)^{2}\times\lfloor\delta^{-1}/2\rfloor\times\ell\geq\frac{\delta^{2}\lfloor\delta^{-1}/2\rfloor}{9}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(K).

This proves the result as
\delta^{2}\lfloor\delta^{-1}/2\rfloor\geq\delta/2
for all
\delta\in(0,1/2)
.
∎

We now proceed to give the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

We will first explain the proof of (
5.1
) and then explain at the end how the same argument gives (
5.2
). We assume that we have chosen
\delta_{0},p_{0}\in(0,1)
so that the assertion of Lemma
4.1
holds. With these parameters, we then assume that we have chosen
R_{2}\geq R_{1}\geq 1
and
S_{2}\geq S_{1}\geq 1
so that the assertion of Lemma
5.3
holds. In what follows, we may therefore view
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
,
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
as constants. We perform the exploration from Lemma
4.4
using the events defined in Definition
5.2
in a recursive manner as follows.

Fix
\delta,\zeta>0
. Suppose that
x_{0},x_{1}\in\partial\mathcal{D}
are so that
\nu_{h}([x,x_{0}]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=\zeta
and
\nu_{h}([x_{0},x_{1}]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=\delta
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be given by taking
\mathcal{D}
and replacing
h
with
h_{0}=h+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log\delta^{-1}
. Let
x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0}
be the corresponding points for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Then
\nu_{h_{0}}([x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
. We then perform the exploration as in Lemma
4.4
in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
from
x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{0}
until it fails. We recall that the exploration fails if it makes either a large upward jump (i.e., of size at least
2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}}(\kappa/4)M}
when exploring chunks with quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
), a large downward jump (i.e., of size at least
2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}}(\kappa/4)M}
when exploring chunks with quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
), requires too many chunks at a particular scale (i.e., at least
c_{F}2^{(1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)M}
when exploring chunks of size in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
for
M>J
or at least
c_{F}2^{(\kappa/4)J}
when exploring chunks of size in
[\delta_{0}2^{-J},2^{-J}]
), or the deviations in the boundary length process are too large (i.e., the absolute value of the quantum length of the top minus the bottom exceeds
c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)M}
when exploring chunks of size in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
). We stop the exploration immediately if either of these possibilities happens, and then proceed as described below.

Figure 5.2.
Illustration of the exploration in
D
from
x_{0}=x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{1}=x_{1}^{0}
(blue) up until it fails due to making a large upward jump (i.e., a large
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop is discovered) shown in red. The prime ends
z_{0}^{0}
and
z_{1}^{0}
correspond to where the loop is rooted on the exploration. When this happens, two new explorations are started in the remaining domain going from
x_{0}^{0}
to
z_{0}^{0}
and from
z_{1}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{0}
.

Figure 5.3.
Illustration of the exploration in
D
from
x_{0}=x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{1}=x_{1}^{0}
(blue) up until it fails due to making a large downward jump with the corresponding chunk shown in red.
Left:
In the case that
x_{0}^{0}
and
x_{1}^{0}
are not disconnected, a new exploration is started in the remaining domain with
x_{0}^{0}
,
x_{1}^{0}
on its boundary from
x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{0}
.
Right:
In the case that
x_{0}^{0}
and
x_{1}^{0}
are disconnected, we let
z_{0}^{0}
and
z_{1}^{0}
be the two prime ends in the two complementary domains corresponding to where the downward jump occurred. Two new explorations are started, one from
x_{0}^{0}
to
z_{0}^{0}
and the other from
z_{1}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{0}
.

Case 1: large upward jump.
See Figure
5.2
for an illustration. If the exploration fails by making a large upward jump, we let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration with
x_{0}^{0}
,
x_{1}^{0}
on its boundary and let
\widetilde{h}_{0}
be the field which describes
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. We let
z_{0}^{0}
,
z_{1}^{0}
be the two points on
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{00}
which correspond to where the loop corresponding to the upward jump is rooted. We then let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be the surface described by
h_{00}=\widetilde{h}_{0}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
where
L_{00}=\nu_{\widetilde{h}_{0}}([x_{0}^{0},z_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the corresponding points. We also let
\mathcal{D}_{01}
be the surface described by
h_{01}=\widetilde{h}_{0}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{01}^{-1}
where
L_{01}=\nu_{\widetilde{h}_{0}}([z_{1}^{0},x_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{01}
,
x_{1}^{01}
for the corresponding points. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{0i},x_{0}^{0i},x_{1}^{0i})
for
i=0,1
as the
children
of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
. We then perform the exploration inside of
\mathcal{D}_{0i}
from
x_{0}^{0i}
to
x_{1}^{0i}
for
i=0,1
starting with
i=0
and then with
i=1
with the following exception. If the exploration with
i=0
fails with a large downward jump which disconnects
x_{0}^{01}=z_{1}^{0}
from
x_{1}^{01}=x_{1}^{0}
then we do not perform the exploration from
x_{0}^{01}=z_{1}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{01}=x_{1}^{0}
and instead at the next stage perform the exploration from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{01}
(analogously to how the exploration proceeds in the case of a large downward jump which does not disconnect the initial and terminal points as described just below).

Case 2: large downward jump.
See Figure
5.3
for an illustration. If the exploration fails by making a large downward jump, there are two possibilities. Either
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
are disconnected or they are not. If
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
are not disconnected, then we let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration with
x_{0}^{0}
,
x_{1}^{0}
on its boundary and let
\widetilde{h}_{0}
be the field which describes
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. We let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be the quantum surface described by
h_{00}=\widetilde{h}_{0}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
where
L_{00}=\nu_{\widetilde{h}_{0}}([x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the corresponding points. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{00},x_{0}^{00},x_{1}^{00})
as the
child
of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
and then continue the exploration in
\mathcal{D}_{00}
from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{00}
.

Now suppose that
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
are disconnected. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
) be the quantum disk with
x_{0}
(resp.
x_{1}
) on its boundary. Let
\widetilde{h}_{0}
(resp.
\widetilde{h}_{1}
) be the field which describes
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
). Let
z_{0}
(resp.
z_{1}
) be the point on
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
(resp.
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
) which corresponds to where the downward jump happened. We then let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be the quantum surface described by
h_{00}=\widetilde{h}_{0}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
where
L_{00}=\nu_{\widetilde{h}_{0}}([x_{0},z_{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and call
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
the corresponding points. We also let
\mathcal{D}_{01}
be the quantum surface described by
h_{01}=\widetilde{h}_{1}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{01}^{-1}
where
L_{01}=\nu_{\widetilde{h}_{1}}([z_{1},x_{1}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and write
x_{0}^{01}
,
x_{1}^{01}
for the corresponding points. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{0i},x_{0}^{0i},x_{1}^{0i})
for
i=0,1
as the children of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
. We then perform the exploration inside of
\mathcal{D}_{0i}
from
x_{0}^{0i}
to
x_{1}^{0i}
for
i=0,1
.

Case 3: too many chunks or top boundary length too long.
Finally, we consider the situation in which the exploration fails by either having
n_{M}^{\uparrow}
,
n_{M}^{\downarrow}
too large for
M\geq J
or the quantum length of the top of the exploration exceeds
C2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}
for a constant
C>0
(we will adjust the value of
C
later in the proof; note that
1-4/\kappa<0
since
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
). We note that the quantum length of the top can only exceed
C2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}
by at most
2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}}(\kappa/4)J}
for otherwise the exploration would have failed due to a large upward jump. We let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration with
x_{0}^{0}
,
x_{1}^{0}
on its boundary. Let
\widetilde{h}_{0}
be the field which describes
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
. We let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be the quantum surface described by
h_{00}=\widetilde{h}_{0}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
where
L_{00}=\nu_{\widetilde{h}_{0}}([x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the corresponding points. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{00},x_{0}^{00},x_{1}^{00})
as the child of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
and then continue the exploration in
\mathcal{D}_{00}
from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{00}
.

Completion of the proof.
The above defines a tree structure of explorations
(\mathcal{D}_{o},h_{o},x_{0}^{o},x_{1}^{o})
together with scaling factors
L_{o}
for
o\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
n\in\mathbf{N}
where each node can either have zero children (exploration does not fail), one child (large downward jump which does not separate the endpoints, too many chunks at a fixed stage, or the top boundary length is too large), or two children (large downward jump which separates the endpoints or a large upward jump). Let
\mathcal{T}
be the tree associated with the exploration and for
o\in\mathcal{T}
we let
o^{\prime}
be the parent of
o
. Note that
x_{0}^{o},x_{1}^{o}
correspond to points
\widetilde{x}_{0}^{o}
,
\widetilde{x}_{1}^{o}
on the boundary of the exploration from
x_{0}^{o^{\prime}}
to
x_{1}^{o^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{D}_{o^{\prime}}
. Moreover,
L_{o}
is the quantum length of the counterclockwise arc from
\widetilde{x}_{0}^{o^{\prime}}
to
\widetilde{x}_{1}^{o^{\prime}}
along the boundary of the exploration from
x_{0}^{o}
to
x_{0}^{o^{\prime}}
. In particular,
L_{0}=\delta
. On the event
E
that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in D
so that
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
h_{0}
satisfies
\mu_{h_{0}}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq c_{0}\delta^{-2}\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in D
so that
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq D
. The reason for this is that adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log\delta^{-1}
to the field has the effect of multiplying quantum areas by the factor
\delta^{-2}
.

Lemma
4.4
implies that by choosing
J\in\mathbf{N}
and
C>0
large enough we can ensure that the mean number of children of each node in
\mathcal{T}
is strictly smaller than
1
so that the corresponding Galton-Watson tree is subcritical. Since the offspring distribution is supported on
\{0,1,2\}
, the size of the Galton-Watson tree also has an exponential tail.

We are now going to use the definition of the
E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
,
E_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
from Definition
5.2
to construct a path
\omega
from
x_{0}
to
x_{1}
contained in
\Upsilon
and bound the tail of
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
.

Let us first consider the exploration from
x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{0}^{1}
in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
in the case that it does not fail. We say that two chunks
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
,
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
for which
E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
and
E_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
occur and
L\geq M
(also
\ell\geq i
if
L=M
) are adjacent if
\eta_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}(0)
is on the top of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
(and the same with
\downarrow
in place of
\uparrow
). Let
\mathcal{N}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbf{Z}
be the bi-infinite sequence of adjacent chunks. Then parts (
ii
) and (
iv
) of Definition
5.2
give us a way to construct a path connecting
x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{0}
as follows. For each
n\in\mathbf{Z}
, we let
\omega_{n}
be the path in
\mathcal{N}_{n}
corresponding to part (
ii
) of Definition
5.2
and we let
\omega_{n-1,n}
be the path in
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{n-1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{n}))
corresponding to part (
iv
) of Definition
5.2
. Then successively concatenating the paths
\omega_{n-1,n}
,
\omega_{n}
leads to a path
\omega
in
\Upsilon
connecting
x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{0}
and we have that

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}}({\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega_{n})+{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega_{n-1,n})).

Let
\varphi_{n}\colon\mathcal{N}_{n}\to\mathbf{D}
be the map as defined in part (
ii
) of Definition
5.2
. Then we know that
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\varphi_{n}(\omega_{n}))\leq R_{1}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}
and
\varphi_{n}(\omega_{n})
has distance at least
R_{1}^{-1}
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
. Let

\overline{d}_{n}=\sup_{z\in B(0,1-R_{1}^{-1})}|(\varphi_{n}^{-1})^{\prime}(z)|\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{d}_{n}=\inf_{z\in B(0,1-R_{1}^{-1})}|(\varphi_{n}^{-1})^{\prime}(z)|.

For each
\zeta>0
, let
A_{n,\zeta}
(resp.
B_{n,\zeta}
) be the
\zeta
-neighborhood of
\varphi_{n}(\omega_{n})
(resp.
\omega_{n}
). Then as
B_{n,\underline{d}_{n}\epsilon}\subseteq\varphi_{n}(A_{n,\epsilon})
we have that

(5.3)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{N}}_{\underline{d}_{n}\epsilon}(\omega_{n})\leq\int_{A_{n,\epsilon}}|(\varphi_{n}^{-1})^{\prime}(z)|^{2}dz\leq R_{1}\overline{d}_{n}^{2}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

By Lemma
5.4
we have for a constant
c_{0}>0
that

(5.4)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\underline{d}_{n}\epsilon}(\omega_{n})\geq c_{0}\underline{d}_{n}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega_{n}).

Distortion estimates for conformal maps imply that
\overline{d}_{n}/\underline{d}_{n}
is bounded from above by a constant which depends only on
R_{1}
. Consequently, by combining (
5.3
) with (
5.4
) we see for a constant
c_{1}>0
that

(5.5)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega_{n})\leq c_{1}\overline{d}_{n}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

If
\mathcal{N}_{n}=\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
or
\mathcal{N}_{n}=\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
, then we have that
\mu_{h_{0}}(\mathcal{N}_{n})\leq R_{1}2^{-M}
. Consequently,
\mu_{h}(\mathcal{N}_{n})\leq R_{1}\delta^{2}2^{-M}
.

It therefore follows that on
E
we have for a constant
c_{2}>0
that

(5.6)

\underline{d}_{n}\leq c_{2}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}2^{-M/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}.

Combining (
5.5
) with (
5.6
) implies for a constant
c_{3}>0
that

(5.7)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega_{n})\leq c_{3}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}2^{-M/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

By a similar argument, we have (possibly increasing
c_{3}>0
) that

(5.8)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega_{n-1,n})\leq c_{3}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}2^{-M/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

Recall from the definition of the exploration succeeding that if it does succeed then the number of chunks with quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
with
M\geq J+1
is at most a constant times
2^{(1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)M}
and with
M=J
is at most a constant times
2^{(\kappa/4)J}
. We assume that we have chosen
\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}}\in(0,1)
sufficiently close to
1
so that
\alpha=(1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)-1/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}<0
. If we sum (
5.7
), (
5.8
) over
n
, we thus see that those terms coming from chunks with natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
for
M\geq J+1
contribute a constant times
2^{-\alpha J}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}
. With
\alpha=(\kappa/4)-\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}^{-1}
, those terms with
M=J
contribute a constant times
2^{\alpha J}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}
. Altogether, we have for a constant
c_{4}>0
that

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq c_{4}2^{\alpha J}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

Let us now describe how we recursively construct the path
\omega
in the case that the exploration fails at some stage. Consider the tree
\mathcal{T}
associated with iterating the exploration as defined above. We note that
\mathcal{T}
has a natural planar structure which comes from the ordering of the exploration. We let
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
consist of the nodes in
\mathcal{T}
which either have zero or two children and we view
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
as a planar tree with the tree structure and ordering coming from that of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
o_{1},\ldots,o_{n}
be the leaves of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
(given according to their contour order). For each
1\leq j\leq n
, we let
\omega_{j}
be the path which is defined in the same manner as
\omega
just as above except using the exploration associated with the node
o_{j}
(which we emphasize does not fail as
o_{j}
is a leaf). We let
\omega
be the concatenation of the
\omega_{j}
. Then

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega_{j})\leq c_{4}2^{\alpha J}\left(\prod_{o}L_{o}^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\right){\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}

where the product is over those
o\in\mathcal{T}
which are ancestors of
o_{j}
. The reason for this is that if we add
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{o}^{-1}
to the field to multiply boundary lengths by
L_{o}^{-1}
it has the effect of multiplying areas by
L_{o}^{-2}
. Since
L_{o}\leq C
,
L_{0}=\delta
, and the number of ancestors of
o_{j}
is trivially at most
|\mathcal{T}|
, the above is in turn bounded from above by

c_{4}2^{\alpha J}C^{2|\mathcal{T}|/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

Therefore

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)\leq c_{4}2^{\alpha J}|\mathcal{T}|C^{2|\mathcal{T}|/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

We note that making
J
large has the effect of improving the tail of
|\mathcal{T}|
. Therefore for every value of
p>0
we can choose
J
sufficiently large so that for a constant
c_{5}>0
we have

\mathbf{E}[(({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega))^{p}{\bf 1}_{E}]\leq c_{5}\delta^{2p/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}.

Fix
\beta\in(0,2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}})
. As
p>0
was arbitrary, the Kolmogorov-Centsov theorem thus gives that the law of the
\beta
-Hölder norm of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
(with respect to the quantum length metric on
\partial D
) satisfies (
5.1
).

We note that (
5.2
) follows from the proof of (
5.1
) except instead of using the paths
\omega_{n}
,
\omega_{n-1,n}
we instead use the images of straight lines under
\varphi_{n}^{-1}
. The same argument used to prove (
5.7
), (
5.8
) implies that the diameter of such a path is at most a constant times
\delta^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}2^{-M/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. Therefore we can string together these paths in place of the
\omega_{n}
,
\omega_{n-1,n}
to complete the proof of (
5.2
).
∎

6.
Tightness in the interior

The aim of this section is to extend the tightness result from Section
5
to points in the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
carpet which are contained in the interior of the domain. As in Section
5
, we will state and prove the result in a more general setting than described in the statement of Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 6.1
.

For each
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>2>\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}>0
there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}>0
so that the following is true. Suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(D,h,x,y)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Fix
\epsilon_{0}>0
and let
E
be the event that for every
z\in D
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
so that
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\leq\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. Let
\Gamma
be an independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
and let
\Upsilon
be its carpet. Let
X_{\epsilon}
be the
\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}
-Hölder norm of
(z,w)\mapsto({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
with respect to the Euclidean metric on
D
. For every
p>0
we have that

\mathbf{P}[E,\ X_{\epsilon}\geq A]=O(A^{-p})\quad\text{as}\quad A\to\infty

where the implicit constants depend only on
p,\epsilon_{0},\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}},\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}
.

The proof of Proposition
6.1
will involve several steps which we will now describe.

Step 1.

We fix
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}>0
and for each
j\in\mathbf{N}
we let
N_{j}=2^{\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}j}
then pick
(z_{n})
i.i.d. from
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
. By Lemma
B.1
, the points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N_{j}}
are very likely to form a
2^{-j}
-net of
\Upsilon
. We then aim to show in the remaining steps that if we have
1\leq k,\ell\leq N_{j}
such that
|z_{\ell}-z_{k}|\leq 2^{-j}
then
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},z_{k};\Gamma)
is very likely to be at most
2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}j}
for some constant
\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}>0
.

Step 2.

We will argue in Lemma
6.2
that there is a constant
\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}>0
so that there does not exist
z\in\Upsilon
and
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
so that
\mathcal{L}\setminus B(z,\delta)
has more than one component of diameter at least
\delta^{1/\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}}
. (We will also need a version where we rule out there being more than one component of
\mathcal{L}\setminus B(z,\delta)
with quantum length at least
\delta^{1/\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}}
.)

Step 3.

We then use the percolation exploration from Section
4
to show that for every
z\in\Upsilon
with
\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial D)<\delta
there exists a path
\eta
in
\Upsilon
so that the component
U
of
D\setminus\eta
containing
z
has diameter at most a power of
\delta
and every pair of points
u,v\in\eta\cap\partial U
satisfy that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v;\Gamma)
is also at most a power of
\delta
(Lemma
6.8
). We now aim to combine this with Steps 1 and 2 to bound
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},z_{k};\Gamma)
as described at the end of Step 1.

Step 4.

For each
z\in\Upsilon
and
r>0
we let
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z,r)
be the set of
w\in\Upsilon
such that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)\leq r
. We next establish a
lower bound
on the Euclidean diameter of
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},r)
for each
1\leq\ell\leq N_{j}
(Lemma
6.9
). The bound will be so that it implies for a constant
\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}>0
that if
|z_{\ell}-z_{k}|\leq 2^{-j}
then it very likely that
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}j})
,
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z_{k},2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}j})
have diameter much larger than
2^{-j}
but it will not be clear at this point that
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}j})
,
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z_{k},2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}j})
intersect.

Step 5.

We now fix a value of
1\leq\ell\leq N_{j}
and consider an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
exploration from a point on
\partial D
targeted at
z_{\ell}
and coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI. We assume we are working on the event that points within distance
2^{-j}
of each other are not separated from each other by a loop of
\Gamma
of size at least
2^{-j/\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}}
. It is a consequence of the results in Appendix
A
that if we parameterize
\eta
according to the amount of quantum area that its trunk
\eta^{\prime}
has disconnected from
z_{\ell}
then
\eta^{\prime}
is Hölder continuous with some exponent
\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}
. For each
m\in\mathbf{N}
we let
t_{m}=m2^{-j/(\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})}
. By Step 3, we have for each
m\in\mathbf{N}
that the condition of Step 3 holds for the component of
D\setminus\eta([0,t_{m}])
which contains
z_{\ell}
with very high probability. Let
\tau
be the first time
t
that
\eta^{\prime}
gets within distance
2^{-j/\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}}
of
z_{\ell}
and let
m_{0}
be such that
\tau\in(t_{m_{0}},t_{m_{0}+1}]
. We fix another value of
1\leq k\leq N_{j}
. On the event that
|z_{\ell}-z_{k}|\leq 2^{-j}
we have that
\eta|_{[0,\tau]}
hence also
\eta|_{[0,t_{m_{0}}]}
does not disconnect
z_{\ell}
,
z_{k}
(as this would only be possible if there were a loop which violated Step 2). Step 3 implies that we can find paths
\omega_{\ell},\omega_{k}
in the component of
D\setminus\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])
which contains
z_{\ell}
which satisfy the properties mentioned above for
z_{\ell}
,
z_{k}
. The sets
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z_{k},2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}j})
,
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}j})
from Step 4 then have to intersect
\omega_{\ell}
,
\omega_{k}
which gives an overall bound on
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}]);\Upsilon)
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{k},\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}]);\Upsilon)
. Proposition
5.1
then gives an upper bound on
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u_{\ell},u_{k};\Upsilon)
where
u_{\ell}
,
u_{k}
are where
\omega_{\ell}
,
\omega_{k}
, respectively, hit
\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])
. Altogether, this gives an upper bound to
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},z_{k};\Upsilon)
which is a power of
2^{-j}
. All of these estimates will be combined carefully in Lemma
6.10
.

Step 6.

We complete the proof by applying an argument analogous to that used to prove the Kolmogorov-Centsov continuity criterion.

6.1.
{\rm CLE}
loop pinch point bound

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a bounded simply connected domain,
(D,h,x,y)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
, and
\Gamma
is an independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
. Fix
\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}>0
. We say that
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
has an
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point if one of the following hold.

(i)

The quantum length of
\mathcal{L}
exceeds
2\epsilon
and there exists
z\in\mathcal{L}
so that if
z_{1}
(resp.
z_{2}
) is the point in
\mathcal{L}
so that
\nu_{h}([z,z_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=\epsilon
(resp.
\nu_{h}([z,z_{2}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=\epsilon
) then
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\cap B(z,\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}})\neq\emptyset
or

(ii)

The diameter of
\mathcal{L}
exceeds
2\epsilon
and there exists
z\in\mathcal{L}
such that if
z_{1}
(resp.
z_{2}
) is the first counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) point in
\mathcal{L}
starting from
z
so that
\mathrm{diam}([z,z_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=\epsilon
(resp.
\mathrm{diam}([z,z_{2}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=\epsilon)
then
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\cap B(z,\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}})\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a bounded simply connected domain and
(D,h,x,y)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Suppose that
\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}},\epsilon_{0}>0
. There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}},\beta>0
depending only on
\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}
so that the following is true. Let
E_{1}
be the event that for every
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in D
so that
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. Let
E_{2}
be the event that there is
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
with an
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point. Then
\mathbf{P}[E_{1}\cap E_{2}]=O(\epsilon^{\beta})
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
.

In what follows, for
\ell>0
we let
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
be the law on quantum surfaces
(D,h)
whose Radon-Nikodym derivative with respect to
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
is given by a normalizing constant times
\mu_{h}(D)
. As
\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h}(D)]<\infty
under
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
, we have that
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
indeed makes sense as a probability measure. We note that it suffices to prove Lemma
6.2
under
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
in place of
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
since
\mu_{h}(D)
has finite negative moments of all orders
[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
. Given that
(D,h)
has law
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
, it is natural to consider
(D,h)
marked by a point
z
chosen from
\mu_{h}
and we will also write
(D,h,z)\sim\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
in this case.

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists
\alpha,\beta,c_{0}>0
so that the following is true. Suppose that
(\mathbf{D},h,0)\sim\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Then

\mathbf{P}[\exists\theta\in[0,2\pi):\nu_{h}([e^{i\theta},e^{i(\theta+\epsilon)}]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})\geq\epsilon^{\beta}]\leq c_{0}\epsilon^{\alpha}\quad\text{for all}\quad\epsilon>0.

Proof.

In the case that
(\mathbf{D},h,-i,i)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
, this follows from the same argument used to prove Lemma
A.6
except here we use the moment upper bound for the boundary length measure for a free boundary GFF. We will thus omit the details in this case. The result in the case of
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
follows since
[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
implies that
\mu_{h}(D)
has a finite
p
th moment for each
p<4/\gamma^{2}

[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
so we can get the result from the case of
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
and apply Hölder’s inequality.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Let
\alpha
,
\beta
be as in Lemma
6.3
and let
\epsilon,\delta>0
. We assume that
(D,h,z)\sim\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. As remarked after the lemma statement, it suffices to give the proof in this setting. Note that
z
is a.s. surrounded by a loop
\mathcal{L}_{z}
of
\Gamma
. Let
D_{z}
be the component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathcal{L}_{z}
which contains
z
. Let
\varphi_{z}\colon D_{z}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal map with
\varphi_{z}(z)=0
,
\varphi_{z}^{\prime}(z)>0
, and let
h_{z}=h\circ\varphi_{z}^{-1}+Q\log|(\varphi_{z}^{-1})^{\prime}|
. Let
F
be the event that
\mathcal{L}_{z}
has quantum length at least
\epsilon
and there exists
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
so that
\nu_{h_{z}}([e^{i\theta},e^{i(\theta+\delta)}]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})\geq\delta^{\beta}
. Letting
\ell>0
be the quantum length of
\mathcal{L}_{z}
, we have that the conditional law of
(\mathbf{D},h_{z},0)
given
\ell
is equal to
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
. Consequently, Lemma
6.3
implies that
\mathbf{P}[F]=O((\delta/\epsilon)^{\alpha})
.

Fix
\xi>1
and
\sigma>0
. Let
G
be the event that

(i)

E_{1}
occurs

(ii)

\mu_{h}(D)\leq\epsilon^{-\sigma}
and

(iii)

there is
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
with quantum length at least
2\epsilon
which satisfies (
i
) in the definition of an
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point and the domain surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
has quantum area at least
\epsilon^{2\xi}
.

Let us now make a few observations. First, it follows from Lemma
B.4
that for each
a>0
there exists
b>0
so that the probability that the number of loops of
\Gamma
in
D
with quantum length at least
\epsilon
is
O(\epsilon^{-4/\kappa-1/2-a})
is
1-O(\epsilon^{b})
. By
[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
, we have that the total quantum area associated with a sample from
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
for
\ell\geq\epsilon
is at least
\epsilon^{2\xi}
off an event whose probability decays to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
faster than any power of
\epsilon
. Combining these two facts implies that off an event whose probability decays to
0
faster than any power of
\epsilon
as
\epsilon\to 0
, the quantum area of the region surrounded by each loop of
\Gamma
with quantum length at least
\epsilon
is at least
\epsilon^{2\xi}
. Moreover, by
[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
we have that
\mathbf{P}[\mu_{h}(D)\geq\epsilon^{-\sigma}]=O(\epsilon^{(4/\gamma^{2}-1)\sigma})
. It thus follows that
\mathbf{P}[E_{1}\cap E_{2}]\leq\mathbf{P}[G]+O(\epsilon^{(4/\gamma^{2}-1)\sigma})
.

We will argue that there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
so that

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[F\,|\,G]\geq c_{0}\epsilon^{2\xi+\sigma}

which, in turn, implies that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[G]\leq\frac{\mathbf{P}[F]}{\mathbf{P}[F\,|\,G]}=\frac{O((\delta/\epsilon)^{\alpha})}{c_{0}\epsilon^{2\xi+\sigma}}=O(\delta^{\alpha}\epsilon^{-\alpha-2\xi-\sigma}).

Combining, this will prove the result provided we take
\delta
to be a sufficiently large power of
\epsilon
.

Suppose that we are working on
G
, that
\mathcal{L}
is a loop of
\Gamma
with quantum length at least
2\epsilon
and suppose that
\mathcal{L}
satisfies (
i
) in the definition of an
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point. We assume further that the domain
U
surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
satisfies
\mu_{h}(U)\geq\epsilon^{2\xi}
and that among all such loops
\mathcal{L}
is leftmost (smallest real part) and among all such leftmost loops is bottommost (smallest imaginary part). We let
w
be the
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point of
\mathcal{L}
which is leftmost and among all leftmost
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch points of
\mathcal{L}
is bottommost. We note that if
d\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
\mathrm{diam}(U)\leq d
then on
E_{1}
we have that
\mu_{h}(U)\leq d^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. That is, we have that
\epsilon^{2\xi}\leq d^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. Therefore
\mathrm{diam}(U)\geq\min(\epsilon^{2\xi/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}},\epsilon_{0})
. Since
\mu_{h}(D)\leq\epsilon^{-\sigma}
and
\mu_{h}(U)\geq\epsilon^{2\xi}
, it follows that
\mathbf{P}[z\in U\,|\,G]\geq\epsilon^{2\xi+\sigma}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{P}[z\in B(w,\delta)\,|\,G,\ z\in U]=O(\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}\epsilon^{-2\xi})
. Suppose that we are working on
G
and
z\in U\setminus B(w,\delta)
. Let
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal transformation with
\varphi(z)=0
and
\varphi^{\prime}(z)>0
. Then there exists a component
L
of
\mathcal{L}\setminus B(z,\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}})
which has quantum length at least
\epsilon
and is not part of the boundary of the component of
U\setminus B(w,\delta)
which contains
z
. The Beurling estimate implies that
\varphi(L)
is mapped to an interval of
\partial\mathbf{D}
of (Euclidean) length
O((\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}}/\delta)^{1/2})
. Altogether, this implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[F\,|\,G]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}[z\in U\setminus B(w,\delta)\,|\,G]\geq\mathbf{P}[z\in U\,|\,G](1-\mathbf{P}[z\in B(w,\delta)\,|\,G,\ z\in U])

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon^{2\xi+\sigma}(1-O(\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}\epsilon^{-2\xi})).

We assume that
\delta
is a sufficiently large power of
\epsilon
so that
O(\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}\epsilon^{-2\xi})=o(1)
as
\epsilon\to 0
. This implies (
6.1
). Taking
\delta
to be an even larger power of
\epsilon
so that the power of
\epsilon
in the right side of (
6.2
) is positive and then
\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}>0
large enough so that
(\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}}/\delta)^{1/2}=O(\delta^{\beta})
completes the proof in the case of
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
pinch points satisfying (
i
). The proof is analogous for
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
pinch points satisfying (
ii
).
∎

6.2.
Proof of tightness in the interior

The first step to completing the proof of Proposition
6.1
is to give the definition of a good chunk for the adaptive
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
exploration of a quantum half-plane
\mathcal{H}=(\mathbf{H},h,0,\infty)
(see Figure
6.1
for an illustration). The definition that we will give below will be different than in Section
5
because the percolation exploration will serve a different purpose. Suppose that
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
from
0
to
\infty
sampled independently of
h
and then subsequently parameterized according to the quantum natural time of its trunk. As in the beginning of Section
5
, for the quantum surface
\mathcal{N}_{t}
disconnected from
\infty
by
\eta|_{[0,t]}
, we let
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{t})
(resp.
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{t})
) denote the leftmost (resp. rightmost) point on the bottom of
\mathcal{N}_{t}
. We also let
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{t})=\eta(0)
.

Figure 6.1.

Top:
Illustration of part (
ii
) of Definition
6.4
. Shown in blue is the exploration up until
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
is discovered and the images of
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
under
\varphi
are respectively given by
-1
,
-i
, and
1
.
Bottom:
Illustration of part (
iii
) of Definition
6.4
. The chunk
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
is shown in light yellow even though it is “good” in order to differentiate it from
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
. The images of
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow})
under
\psi
are
-1
,
-i
, and
1
, respectively.

Definition 6.4
.

We suppose that we have the setup described in Section
4.1
. Fix
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\geq 1
. For each
i,M
, we define
E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
to be the event for
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
and
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
that
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}(\sigma_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\in\partial\mathbf{H}
and the following hold.

(i)

The quantum lengths of the top, the left side of the bottom, and the right side of the bottom of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
are in
[R_{1}^{-1}2^{-(\kappa/4)M},R_{1}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}]
.

(ii)

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal transformation with
\varphi(z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))=-1
,
\varphi(z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))=-i
, and
\varphi(z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))=1
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}=\varphi^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
. Then we have that

R_{2}^{-1}2^{-M}\leq\mu_{h}(\mathcal{B}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\leq\mu_{h}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\leq R_{2}2^{-M}.

(iii)

Let
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
be the chunk whose top contains
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}(0)
(so that the left side of the bottom of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
is glued to the top of
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
) if it exists, otherwise we set
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}=\emptyset
. If
\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}\neq\emptyset
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
be the quantum surface parameterized by
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}\cup\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}))
and let
\psi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal map so that
\psi(z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}))=-1
,
\psi(z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}))=-i
, and
\psi(z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}))=1
. Then

(I)

R_{2}^{-1}2^{-M}\leq\mu_{h}(\psi^{-1}(B(0,1-R_{2}^{-1})))\leq\mu_{h}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})\leq 4R_{2}2^{-M}
and

(II)

\psi^{-1}(B(0,1-R_{2}^{-1}))
contains both
\mathcal{B}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
.

(iv)

If
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
is another
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
chunk defined using the time-interval
[\delta_{0}2^{-j},2^{-j}]
for
j\in\{M-1,M,M+1\}
which starts from a point on the top of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
with clockwise boundary length distance from
\partial\mathcal{H}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
given by an integer multiple of
a_{0}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}
chosen in an
\mathcal{F}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
-measurable manner and with boundary length distance from a point in
\Upsilon
at most
a_{0}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}
, then the conditional probability given
\mathcal{F}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
that the previous item holds for the pair
(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow},\widehat{N})
in place of
(\mathcal{N}_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow},\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow})
is at least
1-R_{3}^{-1}
.

We define
E_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
analogously to
E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose that we have the setup described in Section
4.1
and the events
E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
,
E_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
are as in Definition
6.4
. For every
p_{0}\in(0,1)
there exists
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\geq 1
,
\delta_{0}>0
so that on the event that the exploration has not failed before exploring
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\downarrow}
), we have that
\mathbf{P}[E_{i,M}^{\uparrow}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i-1,M}^{\uparrow}]\geq p_{0}
(resp.
\mathbf{P}[E_{i,M}^{\downarrow}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{i-1,M}^{\downarrow}]\geq p_{0}
) for all
i,M\in\mathbf{N}
.

Proof.

Lemmas
C.2
and
C.4
imply that we can take
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
sufficiently small so that the probability that
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}(\sigma_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\in\partial\mathbf{H}
is at least
1-q_{0}/3
where
q_{0}=1-p_{0}
. The scaling properties of
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy processes imply that (
i
) holds with probability tending to
1
as
R_{1}\to\infty
. Likewise, it is also clear that (
ii
) holds with probability tending to
1
as
R_{2}\to\infty
. By the definition of
E_{\ell,L}^{\uparrow}
, we have that (
iii
) holds with probability at least
1-R_{3}^{-1}
. We choose
R_{3}\geq 1
sufficiently large so that
1-R_{3}^{-1}\geq 1-q_{0}/3
. To see that (
iv
) holds with probability tending to
1
as
R_{2}\to\infty
(when
R_{3}
is fixed), we note that we can construct the gluing of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
as follows. Recall that
\mathcal{H}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
is the quantum half-plane which corresponds to the unexplored region when generating
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
. We suppose that we are working on the event that
\eta_{i,M}^{\uparrow}(\sigma_{i,M}^{\uparrow})\in\partial\mathbf{H}
and properties (
i
) and (
ii
) of Definition
6.4
hold. Then there are at most
R_{1}a_{0}^{-1}
points on the top of
\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
with clockwise boundary length distance to
\partial\mathcal{H}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
given by an integer multiple of
a_{0}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}
and with boundary length distance from a point in
\Upsilon
at most
a_{0}2^{-(\kappa/4)M}
. For a given such point chosen in an
\mathcal{F}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
-measurable manner, we run another
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
process
\widehat{\eta}
in
\mathcal{H}_{i+1,M}^{\uparrow}=\mathcal{H}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}
. Suppose that we have fixed
j\in\{M-1,M,M+1\}
. On the event that
\widehat{\sigma}=\inf\{t\geq\delta_{0}2^{-j}:\widehat{\eta}(t)\in\partial\mathcal{H}_{i+1,M}^{\uparrow}\}\leq 2^{-j}
and property (
i
) of Definition
6.4
holds for the surface
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
disconnected from
\infty
by
\widehat{\eta}|_{[0,\widehat{\sigma}]}
, the surface
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
parameterized by
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{i,M}^{\uparrow}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{N}}))
is homeomorphic to
\mathbf{D}
. Therefore it is clear that (
iv
) holds with probability tending to
1
as
R_{2}\to\infty
(with
R_{1},R_{3}\geq 1
fixed) as well.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose that
D
is a bounded, simply connected domain,
(D,h)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
, and
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
which is independent of
h
. Fix
0<\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}<2<\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}},\epsilon_{0}>0
. Let
E
be the event that for every
z\in D
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
we have that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\leq\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
and no loop of
\Gamma
has an
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point for
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
. For each
\beta>0
there exists
\xi>0
so that the following is true. Fix
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
and let
F
be the event that for every
z\in D
with
\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial D)\leq\epsilon
there exists a curve
\eta
in
\Upsilon
so that

(i)

\mathfrak{d}_{\delta}(u,v;\Gamma)/{\mathfrak{m}}_{\delta}^{\mathrm{HP}}\leq\epsilon^{\xi}
for all
u,v\in\eta
and

(ii)

the component
U
of
D\setminus\eta
which contains
z
satisfies
\mathrm{diam}(U)\leq\epsilon^{\xi}
and
\partial U\cap\partial D\neq\emptyset
.

Uniformly in
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
we have that
\mathbf{P}[E\cap F^{c}]=O(\epsilon^{\beta})
.

Proof.

Step 0: Setup.
Suppose that
x\in\partial D
is picked from
\nu_{h}
. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we let
N_{k}=2^{k}
and let
I_{1},\ldots,I_{N_{k}}
be arcs of
\partial D
of equal quantum length
2^{-k}
with disjoint interior whose union is
\partial D
. We assume that the
I_{j}
are given in counterclockwise order and the left endpoint of
I_{1}
is
x
. Fix
1\leq j\leq N_{k}
and let
I=I_{j}
. Fix
\zeta>0
. We will adjust the value of
\zeta
in the proof. The role of
\epsilon>0
in the statement of the lemma will be played by
2^{-\zeta k}
. Let
U=U_{j}
be the
2^{-\zeta k}
-neighborhood of
I
with respect to the internal (Euclidean) metric in
D
. Suppose that
\delta\in(0,2^{-\zeta k})
. We are going to show that there exists
\xi>0
(which does not depend on
k
) so that there exists a path
\eta
in
\Upsilon
which satisfies (
i
) and such that
\mathrm{diam}(U)\leq\epsilon^{\xi}
off an event of probability
O(2^{-(1+\beta)k})
. The result will then follow by performing a union bound over
1\leq j\leq N_{k}
and then over
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

Let
x_{0}
be the left endpoint of
I_{j-1}
and let
x_{1}
be the right endpoint of
I_{j+1}
, where we take the convention that
I_{0}=I_{N_{k}}
and
I_{N_{k}+1}=I_{1}
. Then
\nu_{h}([x_{0},x_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=3\cdot 2^{-k}
and
I\subseteq[x_{0},x_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. We let
h_{0}=h+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log(2^{k}/3)
so that with
x_{0}^{0}=x_{0}
and
x_{1}^{0}=x_{1}
we have that
\nu_{h_{0}}([x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
. We let
\mathcal{D}_{0}=(D,h_{0})
be the resulting (rescaled) quantum disk. We then perform the exploration as in Lemma
4.4
from
x_{0}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{0}
where the definition of a good chunk is as in Definition
6.4
and where we take
J=\xi_{0}k
and
\xi_{0}>0
is a fixed small constant whose value we will adjust later in the proof.

Step 1. Exploration succeeds.
We first suppose that we are on the event that the exploration does not fail and we will explain why there exists a path
\eta
as claimed. See Figure
6.2
for an illustration of the setup. Afterwards, we will explain how to iterate the exploration to construct a path
\eta
in the case that the exploration fails. Let
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{n}
be the (good) chunks in the exploration which have (the maximal) quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-\xi_{0}k},2^{-\xi_{0}k}]
(as measured using
h_{0}
). Note that if we rescale the boundary length back by the factor
3/2^{k}
(to transform
h_{0}
back to
h
) then quantum natural time gets scaled by the factor
(3/2^{k})^{4/\kappa}
. Therefore the (maximal) quantum natural time of a good chunk under the exploration as measured by
h
is equal to a constant times
2^{-\xi_{1}k}
where
\xi_{1}=\xi_{0}+4/\kappa
. For each
1\leq\ell\leq n-1
, let
\psi_{\ell}
be the unique conformal map from
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\ell+1}))
to
\mathbf{D}
which takes
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
to
-1
,
-i
, and
1
, respectively. We then let
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}=\psi_{\ell}^{-1}(B(0,1-R_{2}^{-1}))
.

Figure 6.2.
Illustration of the definition of
\omega_{\ell}
in the case that the exploration succeeds. Shown is part of
\partial D
which contains
I_{j-1}
,
I_{j}=I
, and
I_{j+1}
. The blue regions are the initial and terminal parts of the exploration before and after the chunks with the maximal size are discovered. Among the chunks with maximal size, those in green are good (with the exception of
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
to differentiate it from
\mathcal{N}_{\ell+1}
) and those in red are bad. Recall that
\psi_{\ell}
is the unique conformal map from
\mathrm{int}(\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\ell+1}))
to
\mathbf{D}
which takes
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
,
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
respectively to
-1
,
-i
, and
1
and
\varphi_{\ell}
is the unique conformal map from
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
to
\mathbf{D}
which takes
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
,
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
respectively to
-1
,
-i
, and
1
. Shown also in red are the regions
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}=\varphi_{\ell}^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
and
\mathcal{B}_{\ell+1}=\varphi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
. The dashed region which contains
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\ell+1}
is
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}=\psi_{\ell}^{-1}(B(0,1-R_{2}^{-1}))
. The path
\omega_{\ell}
(orange) is the image under
\psi_{\ell}^{-1}
of the concatenation of the line segment from
z_{\ell}^{1}=\psi_{\ell}(\varphi_{\ell}^{-1}(0))
to
0
and from
0
to
z_{\ell}^{2}=\psi_{\ell}(\varphi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(0))
.

We are now going to show that we can adjust the value of
\zeta>0
so that
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{n}\cup(\cup_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{A}_{\ell})
contains a path
\omega
which connects
I_{j-1}
to
I_{j+1}
and has (Euclidean) distance at least
2^{-\zeta k}
from
I_{j}
. This, in turn, will imply that there is a component of
D\setminus\omega
which contains
U
. To see this, for each
1\leq\ell\leq n-1
we let
\varphi_{\ell}\colon\mathcal{N}_{\ell}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal map which takes
z_{L}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
,
z_{C}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
, and
z_{R}(\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
to
-1
,
-i
, and
1
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}=\varphi_{\ell}^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
. Then we know from property (
iii
) of Definition
6.4
that
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}
contains
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\ell+1}
. By property (
ii
) of Definition
6.4
,

(6.3)

\mu_{h}(\mathcal{B}_{\ell})=\left(\frac{3}{2^{k}}\right)^{2}\mu_{h_{0}}(\mathcal{B}_{\ell})\geq R_{2}^{-1}2^{-(\xi_{0}+2)k}=R_{2}^{-1}2^{-\xi_{2}k}\quad\text{where}\quad\xi_{2}=\xi_{0}+2.

From the definition of
E
, we have that

\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{B}_{\ell})^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}\geq\mu_{h}(\mathcal{B}_{\ell})\geq R_{2}^{-1}2^{-\xi_{2}k}.

That is, on
E
we have that

(6.4)

\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{B}_{\ell})\geq R_{2}^{-1/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}2^{-\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}.

Assume that
1\leq\ell\leq n-1
. We now define a path
\omega_{\ell}
as follows. We let
z_{\ell}^{1}=\psi_{\ell}(\varphi_{\ell}^{-1}(0))
and
z_{\ell}^{2}=\psi_{\ell}(\varphi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(0))
. We then let
\omega_{\ell}
be the image under
\psi_{\ell}^{-1}
of the concatenation of
[z_{\ell}^{1},0]
with
[0,z_{\ell}^{2}]
. As
B(0,1-R_{2}^{-1})
contains
\psi_{\ell}(\mathcal{B}_{\ell})
and
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}
has diameter at least
R_{2}^{-1/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}2^{-\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
, standard distortion estimates for conformal maps imply that

|(\psi_{\ell}^{-1})^{\prime}(z)|\gtrsim 2^{-\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}\quad\text{for all}\quad z\in B(0,1-R_{2}^{-1})

where the implicit constant in
\gtrsim
depends only on
R_{2}
. Therefore there exists a (
R_{2}
-dependent) constant
c_{0}>0
so that
\mathcal{T}
contains the (Euclidean)
c_{0}2^{-\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
neighborhood of
\omega_{\ell}
. We take
\zeta>\xi_{2}/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}
.

In order to finish proving the claim, it is left to explain why:

•

\mathcal{N}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}_{n}
) intersects
I_{j-1}
(resp.
I_{j+1}
) and

•

We can define a path
\omega_{0}
in
\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{1}
which connects
I_{j-1}
to the starting point of
\omega_{1}
and a path
\omega_{n}
in
\mathcal{A}_{n-1}\cup\mathcal{N}_{n}
which connects the ending point of
\omega_{n-1}
to
I_{j+1}
so that both
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{n}
have distance at least
2^{-\zeta k}
from
I_{j}
.

The first point is in fact a consequence of the definition of the exploration succeeding. This leaves us to address the second point.

Let us first explain how to construct the path
\omega_{0}
. Consider the set
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\varphi_{1}^{-1}(B(0,1/2))
. As explained above, we know that
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{B}_{1})\geq R_{2}^{-1/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}2^{-\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. Let
L_{1}
(resp.
L_{2}
) be the line segment in
\mathbf{D}
from
-1/4
(resp.
1/4
) to the point
z_{1}=-i
(resp. the point
z_{2}
on the bottom of
\partial\mathbf{D}
with real part
1/4
). We claim that there exists a constant
c>0
so that
\mathrm{dist}(\varphi_{1}^{-1}(L_{1}),\varphi_{1}^{-1}(L_{2}))\geq c2^{-\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. To see this, let
B
be a Brownian motion starting from
0
. Then
B
has a positive chance of exiting
\partial\mathbf{D}
in
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
before hitting
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
. Therefore the same is also true for the Brownian motion
\varphi_{1}^{-1}(B)
. The claim therefore follows from the Beurling estimate. We can therefore take
\omega_{0}
to be the image under
\varphi_{1}^{-1}
of the line segment from the point on the bottom of
\partial\mathbf{D}
with real part
1/8
to
0
and we see that the distance of
\omega_{0}
to
I_{j}
is at least
c2^{-\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
(possibly decreasing
c>0
). We define
\omega_{n}
in a similar manner.

We now define the path
\eta
. Let
x\in\partial D\setminus(I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1})
be any fixed point. On the event that the exploration succeeds, its outer boundary as viewed from
x
does not depend on the choice of
x
. We take
\eta
to be the curve which corresponds to the outer boundary of the exploration as viewed from
x
and assume that
\eta
is parameterized by quantum length. We note that in this case the quantum length of
\eta
is at most a constant times
2^{-k}
. On
E
, (
5.2
) from Proposition
5.1
thus gives an upper bound on
\mathrm{diam}(I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1})
and
\mathrm{diam}(\eta)
which is a power of
2^{-k}
off an event which occurs with probability
O(2^{-(1+\beta)k})
, which completes the proof in the case that the exploration does not fail.

Step 2. Exploration fails.
We now consider the possibilities which can occur if the exploration described above fails. Measuring quantities using
h_{0}
, we recall that the exploration fails if it makes either a large upward jump (i.e., of size at least
2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}}(\kappa/4)M}
when exploring chunks with quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
), a large downward jump (i.e., of size at least
2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}}(\kappa/4)M}
when exploring chunks with quantum natural time in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
), requires too many chunks at a particular scale (i.e., at least
c_{F}2^{(1-\alpha_{\mathrm{PERC}})(\kappa/4)M}
when exploring chunks of size in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
for
M>J
or at least
c_{F}2^{(\kappa/4)J}
when exploring chunks of size in
[\delta_{0}2^{-J},2^{-J}]
), or the deviations in the boundary length process are too large (i.e., the absolute value of the quantum length of the top minus the bottom exceeds
c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)M}
when exploring chunks of size in
[\delta_{0}2^{-M},2^{-M}]
). We stop the exploration immediately if either of these possibilities happens, and then proceed as described below.

Figure 6.3.
Illustration of the possibility that the exploration fails by making an upward jump whose size measured using
h_{0}
is at least
3
. Shown in blue is the exploration up until it makes the large upward jump and the corresponding
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop is shown in red. The two prime ends which correspond to where the loop is rooted on the previous exploration are
\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0}
,
\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0}
. The point
z_{0}^{0}
(resp.
z_{0}^{1}
) is the one on the loop so that the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) boundary length from
\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0}
to
z_{0}^{0}
(resp.
\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0}
to
z_{1}^{0}
) measured using
h_{0}
is equal to
1
. Since we are working on the event that
\Gamma
does not have any
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch points for
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
, by choosing
\zeta>0
sufficiently large (depending only on
\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}
) we have that the clockwise arc of the loop from
z_{0}^{0}
to
z_{1}^{0}
does not hit the ball of radius
2^{-\zeta k}
centered at
\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0}
. The point
w_{0}^{0}
(resp.
w_{1}^{0}
) is the one which is
1
unit of boundary length distance in the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) direction from
x_{0}^{0}
(resp.
x_{1}^{0}
) measured using
h_{0}
. The exploration is then continued from
w_{0}^{0}
to
z_{0}^{0}
and from
z_{1}^{0}
to
w_{1}^{0}
.

Case 1: large upward jump.
We let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
