be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration with
x_{0}^{0}
and
x_{1}^{0}
on its boundary. We let
\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0}
,
\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0}
be the two prime ends on
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
which correspond to where the discovered
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop
\mathcal{L}
is rooted to the boundary. There are two possibilities. Either the size of the upward jump (measured using
h_{0}
) is smaller or larger than
3
.

We first suppose that the upward jump (measured using
h_{0}
) has size at most
3
. We let
z_{0}^{0}
(resp.
z_{1}^{0}
) be the point on
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
so that
\nu_{h_{0}}([z_{0}^{0},x_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
(resp.
\nu_{h_{0}}([x_{1}^{0},z_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
). We let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be given by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
after adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
to the field where
L_{00}=\nu_{h_{0}}([z_{0}^{0},z_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the points which correspond to
z_{0}^{0}
,
z_{1}^{0}
. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{00},x_{0}^{00},x_{1}^{00})
as the
child
of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
. We then perform the exploration inside of
\mathcal{D}_{00}
from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{00}
with the definition of the good chunk as in Definition
6.4
. We note in this case that

(6.5)

3-c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}\leq L_{00}\leq 6+c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}.

We now suppose that the upward jump (measured using
h_{0}
) has size at least
3
. See Figure
6.3
for an illustration in this case. We let
z_{0}^{0}
(resp.
z_{1}^{0}
) be the point on
\mathcal{L}
so that
\nu_{h_{0}}([\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0},z_{0}^{0}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=1
(resp.
\nu_{h_{0}}([z_{1}^{0},\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=1
). This means that

\nu_{h}([\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0},z_{0}^{0}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})\geq 2^{-k}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{h}([z_{1}^{0},\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})\geq 2^{-k}.

It thus follows from the definition of
E
that if we assume
\zeta>1/\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}
then
[z_{0}^{0},z_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
does not intersect
B(\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0},2^{-\zeta k})
.

We also let
w_{0}^{0}
(resp.
w_{1}^{0}
) be the point on
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
so that
\nu_{h_{0}}([w_{0}^{0},x_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
(resp.
\nu_{h_{0}}([x_{1}^{0},w_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
. We let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be given by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
after adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
to the field where
L_{00}=\nu_{h_{0}}([w_{0}^{0},z_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the points corresponding to
w_{0}^{0}
,
z_{0}^{0}
. We also let
\mathcal{D}_{01}
be given by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
after adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{01}^{-1}
where
L_{01}=\nu_{h_{0}}([z_{1}^{0},w_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{01}
,
x_{1}^{01}
for the points which correspond to
z_{1}^{0}
,
w_{1}^{0}
. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{0i},x_{0}^{0i},x_{1}^{0i})
for
i=0,1
as the
children
of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
. We then perform the exploration inside of
\mathcal{D}_{0i}
from
x_{0}^{0i}
to
x_{1}^{0i}
for
i=0,1
with the definition of the good chunk as in Definition
6.4
. We note in this case that

(6.6)

2\leq L_{0i}\leq 3+c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}\quad\text{for}\quad i=0,1.

We will now explain how to construct the path
\omega
in the case that both of the explorations succeed. As mentioned just above, we have that the distance between
[z_{0}^{0},z_{0}^{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
and
\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0}
is at least
2^{-\zeta k}
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
be the good chunks from the exploration in
\mathcal{D}_{00}
from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{00}
which have the maximal chunk size. Arguing as in the proof that the exploration succeeds to begin with, we can construct a path inside of
\mathrm{cl}(\cup_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{N}_{j})
which has distance at least
2^{-\zeta k}
(possibly increasing
\zeta>0
) from
I_{j}
and connects
I_{j-1}
to
x_{1}^{00}=z_{0}^{0}
. We then take the concatenation of this path with
[z_{0}^{0},\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Finally, we concatenate this path with
[\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0},z_{1}^{0}]_{\mathcal{L}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and the analogous path constructed from the exploration in
\mathcal{D}_{01}
from
x_{0}^{01}=z_{1}^{0}
to
x_{1}^{01}=x_{1}^{0}
.

If one of the two explorations fail, then we iterate the procedure depending on the type of failure in the same manner that we continue the iteration procedure in the case that the initial exploration has failed with the following exception. If the exploration in
\mathcal{D}_{00}
from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{00}
has a large downward jump whose terminal point disconnects
x_{0}^{01}
and
x_{1}^{01}
, then we do not perform the exploration in
\mathcal{D}_{01}
from
x_{0}^{01}
to
x_{1}^{01}
and only perform an exploration from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{01}
in the component which contains these two points (as in the first type of large downward jump considered just below).

Figure 6.4.
Illustration of the possibility that the exploration fails by making a large downward jump.
Top:
The terminal point of the downward jump is within
2
units of boundary length distance (measured using
h_{0}
) from
I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1}
. Shown is the case that the terminal point is not in
I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1}
but it is also possible for it to be in
I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1}
.
Bottom:
The terminal point of the downward jump is more than
2
units of boundary length distance (measured using
h_{0}
) from
I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1}
.

Case 2: large downward jump.
There are two possibilities in the case that the exploration makes a large downward jump: either the terminal point of the downward jump is within boundary length distance
2
(measured using
h_{0}
) of
I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1}
or it is not. See Figure
6.4
for an illustration of the setup.

Suppose that the terminal point of the downward jump is within boundary length distance
2
(measured using
h_{0}
) of
I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1}
. Let
w_{0}^{0}
(resp.
w_{1}^{0}
) be such that
\nu_{h_{0}}([w_{0}^{0},x_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=3
(resp.
\nu_{h_{0}}([x_{1}^{0},w_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=3
) and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration whose boundary contains
[w_{0}^{0},w_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\mathcal{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
. We then let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be given by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
after adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
to the field where
L_{00}=\nu_{h_{0}}([z_{0}^{0},z_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the points on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{00}
which correspond to
w_{0}^{0}
,
w_{1}^{0}
. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{00},x_{0}^{00},x_{1}^{00})
as the child of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
. We then perform the exploration inside of
\mathcal{D}_{00}
from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{00}
. In the case that this exploration succeeds, we take the path to be defined in an analogous manner as to when the exploration did not have a large downward jump. We note in this case that

(6.7)

4\leq L_{00}\leq 7+c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}.

Now suppose that the terminal point of the downward jump has boundary length distance from
I_{j-1}\cup I_{j}\cup I_{j+1}
at least
2
(measured using
h_{0}
). We let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration with
x_{0}^{0}
on its boundary. We let
\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0}
be the point on
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
which is the terminal point of the large downward jump and let
z_{0}^{0}\in\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be such that
\nu_{h_{0}}([\widetilde{z}_{0}^{0},z_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1/2
. We also let
w_{0}^{0}\in\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be such that
\nu_{h_{0}}([w_{0}^{0},x_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1/2
. We let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be given by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{00}
after adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
to the field where
L_{00}=\nu_{h_{0}}([w_{0}^{0},z_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the points corresponding to
w_{0}^{0}
,
z_{0}^{0}
. We let
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration with
x_{1}^{0}
on its boundary, let
\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0}\in\partial\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the terminal point of the large downward jump, and let
z_{1}^{0}\in\partial\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be such that
\nu_{h_{0}}([z_{1}^{0},\widetilde{z}_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1/2
. We also let
w_{1}^{0}\in\partial\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be such that
\nu_{h_{0}}([x_{1}^{0},w_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1/2
and let
\mathcal{D}_{01}
be given by
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
after adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{01}^{-1}
where
L_{01}=\nu_{h_{0}}([z_{1}^{0},w_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{01}
,
x_{1}^{01}
for the points corresponding to
z_{1}^{0}
,
w_{1}^{0}
, respectively. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{0i},x_{0}^{0i},x_{1}^{0i})
for
i=0,1
as the children of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
. We then perform the exploration inside of
\mathcal{D}_{0i}
from
x_{0}^{0i}
to
x_{1}^{0i}
for
i=0,1
. We note in this case that

(6.8)

1\leq L_{0i}\leq 2+c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}\quad\text{for}\quad i=0,1.

In the case that both explorations succeed, we take the path which is given by concatenating the path associated with
(\mathcal{D}_{00},x_{0}^{00},x_{1}^{00})
followed by the clockwise arc of
\partial D
from
x_{1}^{00}
to
x_{0}^{01}
and then the path associated with
(\mathcal{D}_{01},x_{0}^{01},x_{1}^{01})
. Arguing as in the same way as the exploration succeeds, if both of these explorations succeed then this path will have the desired properties.

If either exploration fails, we continue the exploration as described above in the new quantum disk.

Case 3: too many chunks or top boundary length too long.
Finally, we consider the situation in which the exploration fails by either having
n_{M}^{\uparrow}
,
n_{M}^{\downarrow}
too large for
M\geq J
or the absolute value of the difference of the top of the exploration minus the bottom (as measured using
h_{0}
) exceeds
c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}
. We note that the quantum length of the top can only exceed that of the bottom by at most
c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}+3
for otherwise the exploration would have failed due to a large upward jump. We let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
be the quantum disk in the complement of the exploration with
x_{0}^{0}
,
x_{1}^{0}
on its boundary. Let
\widetilde{h}_{0}
be the field which describes
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and let
w_{0}^{0}
(resp.
w_{1}^{0}
) be the point in
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
so that
\nu_{h_{0}}([w_{0}^{0},x_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
(resp.
\nu_{h_{0}}([x_{1}^{0},w_{0}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=1
). We let
\mathcal{D}_{00}
be the quantum surface described by
h_{00}=\widetilde{h}_{0}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{00}^{-1}
where
L_{00}=\nu_{\widetilde{h}_{0}}([w_{0}^{0},w_{1}^{0}]_{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and we write
x_{0}^{00}
,
x_{1}^{00}
for the points which correspond to
w_{0}^{0}
,
w_{1}^{0}
. We refer to
(\mathcal{D}_{00},x_{0}^{00},x_{1}^{00})
as the child of
(\mathcal{D}_{0},x_{0}^{0},x_{1}^{0})
and then continue the exploration in
\mathcal{D}_{00}
from
x_{0}^{00}
to
x_{1}^{00}
. We note in this case that

(6.9)

2\leq L_{00}\leq 6+c_{F}2^{(1-4/\kappa)J}.

We recall that the probability that the exploration fails at given stage is
O(2^{-cJ})
for a constant
c>0
. This proves that the number of times that the exploration is restarted is dominated by the size of a subcritical Galton-Watson tree where each node can have at most two children and the probability of having either one or two children is
O(2^{-cJ})
. Recalling that we have taken
J=\xi_{0}k
, it therefore follows that there exists
n\in\mathbf{N}
(which does not grow with
k
) so that the probability that the exploration is restarted more than
n
times is
O(2^{-(1+\beta)k})
. The proof is completed by taking a union bound over
1\leq j\leq N_{k}
and then over
k
.

Suppose that we have some
m\in\mathbf{N}
and
o\in\{0,1\}^{m}
so that
(\mathcal{D}_{o},x_{0}^{o},x_{1}^{o})
is part of the exploration. Then the maximal chunk size which makes up chunks in the exploration of
\mathcal{D}_{o}
from
x_{0}^{o}
to
x_{1}^{o}
(relative to the ambient quantum disk
\mathcal{D}
) is given by
2^{-\xi_{0}J}\prod_{o^{\prime}}L_{o^{\prime}}^{4/\kappa}
where the product is over
o^{\prime}
which are along the ancestral line of
o
using the tree structure defined above. Combining the upper and lower bounds from (
6.5
)–(
6.9
) we see that there are constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
so that

2^{-c_{0}mJ}\leq 2^{-\xi_{0}J}\prod_{o^{\prime}}L_{o^{\prime}}^{4/\kappa}\leq 2^{-c_{1}mJ}.

Arguing as in (
6.3
), (
6.4
) it therefore follows that the corresponding path
\omega
has a neighborhood of size at least a constant times
2^{-c_{0}n\xi_{2}k/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
around it which is contained in the aforementioned chunks. Since we will always take
m\leq n
where
n
is chosen as above depending on
\beta
, the result follows by possibly increasing the value of
\zeta>0
.
∎

In what follows, for
L>0
we let
\mathsf{QDiskCLE}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=L}
be the law on quantum surfaces decorated by a loop ensemble and marked point
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
which can be sampled from as follows. First suppose that
(D,\widetilde{h},\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=L}
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is an independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
with carpet
\widetilde{\Upsilon}
. Then the law of
(h,\Gamma)
is the one which has Radon-Nikodym derivative with respect to the law of
(\widetilde{h},\widetilde{\Gamma})
given by
\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\mu_{\widetilde{h},\widetilde{\Upsilon}}
where
\mathcal{Z}
is a normalization constant. Finally, conditionally on everything else,
z
has law
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Suppose that
D
is a bounded, simply connected domain and
(D,h,\Gamma,z)\sim\mathsf{QDiskCLE}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Fix
0<\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}<2<\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Let
E
be the event that for every
w\in D
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
with
B(w,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\leq\mu_{h}(B(w,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. Then the law of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,\partial D;\Gamma)
is tight as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

Let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI and which is targeted at
z
and parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk. Let
\tau_{0}=0
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathcal{D}
, and for each
j\geq 1
we inductively define
\tau_{j}
and
\mathcal{D}_{j}
as follows. We let
\tau_{j}
be the first time
t\geq\tau_{j-1}
that
\eta([\tau_{j-1},\tau_{j}])
disconnects
z
from
\partial\mathcal{D}_{j-1}
and we let
\mathcal{D}_{j}
be the quantum surface parameterized by the component of
\mathcal{D}\setminus\eta([0,\tau_{j}])
containing
z
. We also let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
be the quantum surface parameterized by the other component of
\mathcal{D}_{j-1}\setminus\eta([0,\tau_{j}])
with
\eta(\tau_{j})
on its boundary.

Let
L_{j}
(resp.
\widetilde{L}_{j}
) be the quantum length of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{j}
(resp.
\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
). Fix
p\in(0,4/\kappa-1/2)
; note that this interval is non-empty as
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
. We claim that there exists a constant
c>0
so that
\mathbf{E}[L_{j}^{p}]\leq 2^{-cj}
and
\mathbf{E}[\widetilde{L}_{j}^{p}]\leq c^{-1}2^{-cj}
for each
j\in\mathbf{N}
. To see this, for each
t\geq 0
we let
D_{t}
denote the component of
D\setminus\eta([0,t])
which contains
z
and let
X_{t}
be the quantum length of
\partial D_{t}
. By the construction of
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
given in
[
33
]
, for any stopping time
\tau
for the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma(X_{s}:s\leq t)
we have that
\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(\Upsilon\cap D_{\tau})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]=c_{0}X_{\tau}^{4/\kappa-1/2}
for a constant
c_{0}>0
. In particular, if we fix
r>0
and let
\tau_{r}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq r\}
then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\sup_{t\geq 0}X_{t}\geq r]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}[\tau_{r}<\infty]\leq r^{-4/\kappa+1/2}\mathbf{E}[X_{\tau_{r}}^{4/\kappa-1/2}{\bf 1}_{\{\tau_{r}<\infty\}}]

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}^{-1}r^{-4/\kappa+1/2}\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(\Upsilon)]=O(r^{-4/\kappa+1/2}).

Then the above implies that
\mathbf{E}[(\sup_{t\geq 0}X_{t})^{p}]<\infty
and therefore
\mathbf{E}[L_{1}^{p}]<\infty
. Since
X_{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
a.s. we have that
L_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
a.s. Consequently, it follows from the dominated convergence theorem (with dominating function given by
(\sup_{t\geq 0}X_{t})^{p}
) that
\mathbf{E}[L_{j}^{p}]\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. Thus as
\mathbf{E}[L_{j}^{p}]=(\mathbf{E}[L_{1}^{p}])^{j}
, the only way that
\mathbf{E}[L_{j}^{p}]\to 0
as
j\to\infty
can hold is if
\mathbf{E}[L_{1}^{p}]<1
. This proves that there exists a constant
c>0
so that

(6.10)

\mathbf{E}[L_{j}^{p}]\leq 2^{-cj}\quad\text{for all}\quad j\in\mathbf{N}.

We note that the law of
\widetilde{L}_{j}
is stochastically dominated by
L_{j-1}\sup_{t\geq 0}\widetilde{X}_{t}
where
\widetilde{X}
is an independent copy of
X
. It therefore follows that by possibly decreasing the value of
c>0
we have that

(6.11)

\mathbf{E}[\widetilde{L}_{j}^{p}]\leq c^{-1}\mathbf{E}[L_{j-1}^{p}]\leq c^{-1}2^{-cj}\quad\text{for all}\quad j\in\mathbf{N}.

Let
h_{j}
(resp.
\widetilde{h}_{j}
) be the field which describes
\mathcal{D}_{j}^{z}=\mathcal{D}_{j}-z
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{j}^{z}=\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{j}-z
). Let
\Gamma_{j}
be the loops of
\Gamma
which are contained
\mathcal{D}_{j}
and then translated by
-z
. Let
\Upsilon_{j}
be the carpet of
\Gamma_{j}
. Then
h_{j}
together with
\Upsilon_{j}
and marked by
0
describes a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDiskCLE}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=L_{j}}
. Also,
\widetilde{h}_{j}
describes a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\widetilde{L}_{j}}
. Let
R_{j}=L_{j}^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
and
\widetilde{R}_{j}=\widetilde{L}_{j}^{2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. Set

H_{j}(\cdot)=h_{j}(R_{j}\cdot)+Q\log R_{j}-\frac{2}{\gamma}\log L_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{H}_{j}(\cdot)=\widetilde{h}_{j}(\widetilde{R}_{j}\cdot)+Q\log\widetilde{R}_{j}-\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\widetilde{L}_{j}.

Then
H_{j}
together with
R_{j}^{-1}\Upsilon_{j}
and marked by
0
describes a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDiskCLE}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Also,
\widetilde{H}_{j}
describes a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. On
E
, we have that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\leq\mu_{h_{j}}(B(w,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
w\in\mathcal{D}_{j}^{z}
so that
B(w,\epsilon)\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{j}^{z}
. We thus have on
E
that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}/R_{j})
and
w\in R_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{j}^{z}
with
B(w,\epsilon)\subseteq R_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{j}^{z}
that

\mu_{H_{j}}(B(w,\epsilon))=L_{j}^{-2}\mu_{h_{j}}(B(R_{j}w,R_{j}\epsilon))\geq L_{j}^{-2}R_{j}^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}=\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}.

Suppose that
\beta\in(0,2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}})
. It therefore follows from Proposition
5.1
that on
E
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the expected
\beta
-Hölder norm of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;R_{j}^{-1}\Gamma_{j})
on
\partial R_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{j}^{z}
(with respect to quantum length) is finite. Likewise, on
E
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the expected
\beta
-Hölder norm of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\widetilde{R}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\widetilde{R}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\widetilde{R}_{j}^{-1}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{j})
on
\partial\widetilde{R}_{j}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{j}^{z}
(with respect to quantum length) is also finite. Note that

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{m}}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;R_{j}^{-1}\Gamma_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}}{({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\big(R_{j}^{-2}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{j})\big)=R_{j}^{-2}\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}}{{\mathfrak{m}}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}}\big(({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{j})\big).

Lemma
5.4
implies that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}\geq R_{j}{\mathfrak{m}}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}
. Inserting this into the above implies that

(6.12)

({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{j})\leq R_{j}({\mathfrak{m}}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{R_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;R_{j}^{-1}\Gamma_{j}).

We likewise have that

(6.13)

({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\widetilde{\Gamma}_{j})\leq\widetilde{R}_{j}({\mathfrak{m}}_{\widetilde{R}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\widetilde{R}_{j}^{-1}\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\widetilde{R}_{j}^{-1}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{j}).

By the Borel-Cantelli lemma and (
6.10
), (
6.11
), we have for a constant
a>0
that
R_{j}\leq 2^{-aj}
and
\widetilde{R}_{j}\leq 2^{-aj}
for all
j\in\mathbf{N}
large enough. On these events and
E
, we thus have that the expectations of
\sup_{u,v\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{j}^{z}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v;\Gamma_{j})
and
\sup_{u,v\in\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{j}^{z}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v;\Gamma_{j})
are
O(2^{-aj})
. Combining everything and summing over
j
completes the proof of the result.
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain,
(D,h,x,y)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
,
0<\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}<2<\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}
, and
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Let
E
be the event that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in D
with
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\leq\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. For each
\beta>0
there exists
\beta_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
so that for each
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the following is true. Let
F
be the event that for all (prime ends)
a,b\in\partial D
with
\nu_{h}([a,b]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=\epsilon
(resp.
\nu_{h}([a,b]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=\epsilon
) we have that
\mathrm{diam}([a,b]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})\geq\epsilon^{\beta_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
(resp.
\mathrm{diam}([a,b]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})\geq\epsilon^{\beta_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
). Then
\mathbf{P}[F^{c}\cap E]=O(\epsilon^{\beta})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
z\in[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
is chosen from
\nu_{h}
. Let
\varphi\colon\mathbf{D}\to D
be a conformal transformation which takes
-i
to
x
,
i
to
y
, and
1
to
z
. Let
\widetilde{h}=h\circ\varphi+Q\log|\varphi^{\prime}|
. Then
(\mathbf{D},h,-i,i)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Fix
\epsilon_{0}>0
,
\sigma_{\mathrm{LBD}}>1
, and let
\widetilde{E}
be the event that

(i)

for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in\mathbf{D}
so that
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq\mathbf{D}
we have that
\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\sigma_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
and

(ii)

for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and intervals
I\subseteq\partial\mathbf{D}
with
|I|=\epsilon^{\sigma_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
we have that
\nu_{\widetilde{h}}(I)\leq\epsilon
.

Then for each
p>0
there exists
\sigma_{\mathrm{LBD}}>1
so that
\mathbf{P}[\widetilde{E}^{c}]=O(\epsilon_{0}^{p})
.

Suppose that
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
a,b\in\partial\mathbf{D}
are such that
\nu_{\widetilde{h}}([a,b]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})=\epsilon
. Let
w\in\mathbf{D}
have the same argument as the center of
[a,b]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
with
\mathrm{dist}(w,\partial\mathbf{D})=\mathrm{diam}([a,b]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
and let
r=\mathrm{dist}(w,\partial\mathbf{D})/2
. On
\widetilde{E}\cap E
we have that
r\asymp|[a,b]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}|\geq\epsilon^{\sigma_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. Thus if
\mathrm{diam}(\varphi(B(w,r))\leq\epsilon_{0}
we have that

(6.14)

c_{0}^{-1}\epsilon^{\sigma_{\mathrm{LBD}}^{2}}\leq\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(B(w,r))=\mu_{h}(\varphi(B(w,r)))\leq c_{0}(\mathrm{diam}(\varphi(B(w,r))))^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}.

That is, we either have that
\mathrm{diam}(\varphi(B(w,r)))\gtrsim\epsilon^{\sigma_{\mathrm{LBD}}^{2}/\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
or
\mathrm{diam}(\varphi(B(w,r)))\geq\epsilon_{0}
. In either case, the assertion of the lemma thus follows from elementary harmonic measure considerations.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose that
D
is a bounded, simply connected domain and
(D,h,\Gamma,z)\sim\mathsf{QDiskCLE}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Fix
0<\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}<2<\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Let
E
be the event that for every
w\in D
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
so that
B(w,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\leq\mu_{h}(B(w,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. For each
r>0
let

{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z,r)=\{w\in\Upsilon:({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)\leq r\}.

There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}>0
depending only on
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}},\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}
such that uniformly in
\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
,

\mathbf{P}[E,\ \mathrm{diam}({\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z,\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}}))\leq\delta]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\delta\to 0

faster than any power of
\delta
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is similar to that of Lemma
6.7
. Indeed, we let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI and which is targeted at
z
. Let
\tau_{0}=0
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathcal{D}
, and for each
j\geq 1
we inductively define
\tau_{j}
and
\mathcal{D}_{j}
as follows. We let
\tau_{j}
be the first time
t\geq\tau_{j-1}
that
\eta([\tau_{j-1},\tau_{j}])
disconnects
z
from
\partial\mathcal{D}_{j-1}
and we let
\mathcal{D}_{j}
be the quantum surface parameterized by the component of
\mathcal{D}\setminus\eta([0,\tau_{j}])
containing
z
. We let
L_{j}
be the quantum length of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{j}
. For each
k
, we let
N_{k}
be the number of
j
so that
L_{j}\in(2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
. Then there exists
p\in(0,1)
so that
N_{k}
is stochastically dominated by a geometric random variable with parameter
p
. Let
k_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
be such that
k\geq k_{0}
holds if and only if
2^{-k}\leq\delta
. Fix
a_{1}\in(0,1)
. Then a union bound implies that
\mathbf{P}[\exists k\geq k_{0}:N_{k}\geq 2^{a_{1}k}]\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
faster than any power of
\delta
.

For each
n,k
we let
\tau_{n,k}
be the
n
th value of
j\in\mathbf{N}
so that
L_{j}\in(2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
. Then it follows from Proposition
5.1
as in the proof of Lemma
6.7
(recall in particular (
6.12
)) that

\mathbf{P}[E,\ ({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\sup_{u,v\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\tau_{n_{k}}}}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v;\Upsilon)\geq c2^{-(2/\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}})k}]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad c\to\infty

faster than any negative power of
c
. In particular, there exists
a_{2}\in(0,1)
so that

\mathbf{P}[E,\ ({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\sup_{u,v\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\tau_{n_{k}}}}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v;\Upsilon)\geq 2^{-a_{2}k}]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad k\to\infty

faster than any power of
2^{-k}
. Combining this with a union bound, we thus see that the probability of the event that
E
occurs and either
N_{k}\geq 2^{a_{1}k}
or
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\sup_{u,v\in\partial\mathcal{D}_{\tau_{n_{k}}}}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v;\Upsilon)\geq 2^{-a_{2}k}
for some
k\geq k_{0}
tends to
0
as
\delta\to 0
faster than any power of
\delta
. The same also applies to
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\sup_{u,v\in\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\tau_{n_{k}}}}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v;\Upsilon)
.

Let
N
be the largest
k\in\mathbf{N}
so that
L_{k}\geq\delta
. Then it follows from the above that there exists
a_{3}\in(0,1)
so that the probability of
E
and the event that
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z,\delta^{a_{3}})
does not contain
\cup_{j=N+1}^{\infty}\partial\mathcal{D}_{j}
tends to
0
as
\delta\to 0
faster than any power of
\delta
or does not contain an interval of quantum length at least
\delta
on
\partial\mathcal{D}_{N}
. To complete the proof, it suffices to show that there exists
a_{4}>0
so that the diameter of such an interval is very likely to be at least
\delta^{a_{4}}
. This, in turn, follows from Lemma
6.8
and as the Radon-Nikodym derivative between
\mathsf{QDiskCLE}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=L_{k}}
and
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=L_{k}}
has a finite
p
th moment for some
p>1
.
∎

Figure 6.5.
Illustration of the setup to prove Lemma
6.10
. Shown in blue is
\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])
and
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z,\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}\xi})
,
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(w,\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}\xi})
in green.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose that
D
is a bounded, simply connected domain and
(D,h,\Gamma,z)\sim\mathsf{QDiskCLE}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Fix
0<\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}<2<\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}
,
C,\epsilon_{0}>0
, let
\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}
be as in Lemma
6.2
, and
\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}
be as in Proposition
A.1
.

•

Let
E_{1}
be the event that for every
w\in D
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
with
B(w,\epsilon)\subseteq D
we have that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}\leq\mu_{h}(B(w,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
and also
\mu_{h}(D)\leq C
.

•

Assume that
\Gamma
is generated from a GFF
h^{\mathrm{IG}}
on
D
where the associated exploration tree is rooted at
x\in\partial D
picked from
\nu_{h}
, let
\eta^{\prime}
be the associated space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, and let
E_{2}
be the event that the
\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}
-Hölder norm of
\eta^{\prime}
(when parameterized by
\mu_{h}
) is at most
C
.

•

Let
E_{3}
be the event that no loop of
\Gamma
has an
(\epsilon,\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point for
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
.

Let
E=E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}
. There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}\in(0,1)
depending only on
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}
,
\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}
,
\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}
, and
\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}
so that the following is true. Suppose that
w
is sampled conditionally independently of
z
from
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
given
h
,
\Gamma
and let
F=\{|z-w|\leq\delta\}
. Uniformly in
\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
we have that

\mathbf{P}[E,\ F,\ ({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Upsilon)\geq\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}}]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\delta\to 0

faster than any power of
\delta
.

Proof.

See Figure
6.5
for an illustration of the setup for the proof. Suppose that
x\in\partial D
is picked from
\nu_{h}
and let
\eta^{\prime}
be a space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in
D
starting from
x
. We assume that
\Gamma
and
\eta^{\prime}
are coupled together in that they are both generated from the same instance
h^{\mathrm{IG}}
of the GFF on
D
which, in turn, is taken to be conditionally independent of
h
given
x
. We assume that
\eta^{\prime}
is subsequently parameterized by
\mu_{h}
. Suppose that
w\in\Upsilon
is picked from
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
conditionally independently of
z
given everything else. We let
\eta_{z}
be the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
process generated by
h^{\mathrm{IG}}
and targeted at
z
so that its trunk
\eta_{z}^{\prime}
is equal to
\eta^{\prime}
targeted at
z
. In particular, we assume that
\eta
is parameterized according to the amount of quantum area its trunk
\eta_{z}^{\prime}
has disconnected from
z
. Note that the total amount of time that it takes
\eta^{\prime}
to fill
D
is equal to
\mu_{h}(D)
which, on
E_{1}
, is at most
C
. This implies that on
E_{1}
the time interval on which
\eta_{z}^{\prime}
is defined is contained in
[0,C]
. We note that on
E_{2}
we have that
\eta^{\prime}
has an
\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}
-Hölder norm which is at most
C
and therefore the same is true for
\eta_{z}^{\prime}
.

For each
m\in\mathbf{N}
we let
t_{m}=C^{-1}m\delta^{1/(\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})}
. Fix
\beta>(\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})^{-1}
and let
\xi>0
be such that the conclusion of Lemma
6.6
holds for this set of parameters. For each
m\in\mathbf{N}
we let
F_{m}
be the event that the event that the conclusion of Lemma
6.6
holds for the quantum surface
\mathcal{D}_{m}
which is parameterized by the component of
D\setminus\eta([0,t_{m}])
which contains
z
. Lemma
6.6
implies that
\mathbf{P}[E_{1}\cap F_{m}^{c}]=O(\delta^{\beta})
.

Let
\tau=\inf\{t\geq 0:|\eta_{z}^{\prime}(t)-z|\leq\delta^{1/\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}}\}
. Let
m_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
be such that
\tau\in(t_{m_{0}},t_{m_{0}+1}]
. On
F=\{|z-w|\leq\delta\}
we have that
\eta_{[0,\tau]}
hence also
\eta|_{[0,t_{m_{0}}]}
does not disconnect
z
,
w
(for otherwise
\Gamma
would have a loop with a
(\delta^{1/\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}},\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}})
-pinch point). On
F\cap F_{m_{0}}
, there exists a path
\omega_{z}
(resp.
\omega_{w}
) in
\Upsilon\cap\mathcal{D}_{m_{0}}
which satisfies the assertions of Lemma
6.6
for
z
(resp.
w
). In particular, if
U_{z}
(resp.
U_{w}
) denotes the component of
\mathcal{D}_{m_{0}}\setminus\omega_{z}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}_{m_{0}}\setminus\omega_{w}
) which contains
z
(resp.
w
) then

\sup_{u\in\partial U_{z}}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])\cup\omega_{z};\Upsilon)\leq\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{u\in\partial U_{w}}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u,\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])\cup\omega_{w};\Upsilon)\leq\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{on}\quad F\cap F_{m_{0}}.

Let
\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}>0
be as in the statement of Lemma
6.9
. Let
G
be the event that
\mathrm{diam}({\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z,\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}\xi}))\geq\delta^{\xi}
and
\mathrm{diam}({\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(w,\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}\xi}))\geq\delta^{\xi}
. Lemma
6.9
implies
\mathbf{P}[E_{1}\cap G^{c}]\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
faster than any power of
\delta
. On
F\cap F_{m_{0}}\cap G
, we have that
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(z,\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}\xi})\cap\partial U_{z}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathfrak{B}}_{\epsilon}(w,\delta^{\alpha_{\mathrm{B}}\xi})\cap\partial U_{w}\neq\emptyset
. By combining everything we see that

\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}]);\Upsilon)\leq 2\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(w,\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}]);\Upsilon)\leq 2\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{on}\quad F\cap F_{m_{0}}\cap G.

Moreover, let
u_{z}
(resp.
u_{w}
) be a point in
\partial U_{z}
(resp.
\partial U_{w}
) where
\omega_{z}
(resp.
\omega_{w}
) hits
\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])
. On
F\cap F_{m_{0}}
, we know that
\mathrm{diam}(U_{z})\leq\delta^{\xi}
and
\mathrm{diam}(U_{w})\leq\delta^{\xi}
. Lemma
6.8
implies that by possibly decreasing the value of
\xi>0
we have that the quantum length of
\partial U_{z}\cap\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])
,
\partial U_{w}\cap\eta([0,t_{m_{0}}])
, and the shorter arc of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{m_{0}}
from
u_{z}
to
u_{w}
are all at most
\delta^{\xi}
. For each
m\in\mathbf{N}
, we let
H_{m}
be the event that the event of Proposition
5.1
holds for
\mathcal{D}_{m}
. Using the aforementioned bounds, by possibly decreasing
\xi>0
if necessary, we then have that

\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,u_{z};\Upsilon)\leq 2\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(w,u_{w};\Upsilon)\leq 2\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{on}\quad F\cap F_{m_{0}}\cap G\cap H_{m_{0}}

and likewise

\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(u_{z},u_{w};\Upsilon)\leq\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{on}\quad F\cap F_{m_{0}}\cap G\cap H_{m_{0}}.

Altogether, this implies that

\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Upsilon)\leq 5\delta^{\xi}\quad\text{on}\quad F\cap F_{m_{0}}\cap G\cap H_{m_{0}}.

Let
M_{0}=C^{2}\delta^{-(\alpha_{\mathrm{PP}}\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}})^{-1}}
. On
E
, we have that
[0,t_{M_{0}}]
contains the interval on which
\eta_{z}
is defined. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left[E\cap F\cap\bigcup_{m=1}^{M_{0}}F_{m}^{c}\right]\leq\sum_{m=1}^{M_{0}}\mathbf{P}[E\cap F\cap F_{m}^{c}]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\delta\to 0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left[E\cap F\cap\bigcup_{m=1}^{M_{0}}H_{m}^{c}\right]\leq\sum_{m=1}^{M_{0}}\mathbf{P}[E\cap F\cap H_{m}^{c}]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\delta\to 0,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[E\cap F\cap G^{c}]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\delta\to 0

faster than any power of
\delta
. Combining everything completes the proof.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Let
(z_{n})
be an i.i.d. sequence chosen from
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
. We let
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}>0
be as in the statement of Lemma
B.1
. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we let
N_{j}=2^{\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}j}
. Then Lemma
B.1
implies that there a.s. exists
j_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
so that
j\geq j_{0}
implies that
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N_{j}}
is a
2^{-j}
-net of
\Upsilon
. That is,
\Upsilon\subseteq\cup_{k=1}^{N_{j}}B(z_{k},2^{-j})
. Let
N_{\epsilon}=\lfloor\log_{2}\epsilon^{-2}\rfloor
. Lemma
6.10
and the Borel-Cantelli lemma imply, by possibly increasing the value of
j_{0}
, we have
j_{0}\leq j\leq N_{\epsilon}
implies that if
|z_{\ell}-z_{k}|\leq 2^{-j}
for
1\leq\ell,k\leq N_{j}
then

(6.15)

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{\ell},z_{k};\Gamma)\leq 2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}j}.

Suppose that
z,w\in\Upsilon
and fix
J\in\mathbf{N}
so that
2^{-J-1}\leq|z-w|<2^{-J}
. We assume that
j_{0}\leq J\leq N_{\epsilon}
. Then we can find sequences
m_{j},n_{j}
with
1\leq m_{j},n_{j}\leq N_{j}
for every
j\leq J
so that
z\in B(z_{m_{j}},2^{-j})
and
w\in B(z_{n_{j}},2^{-j})
. It therefore follows from (
6.15
) that

(6.16)

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,z_{m_{J}};\Gamma)\leq\sum_{j={J}}^{N_{\epsilon}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{m_{j+1}},z_{m_{j}};\Gamma)\leq 2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}J}.

We similarly have that

(6.17)

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(w,z_{n_{J}};\Gamma)\leq\sum_{j={J}}^{N_{\epsilon}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{n_{j+1}},z_{n_{j}};\Gamma)\leq 2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}J}.

Note also that (
6.15
) implies

(6.18)

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z_{m_{J}},z_{n_{J}};\Gamma)\leq 2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}J}.

Combining (
6.16
), (
6.17
), and (
6.18
) implies that

({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)\leq 2^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{KC}}J}.

This proves what we want in the case that
J\geq j_{0}
. The result follows for
J<j_{0}
by considering a finite chain of points
x_{0}=z,\ldots,x_{m}=w
in
\Upsilon
with
|x_{i}-x_{i+1}|\leq 2^{-j_{0}}
and applying the triangle inequality.
∎

7.
Positive definiteness and geodesics

The purpose of this section is to prove the positive definiteness of a subsequential limit
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
constructed in Section
6
. We will first show in Section
7.1
that for every sequence
(\epsilon_{j})
of positive real numbers decreasing to
0
one can find a single subsequence
(\epsilon_{j_{k}})
which can be used to define a subsequentially limiting pseudometric for every non-trivial simply connected domain simultaneously, provided one restricts to paths which stay away from the domain boundary. In Section
7.2
, we will develop a notion of a “local set” for a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
instance
\Gamma
equipped with a subsequential limit
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
. We will then prove the positive definiteness of the subsequential limit in Section
7.3
(see also the beginning of Section
7.3
for an outline of the steps used to prove this result). Finally, we will deduce the geodesic property of a subsequential limit in Section
7.4
and prove the comparability of quantiles defined in the finite volume and half-planar setting in Section
7.5
.

7.1.
Simultaneous limits

Throughout, we will need to restrict our attention to a countable family of open sets such that for every open set
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
there exists
D_{1}\subseteq D_{2}\subseteq\cdots
in the family so that
D=\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}D_{n}
. There are various choices that one could make and the particular one is not important for what follows, but for the sake of concreteness we will consider the set
\mathsf{DyadDom}
of domains
U\subseteq\mathbf{C}
for which there exists
k\in\mathbf{N}
and closed squares
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
with side length
2^{-k}
and corners in
(2^{-k}\mathbf{Z})^{2}
so that
U=\mathrm{int}(\cup_{j=1}^{n}S_{j})
. For a domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
, we let
\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
be the set of
U\in\mathsf{DyadDom}
with
\mathrm{cl}(U)\subseteq D
. Finally, we let
\mathsf{DyadDom}_{2}(D)
be the collection of pairs
(U_{1},U_{2})
with
U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
and
\mathrm{cl}(U_{1})\subseteq U_{2}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For every sequence
(\epsilon_{k})
of positive numbers decreasing to
0
there exists a subsequence
(\epsilon_{j_{k}})
so that the following is true. Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain and
(U_{1},U_{2})\in\mathsf{DyadDom}_{2}(D)
. For
z,w\in U_{2}
, let
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}^{U_{2}}(z,w;\Gamma)=\inf_{\omega}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon}(\omega)
where the infimum is over paths
\omega\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon\cap U_{2}
with
\omega(0)=z
,
\omega(1)=w
. Let
E
be the event that there exists a path in
(U_{2}\setminus U_{1})\cap\Upsilon
with positive distance from
\partial U_{1}\cup\partial U_{2}
and which disconnects
\partial U_{1}
from
\partial U_{2}
. Then
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)|_{U_{1}^{2}}{\bf 1}_{E}
converges as
k\to\infty
weakly with respect to
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
.

We emphasize that in the statement of Lemma
7.1
, the subsequence
(\epsilon_{j_{k}})
of
(\epsilon_{k})
does not depend on the choice of
D
and
(U_{1},U_{2})\in\mathsf{DyadDom}_{2}(D)
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain and
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
. Let
(D,h)
have law
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
and let
w\in D
,
x\in\partial D
, respectively, be independently picked from
\mu_{h},\nu_{h}
. Let
(U_{1},U_{2})\in\mathsf{DyadDom}_{2}(D)
and let
E
be as in the statement of the lemma. Let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
in
D
which is coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI starting from
x
and targeted at
w
. Let
\tau
be the first time that
w
is disconnected from
\partial D
and let
U_{w}
be the component of
D\setminus\eta([0,\tau])
which contains
w
. Let
\ell
be the quantum length of
\partial U_{w}
. Given
\ell
, the law of the quantum surface described by
(U_{w},h)
is
\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
. Since the conditional law given
\eta|_{[0,\tau]}
of the loops
\Gamma_{w}
of
\Gamma
contained in
U_{w}
is that of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
U_{w}
(on the event that
U_{w}
is not a loop of
\Gamma
), it follows from Proposition
6.1
that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{w})
is tight with respect to
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
.

Let
(w_{j})
be a sequence chosen from
\mu_{h}
and, for each
j
, we let
\eta_{j}
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
in
D
coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI starting from
x
and targeted at
w_{j}
. We assume that the
w_{j},\eta_{j}
are taken to be conditionally independent given everything else. Let
U_{w_{j}}
and
\Gamma_{w_{j}}
be as above with
w_{j},\eta_{j}
in place of
w,\eta
. By Proposition
3.3
, the conditional probability given
\Upsilon
that a CPI is close to any fixed simple path in
\Upsilon
is positive, hence it follows that on
E
if we let
J
be the smallest
j
so that
U_{1}\subseteq U_{w_{j}}\subseteq U_{2}
then
J<\infty
a.s. We note on
E
that
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)|_{U_{1}^{2}}\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{w_{J}})
. From this, it follows that
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)|_{U_{1}^{2}}{\bf 1}_{E}
is tight with respect to
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
.

What we have explained above implies that for each simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
,
(U_{1},U_{2})\in\mathsf{DyadDom}_{2}(D)
, and every sequence
(\epsilon_{k})
of positive numbers decreasing to
0
there exists a subsequence
(\epsilon_{j_{k}})
so that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)|_{U_{1}^{2}}{\bf 1}_{E}
converges weakly with respect to
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
. Indeed, this follows since the collection of such
(U_{1},U_{2})
,
D
is countable. Our aim now is to upgrade this statement to show that it is possible to make a choice of subsequence of
(\epsilon_{k})
which does not depend on
D
,
U_{1}
, or
U_{2}
.

Let
(\epsilon_{k})
be any sequence of positive numbers which decrease to
0
. First of all, by passing to a diagonal subsequence, we can apply the above argument to get a subsequence
(\epsilon_{j_{k}})
of
(\epsilon_{k})
so that the following is true. For every simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
which is in
\mathsf{DyadDom}
and
(U_{1},U_{2})\in\mathsf{DyadDom}_{2}(D)
we have that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)|_{U_{1}^{2}}{\bf 1}_{E}
converges weakly as
k\to\infty
with respect to
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
.

We now suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain and
(U_{1},U_{2})\in\mathsf{DyadDom}_{2}(D)
. To finish the proof, we will show that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)|_{U_{1}^{2}}{\bf 1}_{E}
converges weakly as
k\to\infty
with respect to
(\mathbf{K}_{4},{\mathbf{d}}_{4})
.

Let
(D_{m})
be a sequence of simply connected domains in
\mathsf{DyadDom}
so that
\cup_{m}D_{m}=D
. We assume that
\mathrm{cl}(U_{2})\subseteq D_{1}
. For each
m
, we let
\Gamma_{m}
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D_{m}
. In order to prove the result, it suffices to show that the total variation distance between the law of the loops
\Gamma^{U_{2}}
of
\Gamma
which intersect
U_{2}
and that of the loops
\Gamma_{m}^{U_{2}}
of
\Gamma_{m}
which intersect
U_{2}
tends to
0
as
m\to\infty
. We assume that
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{m}
are coupled together so that they all come from the same instance of the Brownian loop-soup on
\mathbf{C}
. Then we note that each loop
\mathcal{L}_{m}\in\Gamma_{m}^{U_{2}}
is contained in a loop
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma^{U_{2}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{m}=\mathcal{L}
if
\mathcal{L}
does not intersect
D\setminus D_{m}
. It therefore suffices to show that there a.s. exists
m_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
so that
m\geq m_{0}
implies that none of the loops of
\Gamma^{U_{2}}
intersect
D\setminus D_{m}
. Let
\varphi
be a conformal transformation from
D
to
\mathbf{D}
. If the above did not hold, then
\varphi(\Gamma^{U_{2}})
(the set of loops of the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
given by
\varphi(\Gamma)
which intersect
\varphi(U_{2})
) would with positive probability contain a loop which intersects
\partial\mathbf{D}
. This, in turn, cannot happen as
\varphi(U_{2})\cap\partial\mathbf{D}=\emptyset
.
∎

Suppose that we have the setup as in Lemma
7.1
. By taking as limit as
k\to\infty
, we have that the family
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is defined simultaneously for all
U_{2}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
. We note that
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is decreasing as
U_{2}
increases. Suppose that
(U_{n})
is an increasing sequence of domains in
D
, each of which is in
\mathsf{DyadDom}
, so that
D=\cup_{n}U_{n}
. We let
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
be the limit as
n\to\infty
of
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{n}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
. We note that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
does not depend on
(U_{n})
. Indeed, suppose that
(\widetilde{U}_{n})
is another sequence of such domains. Then for every
n
there exists
m
so that
U_{n}\subseteq\widetilde{U}_{m}
hence
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{n}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)\geq\mathfrak{d}^{\widetilde{U}_{m}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
and likewise for every
n
there exists
m
so that
\widetilde{U}_{n}\subseteq U_{m}
hence
\mathfrak{d}^{\widetilde{U}_{n}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)\geq\mathfrak{d}^{U_{m}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
. Moreover, we note that each for each
U_{1}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
fixed there a.s. exists
n_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
so that
n\geq n_{0}
implies that the event
E
from Lemma
7.1
holds for
(U_{1},U_{2,n})
as the loops of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
with
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
a.s. do not hit the domain boundary.

For a simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
and a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
process
\Gamma
on
D
equipped with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
, we let
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma)
be the closure of the set of pairs of points
z,w\in D
such that there exists
U_{2}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
so that
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2}}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
. We also set
\mathrm{Zero}(\Gamma)
be the closure of the set of pairs of points in
z,w\in D
such that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose that
D
,
\widetilde{D}
are simply connected domains in
\mathbf{C}
and
\varphi\colon D\to\widetilde{D}
is a conformal map. Suppose that
\Gamma
(resp.
\widetilde{\Gamma}
) is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
. Then
\varphi(\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
. The same is true with
\mathrm{Zero}(\Gamma)
,
\mathrm{Zero}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
in place of
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma)
,
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
in the case that
\sup_{z\in D}|\varphi^{\prime}(z)|<\infty
.

We emphasize that in the statement of Lemma
7.2
when we consider
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma)
,
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
we have restricted to points that can be connected by a “zero length path” which stays away from the domain boundary. The particular application that we have in mind for the case
\sup_{z\in D}|\varphi^{\prime}(z)|<\infty
is when
\varphi
is a translation or scaling map.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

Let us first handle the case of
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma)
,
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
. We assume that
\widetilde{\Gamma}=\varphi(\Gamma)
. Using the Skorokhod representation theorem for weak convergence and for each
k
writing
\Gamma_{k}
(resp.
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}
) for a copy of
\Gamma
(resp.
\widetilde{\Gamma}
) with
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}=\varphi(\Gamma_{k})
, we can couple everything together onto a common probability space so that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{U_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{k})
and
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\widetilde{U}_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot;\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k})
a.s. converge as
k\to\infty
for each
U_{2}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
,
\widetilde{U}_{2}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(\widetilde{D})
where the subsequence is as in Lemma
7.1
. We may further assume that the
\Gamma_{k}
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}
converge and call their limit
\Gamma
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}
with carpets
\Upsilon
,
\widetilde{\Upsilon}
, respectively.

Suppose that
U_{2}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
and
z,w\in\Upsilon
satisfy
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2}}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
. Fix
\xi>0
. Then there a.s. exists
k_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
so that for every
k\geq k_{0}
there is a path
\omega_{k}\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon\cap U_{2}
with
\omega_{k}(0)=z
and
\omega_{k}(1)=w
so that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(\omega_{k})\leq\xi
. Now suppose that
\widetilde{U}_{2}\in\mathsf{DyadDom}(\widetilde{D})
is such that
\varphi(U_{2})\subseteq\widetilde{U}_{2}
. As
|\varphi^{\prime}|
is bounded on
U_{2}
it follows that there exists
C>0
depending only on
U_{2}
and
\varphi
so that the
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}}}(\varphi(\omega_{k}))\leq C\xi
. As
\varphi(\eta_{k})
connects
\varphi(z)
to
\varphi(w)
and
\xi>0
was arbitrary, it follows that
\mathfrak{d}^{\widetilde{U}_{2}}(\varphi(z),\varphi(w);\widetilde{\Gamma})=0
. This implies that
\varphi(\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma))\subseteq\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
. The same argument applies with
\varphi^{-1}
in place of
\varphi
to see that
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\widetilde{\Gamma})\subseteq\varphi(\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma))
, which proves the result.

In the case that
\sup_{z\in D}|\varphi^{\prime}(z)|<\infty
, the same argument applies with
\mathrm{Zero}(\Gamma)
,
\mathrm{Zero}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
in place of
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma)
,
\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
.
∎

7.2.
Locality

We will assume that we have taken a subsequence as in Lemma
7.1
so that we have
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\cdot)
as described just before Lemma
7.2
is defined for every non-trivial simply connected domain simultaneously. Although we not yet shown that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\cdot)
is a metric as we have not finished proving that it is positive definite, we can still define the
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\cdot)
-length of a path in the same way that the length is defined for a metric. For
U\subseteq D
open, we can also define the interior-internal pseudometric on
\mathrm{cl}(U)
to be the infimum of the
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\cdot)
-lengths of paths which are contained in
U
except possibly at their endpoints. Throughout, if
K\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(D)
is closed we let
K^{*}
be the closure of the union of
K
and the points surrounded by loops of
\Gamma
which intersect
K
. If
U\subseteq D
is open, we let
U^{*}=D\setminus(D\setminus U)^{*}
.

Definition 7.3
.

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain and
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
. Let
K\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(D)
be a random closed set which is coupled with
\Gamma
so that
K\cup\partial D
is a.s. connected. We say that
K
is
local
for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
if the following is true. For each
\epsilon>0
, we let
\mathcal{F}_{K}^{\epsilon}
be the
\sigma
-algebra which is generated by
K
, the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect
K
, and the interior-internal pseudometric associated with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
in the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
K
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{K}=\cap_{\epsilon>0}\mathcal{F}_{K}^{\epsilon}
. Then the conditional law of
\Gamma
and the interior-internal pseudometric associated with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
in each component
U
of
D\setminus K^{*}
given
\mathcal{F}_{K}
is independently that of
(\Gamma_{U},\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{U}))
where
\Gamma_{U}
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
U
.

We now give a useful criterion for checking locality.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain and let
\Gamma
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
. Let
K\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(D)
be a random closed set which is coupled with
\Gamma
so that
K\cup\partial D
is a.s. connected. Then
K
is local for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
if and only if the following is true. Suppose that
U\subseteq D
is open. For each
\epsilon>0
, let
(D\setminus U)^{\epsilon}
be the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
D\setminus U
and let
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
be the loops of
\Gamma
contained in
(D\setminus U)^{\epsilon}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{U}^{\epsilon}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect
D\setminus U
and
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{\epsilon})
and let
\mathcal{F}_{U}=\cap_{\epsilon>0}\mathcal{F}_{U}^{\epsilon}
. Given
\mathcal{F}_{U}
, the event that
K^{*}\cap U=\emptyset
is independent of the loops
\Gamma_{U}
of
\Gamma
contained in
U^{*}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{U})
.

Proof.

If
K
is local for
\Gamma
, then it is obvious that the property in the lemma statement holds.

Suppose instead that
K
satisfies the property in the lemma statement. For each
n\in\mathbf{N}
, let
K_{n}
be the closure of the union of
K
and the dyadic squares with side length
2^{-n}
and corners in
(2^{-n}\mathbf{Z})^{2}
which intersect
K
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
K_{n}
, the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect
K_{n}
, and the
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
-length of every path which is contained in
K_{n}
. The assumption from the lemma statement implies that the conditional law of
\Gamma
and the interior-internal pseudometric in each component
U
of
D\setminus K_{n}^{*}
is given by a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
U
coupled with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{U})
. The backward martingale convergence theorem implies that the conditional law given
\cap_{n}\mathcal{F}_{n}
of the loops of
\Gamma
and the interior-internal pseudometric in each component
U
of
D\setminus K^{*}
is given by a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}

\Gamma_{U}
in
U
and
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{U})
. This implies the result as
\mathcal{F}_{K}\subseteq\cap_{n}\mathcal{F}_{n}
.
∎

7.3.
Statement and proof of positive definiteness

Proposition 7.5
.

Suppose that
\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{D}
and let
D
be the set of points surrounded by the loop
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma_{D}
which surrounds
0
. Given
\mathcal{L}
, let
\Gamma
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
. Almost surely, for all
z,w\in\Upsilon
distinct we have that
\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
. That is,
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is a.s. a metric on
\Upsilon
.

As we will explain below, using a CPI exploration and Proposition
3.3
we will see that in order to prove Proposition
7.5
it suffices to establish the following proposition.

Proposition 7.6
.

Suppose that
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
in
\mathbf{D}
from
-i
to
i
coupled with a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
process
\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
on
\mathbf{D}
as a CPI. On the event that
\eta
disconnects
0
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
and the component
D
of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\eta
is not a loop of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
and is surrounded counterclockwise by
\eta
, we let
\Gamma
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
. Otherwise, we let
D=\emptyset
. On
D\neq\emptyset
, we almost surely have for all
z,w\in\partial D
distinct that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
. That is,
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)|_{(\partial D)^{2}}
is a.s. a metric on
\partial D
.

Let us now describe the main steps to prove Proposition
7.6
.

Step 1.

We first collect some preliminary lemmas in Section
7.3.1
. In particular, in Lemma
7.7
we will work in the following half-planar setup. We let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)\sim\mathsf{QCone}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=4}
which is decorated by independent
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curves
\eta_{\pm}
so that the surface
\mathcal{H}_{+}
parameterized by the component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus(\eta_{-}\cup\eta_{+})
which is to the left of
\eta_{+}
is a quantum half-plane. We assume that
\eta_{\pm}
are parameterized according to quantum length. We let
\Gamma_{+}
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathcal{H}_{+}
,
\Upsilon_{+}
its carpet, and we let
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{+})
be as above. Then we will show that for any
u<v<r<s
fixed we have
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([u,v]),\eta_{+}([r,s]);\Gamma_{+})>0
with positive probability and
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([u,v]),\eta_{+}([r,s]);\Gamma_{+})>0
has probability tending to
1
as
s\downarrow r
. We next show in Lemma
7.8
that if
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on a simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
,
x,y\in\partial D
are distinct, then the closure
K
of the set of points
z
in the carpet
\Upsilon
of
\Gamma
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};\Gamma)=0
is local and in Lemma
7.9
that the non-loop points on
\partial K
have zero harmonic measure in any component of
D\setminus K^{*}
. We also show in Lemma
7.10
that if
D
is as in Proposition
7.6
and
(D,h)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
then the quantum length of the set of
z\in\partial D
for which there exists
w\in\mathrm{cl}(D)\setminus\{z\}
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma_{+})=0
is a.s. zero, the analogous statement holds for each loop
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
, and the same statements hold with harmonic measure in place of quantum length.

Step 2.

We next define and establish some properties of the “shields” in Section
7.3.2
. We suppose we are working on a Jordan domain
D
as in the statement of Proposition
7.6
and we have a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
process
\Gamma
in
D
and let
\Upsilon
be its carpet. Roughly speaking, a shield is a closed, connected set
A
so that the following is true. If
O\subseteq D
is open,
a_{1},a_{2}\in O\cap\Upsilon
, and there exists
\delta>0
so that
a_{1},a_{2}
are in different components of
O^{\delta}\setminus A
where
O^{\delta}
is the
\delta
-neighborhood of
O
, then
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})>0
where
\Gamma_{O}
are the loops of
\Gamma
contained in
O^{*}
. See Figure
7.2
for an illustration. This condition should be thought of as giving that a “zero-length path” cannot pass through
A
. Such shields exist because
\partial K
from above can be used to build a set which has this property.

Step 3.

We fix a countable dense set
(r_{k})
of
D
and, for each
k
, let
\mathcal{L}_{k}
be the loop which surrounds
r_{k}
(we note that there a.s. exists such a loop for all
k
simultaneously). For
i,j\in\mathbf{N}
we say that
i\sim_{0}j
if
\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
\mathcal{L}_{j}
can be connected by a shield and let
\sim
be the finest equivalence relation which contains
\sim_{0}
. We also let
A_{k}
be the closure of the union of the points surrounded by the
\mathcal{L}_{i}
so that
i\sim k
. We suppose that
z_{1},\ldots,z_{4}\in\partial D
are distinct points given in counterclockwise order. We will show in Lemma
7.15
that for each
\delta>0
there can be at most finitely many such sets
A_{k}
which are not contained in the
\delta
-neighborhood of
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. In Section
7.3.3
, we aim to use the sets
A_{k}
to disconnect
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. If the closure
K
of the union of the
A_{k}
which intersect
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
also intersects
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
then by the aforementioned fact there must exist an
A_{k}
which intersects both
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. If there is no such
A_{k}
, then as
K
is local we can keep repeating the same experiment in the component of
D\setminus K
with
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
on its boundary. As such a shield has a positive chance of connecting the two such arcs, this process will eventually discover a finite number of such sets
K
which disconnect
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Since the part of
\partial K
with distance at least
\delta>0
from
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
is contained in a finite union of the
A_{k}
’s (and likewise when we iterate the exploration), we can find a finite collection of
A_{k}
’s which disconnect
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
from
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
.

Step 4.

We complete the proof by arguing that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},x;\Gamma)>0
where
x
is an intersection point between two adjacent shields. This will complete the proof as a “zero length path” cannot pass through any given shield, so can only connect
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
by passing through such an intersection point. We will prove that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},x;\Gamma)>0
by arguing that such an intersection point is disconnected from
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
by a further shield connecting two “typical points” on two
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loops (one from the previous shield and one from the next shield) and a typical loop point
w
has the property that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
for all
z\in\Upsilon\setminus\{w\}
using the results described in Step 1.

7.3.1.
Preliminary lemmas

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)\sim\mathsf{QCone}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\mathbf{W}=4}
which is decorated by independent
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curves
\eta_{\pm}
so that the surface
\mathcal{H}_{+}
parameterized by the component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus(\eta_{-}\cup\eta_{+})
which is to the left of
\eta_{+}
is a quantum half-plane. We assume that
\eta_{\pm}
are parameterized according to quantum length. We let
\Gamma_{+}
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathcal{H}_{+}
,
\Upsilon_{+}
its carpet, and we let
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{+})
be as above. For any
u<v<r<s
we have that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([u,v]),\eta_{+}([r,s]);\Gamma_{+})>0]>0
. Moreover, for
u<v<r
fixed,
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([u,v]),\eta_{+}([r,s]);\Gamma_{+})>0]\to 1
as
s\downarrow r
.

Proof.

We will first prove the first part of the statement. For contradiction, let us suppose that there exist
u<v<r<s
so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([u,v]),\eta_{+}([r,s]);\Gamma_{+})=0
a.s. By applying Lemma
7.2
with a translation and scaling map, we may assume that
u=0
and
v=1
. We assume that
\mathcal{C}
is embedded into
\mathbf{C}
so that
|\eta_{+}(1)|=1
. Let
\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}
be
\eta_{+}
parameterized by capacity and let
\tau
be the first time
t
that
\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}([0,t])
has quantum length equal to
1
so that
\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}([0,\tau])=\eta_{+}([0,1])
. Then for any
0<a<b
fixed, there is a positive chance that the quantum length of
\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}([0,\tau+a])
is at most
r
and the quantum length of
\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}([0,\tau+b])
is at least
s
, that is,
\eta_{+}([r,s])\subseteq\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}([\tau+a,\tau+b])
. This holds, moreover, even if we condition on
\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}
. It therefore follows that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([0,1]),\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}([\tau+a,\tau+b]);\Gamma_{+})=0
a.s. Since
0<a<b
were arbitrary, it follows that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([0,1]),\eta_{+}^{\mathrm{cap}}([\tau+a,\tau+b]);\Gamma_{+})=0
a.s. for all
0<a<b
simultaneously. Therefore
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([0,1]),\eta_{+}([r,s]);\Gamma_{+})=0
a.s. for all
1<r<s
. By the continuity of
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{+})
, it follows that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([0,1]),\eta_{+}(r);\Gamma_{+})=0
a.s. for all
r>1
. By applying scaling again (Lemma
7.2
), we see that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}(0),\eta_{+}(r);\Gamma_{+})=0
for all
r>0
. Finally, applying a translation and scaling map (Lemma
7.2
) and the continuity of
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{+})
, we have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}(r),\eta_{+}(s);\Gamma_{+})=0
a.s. for all
r,s>0
. This is a contradiction to the comparability of the quantiles
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
, hence proves the first statement.

We now prove the second statement. Suppose that there exist
0<u<v<r
such that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([u,v]),\eta_{+}([r,s]);\Gamma_{+})>0]
does not tend to
1
as
s\downarrow r
. Then
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}([u,v]),\eta_{+}(r);\Gamma_{+})=0]>0
. By Lemma
7.2
and translation and scaling, this implies that there exists
p_{0}>0
so that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}(0),\eta_{+}([R,2R]);\Gamma_{+})=0]=p_{0}
for all
R>0
. Suppose that we have
0<r_{1}<r_{2}
. Let
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
be
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
processes coupled as conditionally independent CPIs in
\Upsilon_{+}
respectively starting from
\eta_{+}(r_{1})
,
\eta_{+}(r_{2})
. Let
\tau_{i}
be the first time that
\eta_{i}
disconnects
0
from
\infty
. Let also
T_{i}
be the part of the boundary of the unbounded component of
\mathcal{H}_{+}\setminus\eta_{i}([0,\tau_{i}])
which is contained in
\eta_{i}([0,\tau_{i}])
. Let
B_{i}
be the part of the boundary of the component of
\mathcal{H}_{+}\setminus\eta_{i}([0,\tau_{i}])
with
\eta_{+}(0)
on its boundary which is contained in
\eta_{i}([0,\tau_{i}])
.

We note that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(T_{1},B_{2};\Gamma_{+})>0]>0
for all
r_{2}>r_{1}
sufficiently large. Indeed, if not, then
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(T_{1},B_{2};\Gamma_{+})>0]=0
for all
r_{2}>r_{1}
. By applying scaling (Lemma
7.2
), this implies that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}(0),B_{1};\Gamma_{+})=0]=1
for all
r_{1}>0
. This is in fact true by translation for all
\eta_{+}(r)
for
r
in some interval. With positive probability we also have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{-}([a,b]),\eta_{+}([a,b]);\Gamma_{+})=0
. We then obtain that with positive probability we have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}(r),\eta_{+}(0);\Gamma_{+})=0
for all
r
in some interval. By the scale invariance of the law of
\mathrm{Zero}(\Gamma_{+})
(Lemma
7.2
), we get that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}
vanishes with positive probability, which we know it cannot by the comparability of the quantiles
{{\mathfrak{q}}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}(p)
. This proves our assertion.

We now define sequences of numbers as follows. Suppose we take
r_{1}=1
and
r_{2}
as above so that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(T_{1},B_{2};\Gamma_{+})>0]>0
. We then let
r_{j}=r_{2}^{j-1}
for all
j\geq 2
. By scaling, there exists
p_{1}>0
so that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(T_{j},B_{j+1};\Gamma_{+})>0]=p_{1}
for all
j
. For each
j
let
U_{j}
be the domain bounded by
T_{j}
,
B_{j+1}
,
\eta_{-}
, and
\eta_{+}
. For each
k
, we let
N_{k}
be the number of
1\leq j\leq k
so that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(T_{j},B_{j+1};\Gamma_{+})>0\,|\,U_{j}]\geq p_{1}/2
. By Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem, we have that
N_{k}/k
a.s. tends to a positive limit as
k\to\infty
. It therefore follows that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(T_{1},B_{k};\Gamma_{+})>0]\to 1
as
k\to\infty
. This contradicts the statement that there exists
p_{0}>0
so that
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\eta_{+}(0),\eta_{+}([R,2R]);\Gamma_{+})=0]=p_{0}
for all
R>0
, which completes the proof of the second assertion.
∎

Suppose that
D\subseteq C
is a simply connected domain and
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
. We let
Z_{x,y}
be the closure of the union of
\{z\in\Upsilon:\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};\Gamma)=0\}
and the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect it. Let also
Z_{x,y}^{+}
be the closure of the union of
\{z\in\Upsilon:\exists(w,z)\in\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma),\ w\in[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\}
and the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect it. If
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is continuous up to
\partial D
, then we note that
Z_{x,y}^{+}\subseteq Z_{x,y}
since if
z\in\Upsilon
and there exists
w\in[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
so that
(w,z)\in\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma)
then
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
hence
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};\Gamma)=0
. We consider both cases since
Z_{x,y}^{+}
is useful due to its conformal invariance properties (Lemma
7.2
) while
Z_{x,y}
is useful in settings in which we will not apply a conformal map.

Lemma 7.8
.

Suppose that
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain,
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D
, and
x,y\in\partial D
are distinct. Then both
Z_{x,y}
and
Z_{x,y}^{+}
are local for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
.

Proof.

Suppose that
U\subseteq D
is open. Using the notation of the statement of Lemma
7.4
, whether
Z_{x,y}\cap U=\emptyset
is determined by
\mathcal{F}_{U}^{\epsilon}
for each
\epsilon>0
and hence by
\mathcal{F}_{U}
. Consequently, the assertion of the lemma for
Z_{x,y}
follows from Lemma
7.4
. The same argument applies for
Z_{x,y}^{+}
.
∎

Lemma 7.9
.

Suppose that we have the setup described in Lemma
7.8
. The following is a.s. true. Suppose that
z
is in the interior of a component
U
of
D\setminus Z_{x,y}
. Then the harmonic measure of the set of points in
\partial U\setminus\partial D
as seen from
z
which are not contained in a loop of
\Gamma
is zero. The same is true with
Z_{x,y}^{+}
in place of
Z_{x,y}
.

Proof.

We will prove the result in the case of
Z_{x,y}
. The argument in the case of
Z_{x,y}^{+}
is analogous. In order for the assertion of the lemma to be non-trivial, we need to assume

(7.1)

\mathbf{P}[Z_{x,y}\setminus\partial D\neq\emptyset]>0.

We note that (
7.1
) implies that with positive probability there exists
w\in\Upsilon
so that if
(U_{2,n})
is an increasing sequence in
\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
with
D=\cup_{n}U_{2,n}
then there exists
n_{0}
so that for all
n\geq n_{0}
we have that
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2,n}}(w,\partial U_{2,n};\Gamma)=0
. By Lemma
7.2
, we note that this statement must hold for every simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
.

Suppose that (
7.1
) holds and the assertion of the lemma does not hold. Then there exists a point
z\in D
with rational coordinates so that with positive probability
z\notin Z_{x,y}
and the harmonic measure of the points in
\partial U\setminus\partial D
,
U
the component of
D\setminus Z_{x,y}
which contains
z
, which are not contained in a loop of
\Gamma
is positive. By Lemma
7.8
, we know that the conditional law of the loops of
\Gamma
contained in
U
is that of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
. That is, we can write these loops as
\varphi(\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}})
where
\varphi\colon\mathbf{D}\to U
is the unique conformal transformation with
\varphi(0)=z
and
\varphi^{\prime}(0)>0
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{D}
with carpet
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{D}}
. By (
7.1
), it is a positive probability event for
\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
that there exists
u\in\Upsilon_{\mathbf{D}}
so that if
(U_{2,n})
is an increasing sequence in
\mathsf{DyadDom}(\mathbf{D})
with
\mathbf{D}=\cup_{n}U_{2,n}
then
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2,n}}(u,\partial U_{2,n};\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}})=0
for all
n
large enough. Consequently, there exists
v_{n}\in\partial U_{2,n}
so that
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2,n}}(u,v_{n};\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}})=0
for all
n
large enough. By passing to a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
(v_{n})
converges to a limit
v\in\partial\mathbf{D}
. Let
\Theta
be uniform in
[0,2\pi]
independently of everything else. Then
e^{i\Theta}\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
has the same law as
\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}
. Let
X
be such that
\varphi(X)
is equal to those points in
\partial U\setminus\partial D
which are not contained in a loop of
\Gamma
. Then we know that
X
has positive harmonic measure as seen from
z
. Therefore there is a positive chance that
e^{i\Theta}v\in X
and it is a positive probability event that
v\in X
. This is a contradiction because on the event that
v\in X
we have by Lemma
7.2
that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\varphi(u),\varphi(X);\varphi(\Gamma_{\mathbf{D}}))=0
, which contradicts the definition of
Z_{x,y}
.
∎

Figure 7.1.
Illustration of the proof of Lemma
7.10
. The set of points disconnected from
w
by
\eta|_{[0,t]}
is shown in blue and recall that
D_{t}
is the component of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\eta([0,t])
containing
w
. The point
v
is such that the quantum length of
[v,\eta(t)]_{\partial D_{t}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
is equal to
u
. The path
\eta_{t,u}
(red) is the right boundary of
\eta|_{[t,\infty)}
stopped when it first hits
v
as viewed from
v
and
\overline{\eta}_{t,u}
its time-reversal. The dashed curve is the remainder of
\eta_{t,u}
given
\eta_{t,u}|_{[0,s]}
and
\overline{\eta}_{t,u}|_{[0,s]}
shown on the event that it does not hit
\partial D_{t}
. On this event, its law is absolutely continuous with respect to an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
from
\eta_{t,u}(s)
to
\overline{\eta}_{t,u}(s)
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose that we have the setup of Proposition
7.6
and are on the event that
D\neq\emptyset
. Suppose
h
is such that
(D,h)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Let
\Gamma
be an independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
. Let
X
be the set of points
z\in\partial D
so that there exists
w\in\mathrm{cl}(D)\setminus\{z\}
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
.

(i)

Then
\mathbf{P}[\nu_{h}(X)=0]=1
and
X
is independent of
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
.

(ii)

Similarly, it a.s. holds that if
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
,
D_{\mathcal{L}}
is the set of points surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
, and
X_{\mathcal{L}}
is the set of
z\in\mathcal{L}
so that there exists
w\in\mathrm{cl}(D)\setminus(D_{\mathcal{L}}\cup\{z\})
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
then
\nu_{h}(X_{\mathcal{L}})=0
.

Moreover, the first assertion of the lemma holds with harmonic measure in
D
in place of
\nu_{h}
and the second assertion holds with harmonic measure in the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathcal{L}
in place of
\nu_{h}
.

Proof.

See Figure
7.1
for an illustration of the setup of the proof. Suppose that
h
is such that
(\mathbf{D},h)\sim\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Let
w
be sampled from
\mu_{h}
and
x
from
\nu_{h}
conditionally independently of everything else. Given
x
,
w
, let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
in
\mathbf{D}
targeted at
w
sampled conditionally independently of everything else and then parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk
\eta^{\prime}
. The
\tau
be the first time that
\eta
disconnects
w
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
and let
E
be the event that the component
D
of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\eta([0,\tau])
is not a loop of
\Gamma
and is surrounded counterclockwise by
\eta
. On
E
and given the quantum length
\ell
of
\partial D
we note that
(D,h)\sim\mathsf{QDiskW}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
. It suffices to prove (
i
) in this setting.

Let
t>0
be rational. Assume we are working on the event that
t<\tau
and let
D_{t}
be the component of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\eta([0,t])
which contains
w
. Fix
u>0
rational and let
v\in\partial D_{t}
be such that
\nu_{h}([\eta(t),v]_{\partial D_{t}}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}})=u
. Then we note that
\eta|_{[t,\infty)}
targeted at
v
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
process in
D_{t}
from
\eta(t)
to
v
. In particular, its trunk is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
process in
D_{t}
from
\eta(t)
to
v
. It thus follows from
[
24
, Theorem 1.4]
(see also
[
36
, Figure 2.5]
) that its right boundary (as viewed from
v
)
\eta_{t,u}
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(2-\kappa;\kappa-4)
process. Assume that
\eta_{t,u}
is parameterized according to quantum length and let
\overline{\eta}_{t,u}
be the time-reversal of
\eta_{t,u}
. Fix
s>0
rational and suppose that
\eta_{t,u}([0,s])\cap\overline{\eta}_{t,u}([0,s])=\emptyset
. On the event that the remainder
\widetilde{\eta}_{t,u}
of
\eta_{t,u}
does not hit
\partial D_{t}
, it follows from Lemma
3.5
that it is absolutely continuous with respect to an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
process in the component
D_{s,t,u}
of
D_{t}\setminus(\eta_{t,u}([0,s])\cup\overline{\eta}_{t,u}([0,s]))
with
\eta_{t,u}(s)
,
\overline{\eta}_{t,u}(s)
on its boundary from
\eta_{t,u}(s)
to
\overline{\eta}_{t,u}(s)
. By considering the setup from Lemma
7.7
, it follows that by applying a conformal transformation
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta_{-}\to D_{s,t,u}
which takes
0
(resp.
\infty
) to
\eta_{t,v}(s)
(resp.
\overline{\eta}_{t,v}(s)
) we have that with
\Gamma_{s,t,u}
a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
