D_{s,t,u}
and
X_{s,t,u}
the set of
z\in\widetilde{\eta}_{s,t,u}
so that there exists
w\in\mathrm{cl}(D_{s,t,u})\setminus\{z\}
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma_{s,t,u})=0
we have that
\mathbf{P}[\nu_{h}(X_{s,t,u})=0]=1
. The first part of (
i
) follows since this statement a.s. holds for all
s,t,u>0
rational simultaneously.

To see that
X
is independent of
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
, we simply note that
X
is determined by the restriction of
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
to the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\partial D
for each
\epsilon>0
.

Suppose that we are on the event that the harmonic measure of
X
is positive in
D
. Then as
X
is independent of
h
, it is easy to see that
\nu_{h}(X)>0
. Thus part (
i
) implies that
X
a.s. has zero harmonic measure in
D
.

We now prove (
ii
). Let
y\in\partial D
be picked from
\nu_{h}
independently of
x
and let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
in
D
from
x
to
y
coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI. Fix
\delta>0
and for each
j\in\mathbf{N}
let
\tau_{j}
be the
j
th time that
\eta
hits a loop of
\Gamma
with quantum length at least
\delta
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{j}
be this loop of
\Gamma
. Assume we have explored
\eta([0,\tau_{j}^{-}])
and the first
\delta
units of quantum length of
\mathcal{L}_{j}
starting from where
\eta
first hits
\mathcal{L}_{j}
and let
\eta_{j}
be the rest of
\mathcal{L}_{j}
. Then the conditional law of
\eta_{j}
given what we have explored is that of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in the remaining domain. In particular, we can sample from the joint law of
\eta_{j}
and the unexplored loops of
\Gamma
by first sampling from the law in the half-planar setup and then applying a conformal transformation from
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta_{-}
as in the proof of the first assertion of the lemma. This proves that the quantum length of the set of points
z
in
\eta_{j}
for which there exists
w\in\Upsilon\setminus\{z\}
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma_{j})=0
is a.s.
0
. Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary, we see that the quantum length of the set of points
z
in
\mathcal{L}_{j}
for which there exists
w\in\Upsilon\setminus\{z\}
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma_{j})=0
is a.s.
0
. This does not quite complete the proof as
\eta
does not hit every loop of
\Gamma
. However, Proposition
3.3
implies that the conditional probability given
\Upsilon
that
\eta
hits any loop
\mathcal{L}
of
\Gamma
in a fixed interval of
\mathcal{L}
is positive. Combining, this implies (
ii
). The assertion regarding the harmonic measure in the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathcal{L}
in place of
\nu_{h}
follows similarly as in the case of
X
described above.
∎

7.3.2.
Definition of the shields

Figure 7.2.
Illustration of the definition of a shield
A
, whose boundary is shown in red. We note that the set of points surrounded by loops contained in
A
are dense in
A
but are not equal to all of
A
since
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loops with
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
do not intersect each other. The region bounded by the dashed line is
O
.

We now suppose that we are in the setting of the statement of Proposition
7.6
and we have four distinct marked points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{4}\in\partial D
given in counterclockwise order. Let
(r_{k})
be an enumeration of the points with rational coordinates in
D
. For each
k
, we let
\mathcal{L}_{k}
be the loop of
\Gamma
which surrounds
r_{k}
. (We note that there a.s. exists a unique such loop for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
simultaneously.) We say that
j\sim_{0}k
if
j=k
or if there exists a closed and connected set
A\subseteq D
(so that
\mathrm{dist}(A,\partial D)>0
) such that
\mathcal{L}_{j},\mathcal{L}_{k}\subseteq A
and the harmonic measure of both
\mathcal{L}_{j}
and
\mathcal{L}_{k}
in
\mathbf{C}\setminus A
are positive and so that the following properties hold (see Figure
7.2
for an illustration).

(I)

Let
U
be the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus A
. Then
A=\mathbf{C}\setminus U
.

(II)

The set of
z
points in
\partial A
so that there exists
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
with
z\in\mathcal{L}
and so that
\mathcal{L}
has positive harmonic measure in
\mathbf{C}\setminus A
is dense in
\partial A
.

(III)

If
O\subseteq D
is open,
a_{1},a_{2}\in O^{*}
,
\Gamma_{O}
is equal to the loops of
\Gamma
which are contained in
O^{*}
, and there exists
\delta>0
so that
a_{1},a_{2}
are in different components of
O^{\delta}\setminus A
where
O^{\delta}
is the
\delta
-neighborhood of
O
, then
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})>0
.

We then let
\sim
be the finest equivalence relation on
\mathbf{N}
so that
j\sim_{0}k
implies
j\sim k
. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
, we let
A_{k}
be closure of the union of the points surrounded by
\mathcal{L}_{j}
such that
j\sim k
. We let

E_{k}=\{A_{k}\cap[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset\},\quad{\mathcal{K}}=\{k\in\mathbf{N}:E_{k}\text{ occurs}\},\quad\text{and}\quad K=\mathrm{cl}(\cup_{k\in{\mathcal{K}}}A_{k}).

We are now going to collect some properties of the
A_{k}
’s. In particular, we will show in Lemma
7.12
that
\mathbf{P}[E_{k}]>0
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
and in Lemma
7.13
that with
Y_{k}=\{z\in A_{k}:\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,\partial A_{k};\Gamma)=0\}
we have that
U_{k}=A_{k}\setminus Y_{k}
is simply connected and
\partial U_{k}=Y_{k}
. Lemma
7.11
is an intermediate step in both of these lemmas as it gives a construction of a set
A
satisfying properties (
I
)–(
III
) from above. We will then define a clockwise exploration of the
A_{k}
’s which hit
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
which we will prove in Lemma
7.14
is local for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
and use in Lemma
7.15
to show that the set of
A_{k}
’s which hit
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and leave the
\delta
-neighborhood of
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
is finite for each
\delta>0
.

Lemma 7.11
.

Suppose that we have the setup described above and fix
x,y\in\partial D
distinct. Let
W
be a component of
D\setminus Z_{x,y}
which is not surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
, let
I
be an arc of
\partial W\cap\partial Z_{x,y}
(in the sense of prime ends in
\partial W
) which has positive distance from
\partial D
, and let
\widetilde{A}
be the closure of the union of
I
and the loops of
\Gamma
whose intersection with
I
has positive harmonic measure in
W
. Let
A
be the complement of the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\widetilde{A}
. On
A\neq\emptyset
, we have that
A
satisfies properties (
I
)–(
III
) above. The same is also true with
Z_{x,y}^{+}
in place of
Z_{x,y}
.

Proof.

We will give the proof in the case of
Z_{x,y}
; the argument in the case of
Z_{x,y}^{+}
is analogous.

Property (
I
) holds by definition. Lemma
7.9
implies that if
w\in W
then the harmonic measure as seen from
w
of the set of points in
I
which are not in a loop of
\Gamma
is zero. Therefore (
II
) holds. Suppose that
O\subseteq D
is an open set,
a_{1},a_{2}\in O^{*}
, and
\delta>0
is such that
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
are in different components of
O^{\delta}\setminus A
where
O^{\delta}
is the
\delta
-neighborhood of
O
. Let
\Gamma_{O}
be the loops of
\Gamma
which are contained in
O^{*}
. If
a_{1}\in W
and
a_{2}\in O\setminus\mathrm{cl}(W)
then by the definition of
A
we must have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})>0
(for otherwise
a_{1}\in Z_{x,y}
). Now suppose that
a_{1},a_{2}\in W
. If
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})=0
then there must exist
u\in A\cap O^{*}
which is not in a loop of
\Gamma
so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},u;\Gamma_{O})=0
. This also contradicts the definition of
Z_{x,y}
so we must have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})>0
(for otherwise
a_{1}\in Z_{x,y}
). If
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
are in different components of
O^{*}
then
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})=\infty
. Lastly suppose that
a_{1},a_{2}\in O\setminus\mathrm{cl}(W)
. Suppose that
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
are in the same component of
O^{*}
(for otherwise
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})=\infty
). Since the loops of
\Gamma
do not intersect each other, it follows that if
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
are in different components of
O^{\delta}\setminus A
then any path in
\Upsilon\cap O^{\delta}
which connects
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
must enter
\overline{W}
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},a_{2};\Gamma_{O})=0
and let
Z_{a_{1}}
be the set of
u\in O^{*}
so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a_{1},u;\Gamma_{O})=0
. If
Z_{a_{1}}\cap W\neq\emptyset
then we get a contradiction to the definition of
Z_{x,y}
because then there exists
u\in W
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};\Gamma)=0
. If
Z_{a_{1}}\cap W=\emptyset
, then as
Z_{a_{1}}
is closed and connected and contains
a_{2}
it follows that its intersection with
\partial W
has positive harmonic measure in
W
. Indeed, the reason for this is that since the loops of
\Gamma
do not intersect each other there must exist
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
which is contained in
A
so that
\mathcal{L}\cup\partial W
disconnects
a_{1},a_{2}
in
O^{\delta/2}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
is in
A
, the points disconnected from
\infty
by
\mathcal{L}\cup I
are in
A
and its intersection with
I
must have positive harmonic measure in
W
. Thus as
Z_{a_{1}}
must contain
\mathcal{L}\cap I
we get a contradiction to Lemma
7.9
. Altogether, this proves that (
III
) holds.
∎

Lemma 7.12
.

Suppose that we have the setup described just above. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
we have that
\mathbf{P}[E_{k}]>0
.

Proof.

We first note that if we have a smooth and simple curve
\omega
which connects
r_{k}
to a point in
(z_{2},z_{3})_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
then with
\widetilde{A}
the closure of the union of the points surrounded by the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect
\omega
and
A
the complement of the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\widetilde{A}
we have that
A
satisfies properties (
I
), (
II
) as above. If property (
III
) holds with positive probability, then we have shown that
\mathbf{P}[E_{k}]>0
.

Suppose now that property (
III
) does not hold for this set. Then we in particular have that with positive probability there exist
z,w\in\Upsilon\setminus\partial D
distinct with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
. Note that for any
z\in\Upsilon
the set
\{w\in\Upsilon:\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0\}
is connected. Consequently, by using a CPI and applying Proposition
3.3
and Lemma
7.2
, we see that with positive probability the set
\{(z,w)\in\mathrm{Zero}^{+}(\Gamma):z\in\Upsilon,\ w\in\partial D\}
is non-empty. Applying Lemma
7.2
a second time implies that there exists
p>0
so that for all
x,y\in\partial D
distinct we have that
\mathbf{P}[Z_{x,y}^{+}\neq\emptyset]\geq p
. Moreover, Lemma
7.7
implies that
\mathrm{diam}(Z_{x,y}^{+})\to 0
in probability as
x\to y
. Thus by choosing
x,y\in(z_{2},z_{3})_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
sufficiently close we have both
Z_{x,y}^{+}\cap[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset
and
Z_{x,y}^{+}\cap\partial D\subseteq[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
with positive probability. On this event, let
W_{x,y}
be the component of
D\setminus Z_{x,y}^{+}
with
z_{4}
on its boundary. Lemma
7.11
implies that if
\widetilde{A}
is the closure of the union of an arc of
\partial W_{x,y}\cap Z_{x,y}^{+}
(in the sense of prime ends in
W_{x,y}
) with positive distance to
\partial D
and the loops of
\Gamma
whose intersection with it has positive harmonic measure in
W_{x,y}
then the complement
A
of the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\widetilde{A}
satisfies properties (
I
)–(
III
). If the intersection of the arc with
\mathcal{L}_{i}
, some
i\in\mathbf{N}
, has positive harmonic measure then all of the points surrounded by the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect the arc and have positive harmonic measure are contained in
A_{i}
and hence
\partial W_{x,y}\cap Z_{x,y}^{+}\subseteq A_{i}
. In particular,
A_{i}\cap[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset
.

To finish the proof, it is left to explain why
A_{k}\cap[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset
with positive probability. Let
\varphi
the unique conformal transformation which takes
r_{i}
to
r_{k}
and fixes
x
. Lemma
7.2
implies that
\varphi(A_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}A_{k}
. Consequently
\mathbf{P}[A_{k}\cap\partial D\neq\emptyset]>0
. Let
x_{0}
be a point in
\partial D
chosen from harmonic measure as viewed from
r_{k}
independently of everything else and let
y_{0}
be such that the harmonic measure of
[x_{0},y_{0}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
as seen from
r_{k}
is equal to that of
[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. If
\mathbf{P}[A_{k}\cap[x_{0},y_{0}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset]=0
then
\mathbf{P}[A_{k}\cap\partial D\neq\emptyset]=0
so we conclude that
\mathbf{P}[A_{k}\cap[x_{0},y_{0}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset]>0
. Let
\psi\colon D\to D
be the unique conformal transformation which fixes
r_{k}
and sends
x_{0}
to
x
. Then
\psi(A_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}A_{k}
by Lemma
7.2
. Altogether, this implies that
\mathbf{P}[A_{k}\cap[x,y]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset]>0
, which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.13
.

Suppose that we have the setup described above. Then the following a.s. hold.

(i)

If
z,w
are in the same component of
\partial A_{k}\setminus\partial D
then
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
.

(ii)

Let
Y_{k}
be the set of
z\in A_{k}
such that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,\partial A_{k};\Gamma)=0
. Then
U_{k}=A_{k}\setminus Y_{k}
is simply connected.

(iii)

\partial U_{k}=Y_{k}
.

Proof.

We will first prove part (
i
). As a first step, we will argue that
\partial A_{k}=\partial\mathrm{int}(A_{k})
. We clearly have that
\partial\mathrm{int}(A_{k})\subseteq\partial A_{k}
. Suppose that
z\in\partial A_{k}
and fix
\epsilon>0
. By the definition of
A_{k}
, there exists
i\in\mathbf{N}
with
i\sim k
and a closed set
A\subseteq D
with
\mathrm{dist}(A,\partial D)>0
satisfying (
I
)–(
III
) which contains
\mathcal{L}_{i}
and
\partial A
contains some point
w\in B(z,\epsilon)\cap A_{k}
. By (
II
), there exists
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
which is contained in
A
, has positive harmonic measure in
\mathbf{C}\setminus A
, and intersects
B(w,\epsilon)
hence
B(z,2\epsilon)
. Since the points surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
are in
\mathrm{int}(A_{k})
, it follows that
\mathrm{int}(A_{k})\cap B(z,2\epsilon)\neq\emptyset
. This proves that
z\in\partial\mathrm{int}(A_{k})
as
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary.

Let
I
be a component of
\partial A_{k}\setminus\partial D
. We assume that there exists
z,w\in I
distinct so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
and we will obtain a contradiction.

Let
X=\{u\in I:\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,\partial D;\Gamma)>0\}
. We first claim that
X
is dense in
I
. Suppose for contradiction that it is not dense in
I
. Then there exists
v\in I
and
\epsilon>0
so that with
J=I\cap B(v,\epsilon)
we have that
a\in J
implies that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a,\partial D;\Gamma)=0
. We claim that this implies that there exists
x,y\in\partial D
distinct,
u\in I
, and
\delta>0
so that
B(u,\delta)\cap I\subseteq Z_{x,y}
and
I
is not contained in
Z_{x,y}
. To see this, suppose that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are distinct points given in counterclockwise order in
\partial D
and write
x_{n+1}=x_{1}
. Let
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{m}\leq n
be a minimal collection so that
B(v,\epsilon)\cap I\subseteq\cup_{\ell=1}^{m}Z_{x_{i_{\ell}},x_{i_{\ell}+1}}
. Then there exists
u\in B(v,\epsilon)\cap I
which is not in
\cup_{\ell=1}^{m-1}Z_{x_{i_{\ell}},x_{i_{\ell}+1}}
. Let
\delta
be the distance of
u
to
\cup_{\ell=1}^{m-1}Z_{x_{i_{\ell}},x_{i_{\ell}+1}}
. Then
\delta>0
as
\cup_{\ell=1}^{m-1}Z_{x_{i_{\ell}},x_{i_{\ell}+1}}
is closed and we must have that
B(u,\delta)\cap I\subseteq Z_{x_{i_{m}},x_{i_{m}+1}}
. If it were true that
I\subseteq Z_{x_{i_{m}},x_{i_{m}+1}}
no matter how close we choose the spacing between the
x_{i}
’s, then by taking a limit as the spacing tends to
0
we would obtain that there exists
x\in\partial D
so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(a,x;\Gamma)=0
for all
a\in I
. This, in turn, contradicts our assumption that there exists exists
z,w\in I
distinct so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
. We can thus find
x,y\in\partial D
distinct,
u\in I
, and
\delta>0
so that
B(u,\delta)\cap I\subseteq Z_{x,y}
and
I
is not contained in
Z_{x,y}
. Then by considering a boundary arc of a component
W
of
D\setminus Z_{x,y}
which is not a loop of
\Gamma
, we get a contradiction to the definition of
A_{k}
because then Lemma
7.11
gives that we can use
Z_{x,y}
to construct a set
A
which satisfies properties (
I
)–(
III
) and connects a loop of
\Gamma
contained in
A_{k}
to a loop of
\Gamma
which is not contained in
A_{k}
. This contradicts the definition of
A_{k}
and therefore
X
is dense in
I
.

Since
X
is dense in
I
and there exists
z,w\in I
distinct so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
we may assume that
z,w\in I
are such that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,\partial D;\Gamma)>0
and
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
. This implies that there exists
\epsilon>0
so that for all
v\in B(z,\epsilon)
we have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(v,\partial D;\Gamma)>0
and
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(v,w;\Gamma)>0
. Let
z_{1}\in\mathrm{int}(A_{k})\cap B(z,\epsilon)
and
z_{2}\in B(z,\epsilon)\setminus A_{k}
. Let
A^{0}
be the set of points
u
so that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,[z_{1},z_{2}]\cup(\partial A_{k}\cap B(z,\epsilon));\Gamma)=0
. Then
A^{0}
has positive distance to
\partial D
. Let
\widetilde{A}
be the closure of the union of
A^{0}
and the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect it and let
A
be the complement of the unbounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\widetilde{A}
. Then
A
satisfies properties (
I
)–(
III
) and contains a loop inside of
A_{k}
and a loop outside of
A_{k}
. This also contradicts the definition of
A_{k}
. Altogether, this completes the proof of part (
i
).

We now turn to prove part (
i
). As
Y_{k}
is connected, to show that
U_{k}
is simply connected it suffices to show that
U_{k}
is connected. Suppose that
U_{k}
is not connected. Let
V_{k}
be the component of
U_{k}
whose closure contains
\mathcal{L}_{k}
and the set of points surrounded by
\mathcal{L}_{k}
. Let
V
be any other component of
U_{k}
and assume that
V
contains the points surrounded by some loop
\mathcal{L}_{i}\in\Gamma
. Then there cannot exist a set
A
which satisfies (
I
)–(
III
) and has a loop
\mathcal{L}_{V}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
) in
V
(resp.
V_{k}
) whose harmonic measure in
\mathbf{C}\setminus A
is positive because (
III
) will in particular be violated. To explain this point in further detail, suppose that there are such loops
\mathcal{L}_{V}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
and such a set
A
. Assume that
\partial V\cap\partial D=\emptyset
so that
u,v\in\partial V
implies
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,v;\Gamma)=0
for simplicity (the case that
\partial V\cap\partial D=\emptyset
is similar). As the harmonic measure of
\mathcal{L}_{V}
in
\mathbf{C}\setminus A
is positive, it follows from Lemma
7.10
that there is a point in
\mathcal{L}_{V}\cap\partial A
which is in
V
hence
\partial V\setminus A\neq\emptyset
. Since
A\cap\partial V
is closed, this implies that
\partial V\setminus A
has at least two points. Let
u,v
be distinct prime ends in
\partial V\setminus A
. Then either
[u,v]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
or
[u,v]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
is disconnected by
A
. Assume that we are in the former situation. Then we can find
O
open and
\delta>0
small so that
u,v\in O
and
A
disconnects
u,v
in the
\delta
-neighborhood of
O
, and
O
contains
[u,v]_{\partial V}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
. By the definition of
A
, this would imply that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,v;\Gamma_{O})>0
, which is a contradiction. The same argument applies in the case that
A
disconnects
[u,v]_{\partial U}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Therefore if
i\sim_{0}k
then the points surrounded by
\mathcal{L}_{i}
are contained in
V_{k}
, which implies that
U_{k}
has only one component.

That (
iii
) holds follows from the same argument used to show that
\partial A_{k}=\partial\mathrm{int}(A_{k})
given in the beginning of the proof.
∎

Figure 7.3.
Illustration of the exploration.
Left:
Shown are the sets
A_{k}
which intersect
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
, i.e.,
E_{k}
occurs (equivalently
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
). There is a natural clockwise ordering of these sets based on their rightmost intersection with
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
(though one has to be careful in the case that multiple such sets have the same rightmost intersection point).
Right:
We can explore the
A_{k}
for which
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
occurs from right to left using the aforementioned ordering and we can also partially explore an
A_{k}
by conditioning on part of the set
Y_{k}
of
z
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,\partial A_{k};\Gamma)=0
starting from the rightmost intersection with
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
as well as the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect this part of
Y_{k}
. One can think of this exploration as being continuously parameterized by using the extremal length in the remaining domain between
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
together with the part of the boundary which is in the exploration.

We are now going to describe an exploration of the
A_{k}
’s for
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
which goes in the clockwise direction. See Figure
7.3
for an illustration. Since we do not have any extra information on the
A_{k}
’s for
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
(e.g., regularity of
\partial A_{k}
) beyond the fact that they are closed sets and satisfy the assertions of Lemma
7.13
, some care will be needed in describing this exploration.

We note that there is a natural clockwise ordering of the
A_{k}
’s for
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
. Indeed, if
i,j\in{\mathcal{K}}
then we say that
i\leq_{\mathcal{K}}j
if the rightmost point of
A_{i}\cap[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
viewed as a prime end in the boundary of the component
W_{i,j}
of
D\setminus A_{j}
containing
A_{i}
is to the right of the rightmost prime end in
\partial W_{i,j}
corresponding to a point in
A_{j}\cap[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. If
i,j\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
i\sim j
then we will write
i=_{\mathcal{K}}j
. Finally, if
i,j\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
i\leq_{\mathcal{K}}j
and
i\neq_{\mathcal{K}}j
then we will write
i<_{\mathcal{K}}j
.

For a simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
and arcs
I,J\subseteq\partial D
we let
\mathrm{EL}(I,J;D)
denote the extremal length between
I
and
J
in
D
. Suppose we are on the event that
K\cap[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}=\emptyset
. Let
W
be the component of
D\setminus K
with
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
on its boundary and let
I=\partial W\cap(K\cup[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
. We note that
\mathrm{EL}(I,[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};W)\leq\mathrm{EL}([z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};D)
. For each
j\in{\mathcal{K}}
we let
K_{j}
be the closure of the union of the
A_{k}
’s for
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
and with
k\leq_{\mathcal{K}}j
. We also let
W_{j}
be the component of
D\setminus K_{j}
with
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
on its boundary and let
I_{j}=\partial W_{j}\cap(K_{j}\cup[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
. We let
{\mathcal{K}}_{L}
be the set of
j\in{\mathcal{K}}
with
\mathrm{EL}(I_{j},[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};W_{j})\leq L
. Finally, we let
K_{0}^{L}=\cap_{j\in{\mathcal{K}}_{L}}K_{j}
,
W^{L}
be the component of
D\setminus K^{L}
with
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
on its boundary, and
I^{L}=\partial W^{L}\cap(K^{L}\cup[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
. Then we have that
\mathrm{EL}(I^{L},[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};W^{L})\leq L
.

If
\mathrm{EL}(I^{L},[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};W^{L})<L
, then there exists
j\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
K_{j}=K_{0}^{L}
. In this case, we let
Y_{j}
and
U_{j}
be as in (
ii
) of Lemma
7.13
and let
\varphi\colon\mathbf{D}\to U_{j}
be a conformal map which sends
1
to the rightmost point of
Y_{j}\cap[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. For each
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, we let
I_{j}^{\theta}=\varphi([1,e^{i\theta}]_{\partial\mathbf{D}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
. We then let
A_{j}^{\theta}
be equal to the closure of the union of
I_{j}^{\theta}
and the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect
I_{j}^{\theta}
. We then let
K_{j}^{\theta}
be equal to the closure of the union of the
A_{k}
with
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
k<_{\mathcal{K}}j
together with
A_{j}^{\theta}
. We let
W_{j}^{\theta}
be the component of
D\setminus K_{j}^{\theta}
with
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
on its boundary and let
J_{j}^{\theta}=\partial W_{j}^{\theta}\cap(K_{j}^{\theta}\cup[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}})
. Finally, we let
\theta_{L}
be the smallest
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
so that
\mathrm{EL}(J_{j}^{\theta},[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial W_{j}^{\theta}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};W_{j}^{\theta})\leq L
. We then let
K^{L}=K_{j}^{\theta_{L}}
.

Lemma 7.14
.

For each
L
, we have that
K^{L}
is local for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
. We also have that
K
is local for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
.

Proof.

We will deduce the assertion from Lemma
7.4
. Suppose that
U\subseteq D
is open,
\epsilon>0
, and we use the notation from the statement of Lemma
7.4
. Then the event
K^{L}\cap U^{*}=\emptyset
is determined by
\mathcal{F}_{U}^{\epsilon}
. Since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, we have that
K^{L}\cap U^{*}=\emptyset
is determined by
\mathcal{F}_{U}
, which proves the result for
K^{L}
. The same argument gives the locality of
K
for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
.
∎

Lemma 7.15
.

Assume that there exist
x,y\in\partial D
distinct so that
\mathbf{P}[Z_{x,y}\neq\emptyset]>0
. For each
\delta>0
, the number of distinct sets
A_{k}
with
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
so that

(i)

A_{k}
is not disconnected from
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
by
A_{j}
for
j\in{\mathcal{K}}
with
j\leq_{\mathcal{K}}k
and

(ii)

A_{k}
is not contained in the
\delta
-neighborhood of
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}

is a.s. finite. In particular,
\partial K\cap\partial W\subseteq\cup_{k\in{\mathcal{K}}}A_{k}
so that for each
z\in\partial K\cap\partial W
there exists
k\in{\mathcal{K}}
with
z\in A_{k}
.

Proof.

Fix
\xi,\delta>0
. We note that by the local finiteness of
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
there a.s. exist at most finitely many distinct sets
A_{k}
so that
A_{k}
contains a loop with diameter at least
\xi
. Recall that
K^{L}
is increasing as
L
decreases. We let
L_{1}
be the largest value of
L
so that
K^{L}=K_{j}
for some
j\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
A_{j}
contains a loop with diameter at least
\xi
. Given that we have defined
L_{1},\ldots,L_{m}
, we let
L_{m+1}
be the largest
L<L_{m+1}
so that
K^{L}=K_{j}
for some
j\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
A_{j}
contains a loop with diameter at least
\xi
.

Fix a value of
m\in\mathbf{N}
and let
w_{m}
be the leftmost point on
K^{L_{m}}\cap\partial D
relative to
z_{3}
. Let
x_{m}
(resp.
y_{m}
) be the first point in the clockwise (resp. counterclockwise) direction along
\partial W^{L_{m}}
from
w_{m}
which has distance at least
\delta^{2}
from
w_{m}
. It follows from Lemmas
7.12
,
7.14
that conditionally on
K^{L_{m}}
, there is a positive chance that there exists
k\in\mathbf{N}
so that
A_{k}
disconnects
[x_{m},y_{m}]_{\partial W^{L_{m}}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
from
W^{L_{m}}\cap\partial B(w_{m},\delta)
in
W^{L_{m}}
. Let
N_{m}
be the smallest value of
n\geq m
so that
K^{L_{n}}
disconnects
[x_{m},w_{m}]_{\partial W^{L_{m}}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
from
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Then the number of sets
A_{j}
discovered by the exploration in
[L_{N_{m}},L_{m}]
which leave the
\delta
-neighborhood of
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
is stochastically dominated by a geometric random variable (whose parameter does not depend on
\xi
). Since
\xi>0
was arbitrary and the parameter of the aforementioned geometric does not depend on
\xi
, the assertion of the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 7.16
.

For each
L
in each component
W
of
D\setminus K^{L}
the harmonic measure as seen from
w\in W
of the set of those points of
\partial K^{L}\setminus\partial K
which are not contained in a loop of
\Gamma
is a.s. zero.

Proof.

Lemma
7.14
implies that
K^{L}
is local for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
. Consequently, the result follows from the same argument used to prove Lemma
7.9
. In particular, if
W
is a component of
D\setminus K^{L}
and the set of those points
z\in\partial K^{L}\setminus\partial K
which are not in a loop of
\Gamma
had positive harmonic measure then with positive probability there would exist some
A_{j}
and
z\in\mathrm{int}(A_{j})
so that with
Y_{j}
as in (
ii
) of Lemma
7.13
we have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,Y_{j};\Gamma)=0
and
z\notin Y_{j}
. This, in turn, is a contradiction.
∎

7.3.3.
Disconnecting the opposing arcs by shields

We consider the following exploration. Let
D_{1}=D
,
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma
, and let
K_{1}
be defined in the same way as
K
in terms of
D_{1}
,
\Gamma_{1}
as described at the beginning of Section
7.3.2
. We also let
z_{2}^{1}=z_{2}
and
z_{3}^{1}=z_{3}
. For each
j\geq 1
, given that we have defined the domain
D_{j}
, marked boundary points
z_{1},z_{2}^{j},z_{3}^{j},z_{4}
, loops
\Gamma_{j}
in
D_{j}
, on
K_{j}\cap[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}=\emptyset
we define
K_{j+1}
,
D_{j+1}
,
z_{1},z_{2}^{j+1},z_{3}^{j+1},z_{4}
, and
\Gamma_{j+1}
as follows. We let
z_{2}^{j+1}
(resp.
z_{3}^{j+1}
) be the leftmost (resp. rightmost) point on
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D_{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
(resp.
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D_{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
) relative to
z_{2}
(resp.
z_{3}
) which is contained in
K_{j}
. We let
D_{j+1}
be the component of
D_{j}\setminus K_{j}
with
[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D_{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
on its boundary and let
\Gamma_{j+1}
be the loops of
\Gamma_{j}
which are contained in
D_{j+1}
. We note that by Lemma
7.14
we have that
\cup_{i=1}^{j}K_{i}
is local for
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma))
. In particular, the conditional law of
\Gamma_{j+1}
and the interior-internal pseudometric in
D_{j+1}
is given by that of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
D_{j+1}
equipped with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{j+1})
. Let
K_{j+1}
be defined in the same way as
K
as described at the beginning of Section
7.3.2
but in terms of
D_{j+1}
,
\Gamma_{j+1}
,
z_{2}^{j+1}
, and
z_{3}^{j+1}
. As
\mathrm{EL}([z_{2}^{j},z_{3}^{j}]_{\partial D_{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D_{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};D_{j})
is decreasing in
j\in\mathbf{N}
, we see from Lemmas
7.2
,
7.12
that there exists
p_{0}>0
so that

(7.2)

\mathbf{P}[K_{j+1}\cap[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset\,|\,K_{j}\cap[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}=\emptyset]\geq p_{0}\quad\text{for each}\quad j\in\mathbf{N}.

Let
N
be the first
j
so that
K_{j}\cap[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset
. Then (
7.2
) implies that
N
is stochastically dominated by a geometric random variable with parameter
p_{0}
. In particular,
N
is a.s. finite.

For each
1\leq j\leq N
and
k\in\mathbf{N}
we let
A_{k}^{j}
,
E_{k}^{j}
be defined as above using
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{j})
and
[z_{2}^{j},z_{3}^{j}]_{\partial D_{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
in place of
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
and
[z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Lemma
7.15
implies that there a.s. exist
k_{1},\ldots,k_{N}
so that the
A_{k_{j}}^{j}
disconnect
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. For
\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})\in\mathbf{N}^{n}
, we let
E_{\mathbf{k}}
be the event that
A_{k_{1}}^{1},\ldots,A_{k_{n}}^{n}
are distinct, disconnect
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
, and that no proper subset of
\{A_{k_{1}}^{1},\ldots,A_{k_{n}}^{n}\}
disconnects
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
and
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Then what we have shown so far implies that
\cup_{\mathbf{k}}E_{\mathbf{k}}
a.s. occurs where the union is over
\mathbf{k}\in\cup_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\mathbf{N}^{n}
.

Suppose that we are working on
E_{\mathbf{k}}
. We let
x_{0}
be the rightmost point in
A_{1}^{1}\cap\partial D
relative to
z_{2}
. For each
1\leq j\leq n-1
we let
x_{j}
be the rightmost intersection point of
A_{k_{j+1}}^{j+1}
with
A_{k_{j}}^{j}
. We let
x_{n}
be the rightmost intersection point of
A_{k_{n}}^{n}
with
\partial D
. For each
1\leq j\leq n
, we let
Y_{k_{j}}^{j}
and
U_{k_{j}}^{j}
be as in (
ii
) of Lemma
7.13
and let
C_{j}=[x_{j-1},x_{j}]_{\partial U_{k_{j}}^{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Then we note that
\cup_{j=1}^{n}C_{j}
disconnects
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
from
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. For each
1\leq j\leq n
, we let
Q_{j}
be the closure of the union of
C_{j}
and the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect it. We let
Q_{\mathbf{k}}=\cup_{j=1}^{n}Q_{j}
. We note that
Q_{\mathbf{k}}
cannot intersect
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. Indeed, if
Q_{j}\cap[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset
then as the loops of
\Gamma
cannot hit
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
we would have that
C_{j}\cap[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}\neq\emptyset
. This implies that
C_{j}
intersects
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
at one of its endpoints (i.e.,
x_{j-1}
or
x_{j}
is in
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
) or there exists
y\in(x_{j-1},x_{j})_{\partial U_{k_{j}}^{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
which is also in
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. The latter cannot happen as it would contradict Lemma
7.13
which implies that each
U_{k_{j}}^{j}
is connected. The former a.s. cannot happen because the probability that any fixed boundary point is the rightmost in
A_{k_{j+1}}^{j+1}
is a.s. zero by Lemma
7.2
. Let
W_{\mathbf{k}}
be the component of
D\setminus Q_{\mathbf{k}}
with
[z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
on its boundary. We also let
\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}}
be the loops of
\Gamma
contained in
W_{\mathbf{k}}
.

We note that it can be that
Q_{1}
does not contain
\mathcal{L}_{k_{1}}
. Since
K^{L}
as defined earlier visits
Y_{k_{1}}^{1}
in counterclockwise order, it follows from Lemma
7.16
that there exists
i\in\mathbf{N}
so that
Q_{1}
contains
\mathcal{L}_{i}
. Applying the same principle to
2\leq j\leq n
implies that each
Q_{j}
also contains a loop of
\Gamma
. In particular, if we let
F_{\mathbf{k}}
be the event that
E_{\mathbf{k}}
occurs and each
Q_{j}
contains
\mathcal{L}_{j}
we have that
\cup_{\mathbf{k}}F_{\mathbf{k}}
a.s. occurs where the union is over
\mathbf{k}\in\cup_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\mathbf{N}^{n}
.

Lemma 7.17
.

On
F_{\mathbf{k}}
, given
Q_{\mathbf{k}}
the conditional law of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}}
and the associated interior-internal pseudometric in
W_{\mathbf{k}}
is given by that of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
W_{\mathbf{k}}
equipped with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})
.

Proof.

This follows from the same argument used to prove Lemma
7.4
. In particular, suppose that
U\subseteq D
is open,
\epsilon>0
, and we let
\mathcal{F}_{U}^{\epsilon}
,
\mathcal{F}_{U}
be as in the statement of Lemma
7.4
. Then it suffices to show that the event
F_{\mathbf{k}}\cap\{Q_{\mathbf{k}}\cap U=\emptyset\}
is determined by
\mathcal{F}_{U}^{\epsilon}
. This, however, is easy to see from the definition of
F_{\mathbf{k}}
and
Q_{\mathbf{k}}
.
∎

Lemma 7.18
.

Suppose we are working on
F_{\mathbf{k}}
and that
z\in W_{\mathbf{k}}
. Then the harmonic measure as seen from
z
of the points in
D\cap\partial Q_{\mathbf{k}}
which are not in a loop of
\Gamma
is a.s. equal to
0
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Lemma
7.16
.
∎

Figure 7.4.
Illustration of the proof of Proposition
7.6
. The sets
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,
C_{3}
are shown in blue and the loops of
\Gamma
which intersect them are shown in red. The green sets are
B_{2}
and
B_{3}
and the loops of
\Gamma
which hit
B_{2}
,
B_{3}
are shown in dark green. We note that the illustration might suggest that the argument used to prove Proposition
7.6
gives that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{2},z_{3}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},[z_{4},z_{1}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};\Gamma)=0
since if
z,w
are in the same component of
C_{i}\setminus\partial D
for some
i
then
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
. However, there is the possibility that one of the
C_{i}
’s hits
[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
in which case there may be
z,w
in different components of
C_{i}\setminus\partial D
with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(z,w;\Gamma)>0
.

Proof of Proposition
7.6
.

See Figure
7.4
for an illustration of the proof. We assume that we have the setup described just before the statement of Lemma
7.17
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},[z_{3},z_{4}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}};\Gamma)=0
. Fix
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbf{N}^{n}
, and suppose that we are working on
F_{\mathbf{k}}
. By the definition of
Q_{\mathbf{k}}
, we must have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},x_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})=0
for some
j
. Thus to prove the result it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},x_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})>0
for all
0\leq j\leq n
.

Let
(U_{2,m})
be an increasing sequence in
\mathsf{DyadDom}(D)
with
\cup_{m}U_{2,m}=W_{\mathbf{k}}
. Fix
1\leq j\leq n
and let
a_{j}
,
b_{j}
be distinct in
\partial W_{\mathbf{k}}
and distinct from
x_{j}
so that
x_{j}\in I_{j}=(a_{j},b_{j})_{\partial W_{\mathbf{k}}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}
. For each
\epsilon>0
we let
I_{j}^{\epsilon}
be the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
I_{j}
. We let
B_{j}
be the set of
u\in W_{\mathbf{k}}
such that for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
m_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
so that
m\geq m_{0}
implies that
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2,m}}(u,I_{j}^{\epsilon};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})=0
. We note that
\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})\to 0
as
a_{j}\to x_{j}
from the left and
b_{j}\to x_{j}
from the right by Lemmas
7.2
and
7.7
. Fix
\delta>0
and assume we have chosen
a_{j},b_{j}
sufficiently close to
x_{j}
so that the conditional probability that
\mathrm{diam}(B_{j})\leq\delta
given everything else is at least
1-\delta
.

We note that if
u\in W_{\mathbf{k}}\setminus B_{j}
then
\mathfrak{d}^{U_{2,m}}(u,B_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})>0
for all
m\in\mathbf{N}
. In particular, the only way that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,x_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})=0
can hold is if
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,a_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})=0
or
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,b_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})=0
. By Lemma
7.18
, we know that there are loops of
\Gamma
in
\partial W_{\mathbf{k}}
which are arbitrarily close to
x_{j}
. Combining this with Lemma
7.10
we also know that there exist points
a_{j},b_{j}\in\partial W_{\mathbf{k}}
which are arbitrarily close to
x_{j}
with the property that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,a_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})>0
for all
u\in\mathrm{cl}(W_{\mathbf{k}})\setminus\{a_{j}\}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,b_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})>0
for all
u\in\mathrm{cl}(W_{\mathbf{k}})\setminus\{b_{j}\}
. Moreover, whether
a_{j}
,
b_{j}
have this property is independent of
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})
. Therefore we may assume without loss of generality that we have made this choice. Altogether, we see that the conditional probability that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},x_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})>0
given everything else is at least
1-\delta
. Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary, we have that
\mathfrak{d}^{+}([z_{1},z_{2}]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}},x_{j};\Gamma_{\mathbf{k}})>0
a.s. Combining completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.5
.

Suppose that we have the setup of Proposition
7.5
. Suppose that with positive probability there exist
z,w\in\Upsilon
distinct with
\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
. We claim that we may assume without loss of generality that
z,w\in\Upsilon\cap D
. To see this, let
Z=\{u\in\Upsilon:\mathfrak{d}(z,u;\Gamma)=0\}
. Then
Z
is a connected set. If
Z\subseteq\partial D
, then we can write
Z=[a,b]_{\partial D}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}
for
a,b\in\partial D
distinct. This, in turn, contradicts the statement of Lemma
7.10
which completes the proof of the claim in this case. For the remainder of the proof, we shall therefore assume that we have
z,w\in\Upsilon\setminus\partial D
with
\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)=0
.

Fix
x,y\in\partial D
distinct and let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{1}(\kappa-6)
process in
D
from
x
to
y
coupled with
\Gamma
as a CPI. It follows from Proposition
3.3
that with positive conditional probability given everything else,
\eta
surrounds a domain
U
counterclockwise (so that there are no loops of
\Gamma
on its boundary) so that there are points
u,v\in\partial U
distinct with
\mathfrak{d}^{+}(u,v;\Gamma_{U})=0
where
\Gamma_{U}
denotes the loops of
\Gamma
contained in
U
. By applying a conformal transformation
D\to\mathbf{D}
and using Lemma
7.2
, we get a contradiction to Proposition
7.6
, which completes the proof.
∎

7.4.
Subsequential limits are geodesic

Proposition 7.19
.

Suppose that we have the setup of Theorem
1.1
and that
(\epsilon_{j})
is a sequence of positive numbers decreasing to
0
so that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
converges weakly as
j\to\infty
. Let
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
have the law of the subsequentially limiting metric on
\Upsilon
. Then
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is a.s. geodesic.

Proof.

To prove that a metric space
(X,d)
equipped with a good probability measure
\mu
(i.e.,
\mu(U)>0
for every non-empty open set
U\subseteq X
) is geodesic, it suffices to show that if
z,w
are independent samples from
\mu
then there a.s. exists
u
such that
d(z,u)=d(u,w)=d(z,w)/2
. That is,
z,w
a.s. have a midpoint. In our case, we will take the metric to be
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
and the good measure to be given by
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
where
h
is a field which describes an independent sample from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
parameterized by
D
.

Let
(\epsilon_{j})
be a sequence of positive numbers tending to
0
as
j\to\infty
along which
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
converges weakly. Suppose that we pick
z,w
conditionally independently from
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
given everything else and let
\omega_{j}\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon
be a continuous path such that
\omega_{j}(0)=z
,
\omega_{j}(1)=w
and
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(z,w;\Gamma)+\epsilon_{j}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}
. Let
s_{j}\in[0,1]
be such that

(7.3)

\displaystyle|{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([0,s_{j}]))-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(z,w;\Gamma)|\leq 2\epsilon_{j}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

Fix
A,\delta>0
and let
t_{j}
be the last time after
s_{j}
that
\mathrm{dist}(\omega_{j}(t_{j}),\omega_{j}([0,s_{j})))=\delta
. Let
\beta>0
be as in the statement of Proposition
6.1
and let
X_{j}
be the
\beta
-Hölder norm of
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
. On
\{X_{j}\leq A\}
, we have that
\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}(t_{j}),\omega_{j}([0,s_{j}]);\Gamma)\leq A\delta^{\beta}
hence there exists
r_{j}\in[0,s_{j}]
and a path
\widetilde{\omega}_{j}\colon[0,1]\to\Upsilon
with
\widetilde{\omega}_{j}(0)=\omega_{j}(r_{j})
and
\widetilde{\omega}_{j}(1)=\omega_{j}(t_{j})
so that
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{j})\leq(A\delta^{\beta}+\epsilon_{j}){\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}
. Let
\widehat{\omega}_{j}
be given by starting with
\omega_{j}
and then replacing
\omega_{j}|_{[r_{j},t_{j}]}
with
\widetilde{\omega}
. Then we have that

(7.4)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\widehat{\omega}_{j}([r_{j},t_{j}]))\leq(A\delta^{\beta}+\epsilon_{j}){\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}\quad\text{on}\quad\{X_{j}\leq A\}.

On
\{X_{j}\leq A\}
we also have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(z,w;\Gamma)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\widehat{\omega}_{j}([0,1]))

\displaystyle\leq{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([0,r_{j}]))+{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{j}([r_{j},t_{j}]))+{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([t_{j},1]))

(7.5)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(z,w;\Gamma)+(3\epsilon_{j}+A\delta^{\beta}){\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}+{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([t_{j},1]))\quad\text{(by ~\eqref{eqn:s_j_def}, \eqref{eqn:middle_part_bound})}.

Rearranging (
7.5
) implies that

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([t_{j},1]))\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(z,w;\Gamma)-(3\epsilon_{j}+A\delta^{\beta}){\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}\quad\text{on}\quad\{X_{j}\leq A\}.

Assuming that
j
is sufficiently large so that
2\epsilon_{j}\leq\delta
, we have that
\mathrm{dist}(\omega_{j}([0,r_{j}]),\omega_{j}([t_{j},1]))\geq\delta\geq 2\epsilon_{j}
so that

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([0,r_{j}]))+{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([t_{j},1]))\leq{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([0,1])).

Applying (
7.3
) again and rearranging gives

(7.6)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\omega_{j}([t_{j},1]))\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(z,w;\Gamma)+2\epsilon_{j}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

By passing to a further subsequence if necessary, we may assume that the joint law of
z
,
w
,
\omega_{j}(r_{j})
,
\omega_{j}(t_{j})
,
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
,
h
, and
\Gamma
converges weakly. Write
z
,
w
,
u
,
v
,
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
,
h
,
\Gamma
for the limit. Let
X
be the
\beta
-Hölder norm of
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
. On
\{X\leq A\}
we then have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(z,u;\Gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)\quad\text{(by~\eqref{eqn:s_j_def})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(u,v;\Gamma)

\displaystyle\leq A\delta^{\beta}\quad\text{(by~\eqref{eqn:middle_part_bound})}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(v,w;\Gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)+A\delta^{\beta}\quad\text{(by~\eqref{eqn:t_j_to_1_bound})}.

This implies that

\mathfrak{d}(u,w;\Gamma)\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)+2A\delta^{\beta}\quad\text{hence}\quad\mathfrak{d}(z,u;\Gamma)\geq\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)-\mathfrak{d}(u,w;\Gamma)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}(z,w;\Gamma)-2A\delta^{\beta}.

Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary and the metric
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
is compact, we see that on
\{X\leq A\}
there is a.s. a midpoint for
z
,
w
. This completes the proof as
X
is a.s. finite and
A>0
was arbitrary.
∎

7.5.
Comparability of normalizations

Proposition 7.20
.

There exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
we have that

c_{1}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}\leq{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}\leq c_{2}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{HP}}.

Proof.

Let
D_{\epsilon}=\sup_{z,w\in\partial D}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon}(z,w;\Gamma)
. By the definition of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}
, we have that

\mathbf{P}[{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}D_{\epsilon}\geq 1]\geq 1/2\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}[{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}D_{\epsilon}\leq 1]\geq 1/2.

Suppose that there exists a sequence
(\epsilon_{j})
of positive numbers with
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
such that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}/{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. By passing to a subsequence if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}\mathfrak{d}_{\epsilon_{j}}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
converges weakly as
j\to\infty
. Then
({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}D_{\epsilon_{j}}
converges weakly to a positive and finite random variable. Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{-1}D_{\epsilon_{j}}\geq 1]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\!\left[\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}}{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}D_{\epsilon_{j}}\geq 1\right]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad j\to\infty.

This contradicts the definition of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}
. Similarly, if there exists a sequence
(\epsilon_{j})
of positive numbers with
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
such that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}/{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
then (passing to a further subsequence if necessary) we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{-1}D_{\epsilon_{j}}\leq 1]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\!\left[\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}}}{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}}({\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}^{\mathrm{HP}})^{-1}D_{\epsilon_{j}}\leq 1\right]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad j\to\infty.

This also contradicts the definition of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\epsilon_{j}}
. Combining proves the result.
∎

Appendix A
Modulus of continuity of space-filling
{\rm SLE}
with quantum parameterization

Proposition A.1
.

There exists a constant
\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}\in(0,1)
so that the following is true. Suppose that
(\mathbf{D},h,-i,i)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be a space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loop on
\mathbf{D}
from
-i
to
-i
which is sampled independently of
h
and then subsequently parameterized by quantum area. Then
\eta^{\prime}
is a.s.
\alpha_{\mathrm{HO}}
-Hölder continuous.

We will prove Proposition
A.1
by using that for each
\xi>1
and all
\epsilon>0
small enough, whenever a space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
travels distance
\epsilon
it fills in a ball of radius
\epsilon^{\xi}
(Proposition
A.2
) together with a lower bound for the quantum measure on a quantum disk (Lemma
A.6
).

Proposition A.2
.

Suppose that
\eta^{\prime}
is a space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
on
\mathbf{D}
from
-i
to
-i
. For each
\xi>1
there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the following is true. For any
0\leq a<b
, on the event that
\mathrm{diam}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]))\geq\epsilon
we have that
\eta^{\prime}([a,b])
contains a disk of radius at least
\epsilon^{\xi}
.

The following is a restatement of
[
11
, Proposition 3.4]
as well as the discussion in
[
11
, Remark 3.9]
.

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose that
\eta^{\prime}
is a space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
from
\infty
to
\infty
in
\mathbf{C}
. For
\xi>1
,
R>0
, and
\epsilon>0
, we let
E_{\epsilon}
be the event that the following is true. For each
\delta\in(0,\epsilon]
and each
a<b\in\mathbf{R}
with
\eta^{\prime}([a,b])\subseteq B(0,R)
and
\mathrm{diam}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]))\geq\delta
the set
\eta^{\prime}([a,b])
contains a ball of radius at least
\delta^{\xi}
. Then
\mathbf{P}[E_{\epsilon}]\to 1
as
\epsilon\to 0
faster than any power of
\epsilon
.

Lemma A.4
.

Suppose that
M>0
and that
h
is a GFF on
\mathbf{D}
with Dirichlet boundary conditions which are at most
M
in absolute value. Fix
z\in\mathbf{D}
and
\epsilon>0
so that
B(z,2\epsilon)\subseteq\mathbf{D}
. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the harmonic extension of the values of
h
from
\partial B(z,2\epsilon)
to
B(z,2\epsilon)
. Then for every
p>0
there exist a constant
C_{p,M}<\infty
so that

\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(p\sup_{w\in B(z,\epsilon)}|\mathfrak{h}(w)-\mathfrak{h}(z)|\right)\right]\leq C_{p,M}.

The same likewise holds if
h
is a whole-plane GFF normalized so that its average on
\partial\mathbf{D}
is any fixed value in
[-M,M]
.

Proof.

This is a standard sort of calculation for the GFF; see the proof of
[
23
, Lemma 4.4]
as well as the argument used to prove Lemma
B.2
, in particular (
B.2
), (
B.3
), and (
B.4
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.2
.

Suppose that
\widetilde{h}
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by
-\lambda^{\prime}+2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
-\lambda^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. Let
h=\widetilde{h}\circ\psi-\chi\arg\psi^{\prime}
where
\psi\colon\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{H}
is a conformal map which takes
-i
to
0
. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loop associated with
h
. Fix
z\in\mathbf{D}
and
r>0
so that
B(z,2r)\subseteq\mathbf{D}
. Let
\widehat{h}
be a whole-plane GFF with its additive constant taken so that its average on
\partial\mathbf{D}
is uniform in
[0,2\pi\chi]
so that
\widehat{h}
has the law of a representative of a whole-plane GFF with values modulo
2\pi\chi
. Let
\phi
be a
C_{0}^{\infty}
function which is
1
on
B(z,r)
and
0
outside of
B(z,2r)
. Let also
\mathfrak{h}
(resp.
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}
) be the function which is harmonic in
B(z,2r)
with values given by those of
h
(resp.
\widehat{h}
) on
\partial B(z,2r)
. Finally, let
g=\phi(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}-\mathfrak{h})
so that
h+g=\widehat{h}
in
B(z,r)
. We assume that
h
,
\widehat{h}
are coupled together onto a common probability space so that the projections of
h
,
\widehat{h}
onto the space of functions which are supported in
B(z,r)
are the same and the projections of
h
,
\widehat{h}
onto the orthogonal complement are independent. Then the Radon-Nikodym derivative between the law of
\widehat{h}|_{B(z,r)}
and
h|_{B(z,r)}
is given by
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbf{E}[\exp((h,g)_{\nabla}-\|g\|_{\nabla}^{2}/2)\,|\,h|_{B(z,r)}]
. Lemma
A.4
and Jensen’s inequality then imply that
\mathcal{Z}
has finite moments of all orders (see, e.g., the proof of
[
23
, Lemma 4.2]
). Combining this with Lemma
A.3
implies that the following is true. Fix
\xi>1
. For
\epsilon\in(0,r)
we let
E_{z,r,\epsilon}
be the event that for every
0\leq a<b<\infty
such that
\eta^{\prime}([a,b])\subseteq B(z,r)
with
\mathrm{diam}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]))\geq\epsilon
we have that
\eta^{\prime}([a,b])
contains a ball of diameter at least
\epsilon^{\xi}
. Then
\mathbf{P}[E_{z,r,\epsilon}]\to 1
as
\epsilon\to 0
faster than any power of
\epsilon
. By combining this with the Borel-Cantelli lemma it thus follows that the following is true. There a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
0\leq a<b
such that
\mathrm{dist}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]),\partial\mathbf{D})\geq\mathrm{diam}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]))\geq\epsilon
we have that
\eta^{\prime}([a,b])
contains a ball of diameter
\epsilon^{\xi}
.

In order to finish proving the result, we need to consider the case that
\mathrm{dist}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]),\partial\mathbf{D})\leq\mathrm{diam}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]))
. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
and
1\leq j\leq N_{k}:=2^{k}
we let
z_{j,k}=\exp(2\pi ij/N_{k})
so that
z_{1,k},\ldots,z_{N_{k},k}
are equally spaced points on
\partial\mathbf{D}
. We also let
\eta_{j,k}
be the flow line of
h
starting from
z_{j,k}
with angle
\pi/2
stopped at the first time it exits
B_{j,k}=B(z_{j,k},2^{-\xi k})
. Then
\eta_{j,k}
gives part of the left boundary of
\eta^{\prime}
stopped upon hitting
z_{j,k}
. We let
F_{j,k}
be the event that
\eta_{j,k}
hits
\partial B_{j,k}
at distance at least
2^{-\xi k}/100
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
. It follows from
[
36
, Lemma 2.3]
that there exists
p>0
(which does not depend on
k
) so that
\mathbf{P}[F_{j,k}]\geq p
for each
j
. For each
j
, let
\mathcal{G}_{j,k}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\eta_{i}
for
i\neq j
. We claim further that there exists
p>0
(which does not depend on
k
) so that
\mathbf{P}[F_{j,k}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{j,k}]\geq p
. To see that this is the case, we let
\varphi_{j}
be the unique conformal transformation from the component of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\cup_{i\neq j}\eta_{i}
which contains
0
to
\mathbf{D}
which fixes
0
and
z_{j}
. Consider the GFF
h\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}-\chi\arg(\varphi_{j}^{-1})^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{D}
. Its boundary data in a neighborhood of
z_{j}=\varphi_{j}(z_{j})
is the same as that of
h
. Moreover, distortion estimates for conformal maps imply that
\varphi_{j}
looks like the identity near
z_{j}
. It thus follows from
[
36
, Lemma 2.3]
that there exists
p>0
so that
\varphi_{j}(\eta_{j})
has chance at least
p
of exiting
B(\varphi_{j}(z_{j}),2^{-\xi k})
at distance at least
2^{-\xi k}/10
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
. Altogether, we see that the collection of events
F_{j,k}
is stochastically dominated from below by a collection of
N_{k}
i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables with success probability
p
. In particular, for each
\alpha>0
there exists
C>0
so that the probability that there exists a run of
Ck
of the
F_{j,k}
which do not occur is at most
2^{-\alpha k}
. Combining, this implies that the following is true. If we have any segment of
\eta^{\prime}
which travels distance
Ck2^{-k}
along
\partial\mathbf{D}
, it must with overwhelming probability get to distance at least
2^{-\xi k}/100
from
\partial\mathbf{D}
. So the claim proved in the first paragraph implies that it also fills a ball of size at least
c_{0}2^{-\xi^{2}k}
for a constant
c_{0}>0
. This proves the result as
\xi>1
was arbitrary.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbf{R}
there exists a constant
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
so that the following is true. Suppose that
h
is equal to the sum of a free boundary GFF on
\mathbf{H}
and
-\alpha\log|\cdot|
with the additive constant fixed so that its average on
\mathbf{H}\cap\partial\mathbf{D}
is equal to
0
. There a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in\mathbf{H}\cap\mathbf{D}
with
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq\mathbf{H}\cap\mathbf{D}
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
.

Proof.

We will give the proof in the case that
\alpha=0
. The result for general values of
\alpha
follows because adding
-\alpha\log|\cdot|
to the field decreases the mass in a ball of radius
\epsilon>0
in
\mathbf{H}\cap\mathbf{D}
by at most a constant times the factor
\epsilon^{\alpha\gamma}
.

We note that we can write
h
as the even part of a whole-plane GFF
\widetilde{h}
on
\mathbf{C}
with the additive constant fixed so that its average on
\partial\mathbf{D}
is equal to
0
(see
[
41
, Section 3.2]
). Fix
\delta>0
. Since there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that
|\widetilde{h}_{\epsilon}(z)|\leq(2+\delta)\log\epsilon^{-1}
for all
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in\mathbf{D}

[
15
]
, it follows that
|h_{\epsilon}(z)|\leq(2+\delta)\sqrt{2}\log\epsilon^{-1}
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in\mathbf{D}\cap\mathbf{H}
. The result thus follows by combining this with
[
10
, Proposition 4.6]
and the Borel-Cantelli lemma.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
so that the following is true. Suppose that
(\mathbf{D},h,-i,i)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. There a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in\mathbf{D}
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
.

Proof.

We suppose that
(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)
is sampled from the infinite measure on quantum disks conditioned on having its projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
being at least
0
. Let
X_{u}
be the average of
h
on
u+(0,i\pi)
. We take the embedding into
\mathscr{S}
so that
\inf\{u\in\mathbf{R}:X_{u}=0\}=0
. Then we know that
X_{u}=B_{2u}+(\gamma-Q)u
for
u\geq 0
where
B
is a standard Brownian motion with
B_{0}=0
. Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{S}\to\mathbf{H}
be the map
z\mapsto\exp(-z)
. Then the restriction to
\mathbf{H}\cap\mathbf{D}
of
\widetilde{h}=h\circ\varphi^{-1}+Q\log|(\varphi^{-1})^{\prime}|
has the law of the corresponding restriction of the sum of a free boundary GFF on
\mathbf{H}
and
-\gamma\log|\cdot|
with the additive constant fixed so its average on
\mathbf{H}\cap\partial\mathbf{D}
is equal to
0
. Consequently, Lemma
A.5
implies that there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
and a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
we have that
\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
for all
z\in\mathbf{H}\cap\mathbf{D}
with
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq\mathbf{H}\cap\mathbf{D}
. It therefore follows that the same is true with
\mu_{h}
in place of
\mu_{\widetilde{h}}
and
z\in[0,\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}]\times(0,\pi)
.

Let
\tau=\sup\{u\in\mathbf{R}:X_{u}=0\}
. Then as
t\mapsto X_{-t+\tau}
has the same law as
X
, it follows that the above statement holds for all
z\in[\tau-\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon},\tau]\times(0,\pi)
.

Altogether, we have proved that the following is true. There exists a constant
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
so that for a.e. instance of
(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)
there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in[-\log\epsilon^{-1},\log\epsilon^{-1}]\times(0,\pi)
with
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq\mathscr{S}
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. In particular, the result holds for
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
for a.e. value of
\ell\in[1,2]
. Since a sample from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
can be produced by first sampling from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
and then adding
-\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell
to the field, it follows that the result also holds for a sample from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
which is parameterized by
\mathscr{S}
. This proves the result because then we can conformally map
\mathbf{D}
to
\mathscr{S}
with
\pm i
sent to
\pm\infty
and note that a ball of radius
\epsilon
centered at
\pm i
will contain
[\log\epsilon^{-1},\infty)\times(0,\pi)
and
(-\infty,\log\epsilon]\times(0,\pi)
, respectively.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.1
.

Suppose that
(\mathbf{D},h,-i,i)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
and
\eta^{\prime}
is a space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
from
-i
to
-i
which is sampled independently of
h
and then parameterized by quantum area. Let
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
be as in the statement of Lemma
A.6
. Then Lemma
A.6
implies that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in\mathbf{D}
with
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq\mathbf{D}
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. Fix
\xi>1
. Proposition
A.2
implies that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for every
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
0\leq a<b
with
\mathrm{diam}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]))\geq\epsilon
we have that
\eta^{\prime}([a,b])
fills in a ball of diameter at least
\epsilon^{\xi}
. This implies that
\mu_{h}(\eta^{\prime}([a,b]))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}\xi}
, which gives that
b-a\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}\xi}
as
\eta^{\prime}
is parameterized by quantum area. This proves that
\eta^{\prime}
is
(\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}\xi)^{-1}
-Hölder continuous, which gives the result.
∎

We are now going to use Proposition
A.2
to establish a non-self-tracing property for
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loops. We state the result when the domain is taken to be
\mathscr{S}
rather than
\mathbf{D}
due to how we will use it in Appendix
B
.

Lemma A.7
.

Fix
\xi>1
and suppose that
\Gamma
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
\mathscr{S}
. There a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
so that the following is true for every
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
.

(i)

For every loop
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
contained in
[\log\epsilon,\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}]\times(0,\pi)
, and
x\in\mathcal{L}
there exists
z\in\mathscr{S}
not surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
such that
B(z,\epsilon^{\xi})\cap\mathcal{L}=\emptyset
and
|x-z|\leq\epsilon
.

(ii)

Suppose that
k\in\mathbf{N}
and
U
is a complementary component after performing the iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(0)
/
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
construction of
\Gamma

k
times which is not surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
. If
\mathrm{diam}(U)\geq\epsilon
and
U\subseteq[\log\epsilon,\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}]
, then there exists
z\in U
such that
B(z,\epsilon^{\xi})\subseteq U
.

In order to prove Lemma
A.7
, let us recall how the iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
construction of
\Gamma
works in the context of imaginary geometry. Suppose that
h
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by
\lambda^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
\lambda^{\prime}-2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. For each
x\in\mathbf{R}
, we let
\eta_{x}^{\prime}
be the counterflow line of
h
from
0
to
x
. Then the collection of counterflow lines
\eta_{x}^{\prime}
together form the tree which generates a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(0)
, say
\Gamma_{0}
. Let
U
be a component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\Gamma_{0}
. Then
U
is either surrounded by a loop (clockwise) or by a false loop (counterclockwise) of
\Gamma_{0}
.

Suppose that
U
is surrounded clockwise. Then there exists
x\in\mathbf{R}
so that
U
is a component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta_{x}^{\prime}
. Let
z
be the first (equivalently, last) point on
\partial U
which is visited by
\eta_{x}^{\prime}
. Let
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbf{H}
be a conformal transformation which takes
z
to
0
and consider the field
\widetilde{h}=h\circ\varphi^{-1}-\chi\arg(\varphi^{-1})^{\prime}
. Then
\widetilde{h}
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by
-\lambda^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
-\lambda^{\prime}-2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. Let

c_{1}=\lambda(1-\kappa/2)+\lambda^{\prime}+2\pi\chi=\frac{5\pi}{2}\chi.

Then
\widetilde{h}+c_{1}
has boundary conditions given by
\lambda(1-\kappa/2)+2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
\lambda(1-\kappa/2)
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. For each
x\in\mathbf{R}
, we let
\eta_{x}
be the flow line of
\widetilde{h}+c_{1}
starting from
0
and targeted at
x
. Then the collection of flow lines
\eta_{x}
together form the tree which generates a
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}_{\kappa}(-\kappa/2)
, say
\Gamma_{1}
. Let
V
be a component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\Gamma_{1}
. Then
V
is surrounded by a loop (counterclockwise) or a false loop (clockwise) of
\Gamma_{1}
.

If
V
is surrounded counterclockwise, then
V
corresponds to a loop of the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
associated with the iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
exploration so the process terminates. Suppose that
V
is surrounded clockwise. Then there exists
w\in\partial V
with the property that if
x\in\mathbf{R}
is such that
\eta_{x}
traces part of
\partial V
then it hits
w
first. Let
\psi\colon V\to\mathbf{H}
be a conformal transformation which takes
w
to
0
. Then
\widehat{h}=\widetilde{h}\circ\psi^{-1}-\chi\arg(\psi^{-1})^{\prime}+c_{1}
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by
\lambda
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
\lambda-2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. Let

c_{2}=-\frac{\pi}{2}\chi.

Then
\widehat{h}+c_{2}
has boundary conditions
\lambda^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
\lambda^{\prime}-2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. These are the same as the boundary conditions that we started with and so we can start the exploration afresh.

Suppose that
U
is surrounded counterclockwise and let
\varphi
,
\widetilde{h}
be as defined above. Then
\widetilde{h}
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by
\lambda^{\prime}+2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
\lambda^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. Let

c_{3}=-2\pi\chi.

Then
\widetilde{h}+c_{3}
has boundary conditions
\lambda^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and
\lambda^{\prime}-2\pi\chi
on
\mathbf{R}_{+}
. These are the same boundary conditions as in the beginning of the exploration, so we start the exploration afresh.

Altogether, we see that in order to explore to a given loop
\mathcal{L}
of
\Gamma
, we have a certain number of counterclockwise steps by counterflow lines (each leading to an angle change of
-2\pi
) and then every clockwise step by a counterflow line (each leading to an angle change of
5\pi/2
) is followed by a successful counterclockwise step by a flow line (exploration terminates) or a clockwise step by a flow line (leading to an angle change of
-\pi/2
). Modulo
2\pi
, we thus have that there is just one angle for the flow lines which make up the associated
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loops, namely
\pi/2
. Similarly, the counterflow lines which are involved all have the same “angle” modulo
2\pi
. This means that they can all be viewed as arising from the same space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
.

Proof of Lemma
A.7
.

We will first prove the first assertion of the lemma. Fix any compact set
K\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(\mathbf{H})
and
\xi>1
. We assume that we have a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
process
\Gamma
which has been generated from an iterated
\mathrm{BCLE}
procedure using the GFF
h
on
\mathbf{H}
as described just above. Let
\eta^{\prime}
be the space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
process associated with
h
. Recall that its left boundary stopped upon hitting any point
z\in\mathbf{H}
is the flow line starting from
z
with angle
\pi/2
. By
[
11
, Proposition 3.4]
we have that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
x\in K
the following is true. Let
\tau_{x}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\eta^{\prime}(t)=x\}
and let
\sigma_{x}=\inf\{t\geq\tau_{x}:|\eta^{\prime}(t)-x|\geq\epsilon\}
. Then
\eta^{\prime}|_{[\tau_{x},\sigma_{x}]}
fills all of the points of a ball of radius
\epsilon^{\xi}
. Now suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a loop of
\Gamma
which is contained in
K
. As explained above,
\mathcal{L}
corresponds to a flow line of
h
with angle
\pi/2
. In particular,
\eta^{\prime}
visits the points of
\mathcal{L}
in reverse chronological order (relative to the order in which the points are drawn by the corresponding flow line) and visits all of the points of
\mathcal{L}
before entering its interior. Suppose that
x\in\mathcal{L}
. As explained above, we have that
\eta^{\prime}|_{[\tau_{x},\sigma_{x}]}
fills all of the points of a ball of radius
\epsilon^{\xi}
provided
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
. During this interval of time,
\eta^{\prime}
cannot enter into the region surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
, so this ball is disjoint from and is not surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
. The result follows by applying a conformal transformation
\mathbf{H}\to\mathscr{S}
.

The second assertion of the lemma follows from the same argument as the first assertion since once the space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
enters
U
it must fill it entirely before leaving.
∎

Appendix B
Density of quantum carpet typical points

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
and
\Gamma
is an independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
\mathcal{D}
. There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}>0
so that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the following is true. Let
(x_{j})
be an i.i.d. sequence picked from
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
and let
N_{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}}
. For all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
we have that
\Upsilon\cap([\log\epsilon,\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}]\times(0,\pi))\subseteq\cup_{j=1}^{N_{\epsilon}}B(x_{j},\epsilon)
.

Since the proof of Lemma
B.1
will require several steps, let us first outline the argument before proceeding with the details.

Step 1.

Use that each loop has a ball nearby which is not surrounded by a loop (Lemma
A.7
). This is a version of (and is deduced from) the non-self-tracing property of space-filling
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
.

Step 2.

We will consider a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
parameterized by
\mathscr{S}
and then prove an upper bound on the modulus and Dirichlet energy of the harmonic extension of the field values to dyadic squares (Lemma
B.2
). This will be used later on to control various Radon-Nikodym derivatives.

Step 3.

We will establish an upper bound on the quantum measure of small Euclidean balls for a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
when parameterized by
\mathscr{S}
(Lemma
B.3
).

Step 4.

We will establish a lower bound on
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(B(x,\epsilon))
where
x\in\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
is a loop with quantum length at least
\epsilon
(Lemma
B.5
).

Step 5.

We will prove in Lemma
B.6
that if we have a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
parameterized by
\mathbf{D}
then we need at most a polynomial number of independent samples from
\mu_{h}
in order to obtain an
\epsilon
-net of
\mathbf{D}
.

Step 6.

We combine the above estimates in order to complete the proof of Lemma
B.1
. We first let
N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
be a large negative power of
\epsilon
and then choose i.i.d. points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}
from
\mu_{h}
. Step 5 implies that this collection of points is likely to be an
\epsilon
-net of
\mathbf{D}
. We aim to show that if
B(z_{j},\epsilon)\cap\Upsilon\neq\emptyset
then
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(B(z_{j},2\epsilon))
is with overwhelming probability at least a power of
\epsilon
. Since
\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(\Upsilon)]<\infty
, we can also obtain an upper bound on
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(\Upsilon)
, so assuming the previous claim we have that if we pick
x
according to
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
then it has probability at least a power of
\epsilon
of being in
B(z_{j},2\epsilon)
in which case
B(z_{j},\epsilon)\subseteq B(x,4\epsilon)
. As the collection
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}
is likely to form an
\epsilon
-net of
\mathbf{D}
, it follows that if we choose
N_{\epsilon}
to be a sufficiently negative power of
\epsilon
and then choose
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N_{\epsilon}}
i.i.d. from
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
then the collection
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N_{\epsilon}}
is likely to be a
4\epsilon
-net of
\Upsilon
. By Step 4, if
B(x,\epsilon)\cap\mathcal{L}\neq\emptyset
where
\mathcal{L}\in\Gamma
has quantum length at least
\epsilon
then
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(B(x,2\epsilon))
is at least a power of
\epsilon
. Since
\mu_{h}(\Upsilon)=0
a.s., we have that
x
is a.s. in a component of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\Upsilon
which is surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
. The remainder of the proof is focused on bounding the total quantum area surrounded by loops of
\Gamma
with small quantum length to conclude that the loop which surrounds
x
is likely to be at least a power of
\epsilon
.

At this point in the article, it will be important for us to recall the precise definition of
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
given in
[
9
]
. We recall that the Dirichlet inner product of functions
f,g\in C_{0}^{\infty}
is defined by

(f,g)_{\nabla}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\nabla f(z)\cdot\nabla g(z)dz

where
dz
denotes Lebesgue measure. We also let
\|\cdot\|_{\nabla}
denote the associated norm. Then the Dirichlet inner product is defined more generally for
f,g
with
\|f\|_{\nabla}<\infty
,
\|g\|_{\nabla}<\infty
. It is easiest to give the definition of
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
parameterized by
\mathscr{S}
. We let
\mathcal{H}(\mathscr{S})
be the closure with respect to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nabla}
of those functions which are
C^{\infty}
in
\mathscr{S}
and have zero mean. We recall that
\mathcal{H}(\mathscr{S})
admits the orthogonal decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
where
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
consists of those functions which have mean zero on vertical lines of the form
u+[0,i\pi]
for
u\in\mathbf{R}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
consists of those functions which are constant on such vertical lines. We first consider the infinite measure
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}
on distributions
h
on
\mathscr{S}
which can be “sampled” from as follows (see
[
9
, Section 4.5]
for more details).

•

“Sample” an excursion
Z
of a Bessel process of dimension
3-\tfrac{4}{\gamma^{2}}
. Take the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
to be given by
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log Z
reparameterized to have quadratic variation
2dt
.

•

Take its projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
to be the corresponding projection of an independent GFF on
\mathscr{S}
with free boundary conditions.

As in
[
9
, Section 4.5]
, for each
\ell>0
we then define
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
to be the law on quantum surfaces given by taking the above distribution and conditioning the boundary length of
\partial\mathscr{S}
to be exactly
\ell
. We note that there is one free parameter to fix the embedding of a sample from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
into
\mathscr{S}
as defined above, namely the horizontal translation. There are a variety of different choices which can be convenient based on the particular situation. The points at
\pm\infty
are special in the sense that they are “quantum typical” boundary points. More precisely, this means that the following is true. Suppose that
(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
and that
x,y\in\partial\mathscr{S}
are picked independently from
\nu_{h}
and
\varphi\colon\mathscr{S}\to\mathscr{S}
is a conformal transformation which takes
-\infty
to
x
and
+\infty
to
y
. Then, modulo horizontal translation,
h
and
h\circ\varphi+Q\log|\varphi^{\prime}|
have the same law
[
9
, Proposition A.8]
.

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. We choose the horizontal translation so that
(-\infty,0]
and
[0,\infty)
have the same quantum length. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
be the set of dyadic squares
S
with side length
2^{-n}
such that the square
\widetilde{S}
with twice the side length and the same center as
S
is contained in
[-n,n]\times(0,\pi)
. For each
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
, we let
\mathfrak{h}_{S}
be the function which is harmonic in
\widetilde{S}
with boundary values given by those of
h
. There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{HARM}}>0
so that there a.s. exists
n_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
so that the following is true. For every
n\geq n_{0}
and
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
we have that

(B.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{z\in S}|\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z)|\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{HARM}}n,&\quad\sup_{z,w\in S}|\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z)-\mathfrak{h}_{S}(w)|\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{HARM}}n^{1/2},\quad\text{and}\\
&\int_{S}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z)|^{2}dz\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{HARM}}n.\end{split}

Proof.

We first note that since
\mathfrak{h}_{S}
is harmonic there exists a constant
c>0
so that if
\sup_{z,w\in S}|\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z)-\mathfrak{h}_{S}(w)|\leq\alpha_{\mathrm{HARM}}n^{1/2}
then we have that
\sup_{z\in S}|\nabla\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z)|\leq c\alpha_{\mathrm{HARM}}n^{1/2}/2^{-n}
. Thus it suffices to establish the first two inequalities in (
B.1
) (and then possibly increase the value of
\alpha_{\mathrm{HARM}}
).

We will prove the result in a slightly different setting and then deduce it as a consequence for
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. In particular, we suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)
is sampled from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}
conditioned so that the supremum of its projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
is equal to
0
. We recall that its projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
is equal to that of the corresponding projection of an independent GFF on
\mathscr{S}
with free boundary conditions.

Suppose that
\widetilde{h}
is a GFF on
\mathscr{S}
with free boundary conditions with its additive constant fixed so that its average on
(0,i\pi)
is equal to
0
. Let
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
be such that
\widetilde{S}
is adjacent to the
y
-axis. Let
\widehat{S}
be the square with the same center as
S
but with
3/2
times the side length. Thus,
S\subseteq\widehat{S}\subseteq\widetilde{S}
. Let
p
be the Poisson kernel in
\widehat{S}
and let
z_{0}
be the center of
S
. Then there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
(which does not depend on
n
) so that for all
z\in S
and
w\in\partial\widehat{S}
we have that
p(z,w)\leq c_{0}p(z_{0},w)
. Let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}
be the function which is harmonic in
\widetilde{S}
with the same boundary conditions as
\widetilde{h}
. We thus have for all
z\in S
that

(B.2)

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z)|
