\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\partial\widehat{S}}p(z,w)\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w)dw\right|\leq\int_{\partial\widehat{S}}p(z,w)|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w)|dw\leq c_{0}\int_{\partial\widehat{S}}p(z_{0},w)|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w)|dw

where
dw
denotes Lebesgue measure on
\partial\widehat{S}
. By Jensen’s inequality, we have that

(B.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\exp\left(c_{0}\int p(z_{0},w)|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w)|dw\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\int p(z_{0},w)\mathbf{E}[\exp(c_{0}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w)|)]dw.

It follows from the explicit form of the Green’s function with Neumann boundary conditions on
\mathscr{S}
that there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
so that for
w\in\partial\widehat{S}
we have that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w)
is a Gaussian random variable of mean zero and variance at most
c_{1}n
. Therefore there exists a constant
c_{2}>0
so that
\mathbf{E}[\exp(c_{0}\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w))]\leq e^{c_{2}n}
and
\mathbf{E}[\exp(-c_{0}\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w))]\leq e^{c_{2}n}
for all
w\in\partial\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
. This implies that there exists a constant
c_{3}>0
so that

(B.4)

\mathbf{E}[\exp(c_{0}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(w)|)]\leq\exp(c_{3}n).

Altogether, this implies that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\exp\left(\sup_{z\in S}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z)|\right)\right]\leq\exp(c_{3}n).

Therefore there exists a constant
c_{4}>0
so that

\mathbf{P}\!\left[\sup_{z\in S}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z)|\geq c_{4}n\right]\leq 2^{-3n}.

We now suppose more generally that we have
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
(with
\widetilde{S}
not necessarily adjacent to the
y
-axis) and let
\widetilde{X}
be the average of
\widetilde{h}
on the vertical line
L
which is adjacent to the right side of
\widetilde{S}
. As
\widetilde{h}-\widetilde{X}
has the law of a free boundary GFF on
\mathscr{S}
with the additive constant normalized so that its average on
L
is equal to
0
, what we have argued above implies that

\mathbf{P}[\sup_{z\in S}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z)-\widetilde{X}|\geq c_{4}n]\leq 2^{-3n}.

We note that
\widetilde{X}
is a Gaussian random variable with mean zero and variance at most
2n
. Thus by possibly increasing the value of
c_{4}
, the above in turn implies

\mathbf{P}[\sup_{z\in S}|\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z)|\geq c_{4}n]\leq 2^{-3n}.

We assume that
h
and
\widetilde{h}
are coupled together so that their projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
is the same. Let
\widetilde{X}_{*}
be the infimum of the average of
\widetilde{h}
on vertical lines with real part in
[-n,n]
. As the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
is in
\mathbf{R}_{-}
and the projection of
\widetilde{h}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
restricted to
[-n,n]\times(0,\pi)
is at least
\widetilde{X}_{*}
we have for every
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
and
z\in S
that
\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z)\leq\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z)-\widetilde{X}_{*}
. Since there exists a constant
c_{5}>0
so that
\mathbf{P}[|\widetilde{X}_{*}|\geq c_{5}n]\leq 2^{-3n}
, it follows from the above and a union bound that for a constant
c_{6}>0
we have that

\mathbf{P}[\max_{S\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}(\sup_{z\in S}\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z))\geq c_{6}n]\leq 2^{-n}.

By possibly increasing the value of
c_{6}
, we similarly have that

\mathbf{P}[\min_{S\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}(\inf_{z\in S}\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z))\leq-c_{6}n]\leq 2^{-n}.

Altogether, this proves the first inequality in (
B.1
) for the field
(\mathcal{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)
by applying the Borel-Cantelli lemma. The same argument with
\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z)-\mathfrak{h}_{S}(z_{S})
,
z_{S}
the center of
S
, in place of
\mathfrak{h}_{S}
gives the second inequality in (
B.1
). The reason that we obtain an
O(n^{1/2})
rather than
O(n)
upper bound is that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z)-\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{S}(z_{S})
has bounded variance. The same argument more generally implies that the same is true if
(\mathcal{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)
is sampled from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}
conditioned on the supremum of the projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
being equal to any fixed constant.

Let us now explain how to deduce the result where
(\mathcal{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
with the horizontal translation taken as in the statement of the lemma. Note that in this case
h
is dominated from above by the corresponding field when we sample it from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}
conditioned so that the boundary length is in
[1,2]
and from below when the boundary length is conditioned on being in
[1/2,1]
(with the horizontal translation taken in the same manner). Indeed, this follows because for each
\ell>0
we can produce a sample from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
by first sampling from the law
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
and then adding
\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell
to the field. Therefore it suffices to prove the statement when the boundary length is conditioned on being in any fixed non-trivial interval
[a,b]
. This, in turn, follows as we have shown above that it holds a.s. when the maximum of the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
is equal to any fixed value. In particular, the result holds for a.e. “sample” from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose that
\gamma\in(\sqrt{8/3},2)
and that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}>0
so that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that the following is true. For every
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and square
S\subseteq\mathscr{S}
with side length at most
\epsilon
we have that
\mu_{h}(S)\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. The same is also true if
\mathcal{D}
is parameterized by
\mathbf{D}
instead of by
\mathscr{S}
.

The statement of Lemma
B.3
in fact holds for all
\gamma\in(0,2)
but we have restricted to the case that
\gamma\in(\sqrt{8/3},2)
in order to give a shorter proof.

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

Suppose that
(\mathbf{H},\widetilde{h},0,\infty)
is a quantum half-plane. We note that there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}>0
so that for any fixed compact set
K\subseteq\mathbf{H}
there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
z\in K
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. Indeed, this follows from
[
37
, Proposition 3.5]
and the Borel-Cantelli lemma.

Let
\Gamma
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{H}
which is independent of
\widetilde{h}
and let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
process in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
which is coupled with
\Gamma
to be a CPI. Let
\tau
be the first time that
\eta
hits a loop
\mathcal{L}
of
\Gamma
with quantum length at least
1
and let
\ell
be the quantum length of
\mathcal{L}
. Let
D_{\mathcal{L}}
be the domain which is surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
. Then we have that
(D_{\mathcal{L}},h)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell}
and therefore
(D_{\mathcal{L}},h-\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. We can thus couple the quantum surface
(D_{\mathcal{L}},h-\tfrac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell)
and the quantum surface described by
(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)
to be the same. Let
\varphi\colon\mathscr{S}\to D_{\mathcal{L}}
be the corresponding embedding map. Then we know that
\varphi
is a.s. Hölder continuous
[
38
]
.

Fix
p\in(0,1)
and a compact set
K
so that
\mathbf{P}[D_{\mathcal{L}}\subseteq K]\geq 1-p
. Then by what is explained in the first paragraph, we see that on this event there exists
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
so that for all
z\in D_{\mathcal{L}}
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. Since
p\in(0,1)
was arbitrary we see that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
so that for all
z\in D_{\mathcal{L}}
with
B(z,\epsilon)\subseteq D_{\mathcal{L}}
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. The result thus follows by the a.s. Hölder continuity of
\varphi
.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(D,h,x,y)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. Let
\Gamma
be a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
D
which is independent of
h
. For each
\epsilon>0
, let
N_{\epsilon}
be the number of loops of
\Gamma
with quantum length at least
\epsilon
. Then for each
a>0
there exists
b>0
so that
\mathbf{P}[N_{\epsilon}\geq\epsilon^{-4/\kappa-1/2-a}]=O(\epsilon^{b})
.

Proof.

Fix
a>0
. Then we want to show that

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\log\mathbf{P}[N_{\epsilon}\geq\epsilon^{-4/\kappa-1/2-a}]}{\log\epsilon^{-1}}<0.

Suppose that this is not the case. Then there exists a sequence
(\epsilon_{j})
of positive numbers with
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
so that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log\mathbf{P}[N_{\epsilon_{j}}\geq\epsilon_{j}^{-4/\kappa-1/2-a}]}{\log\epsilon_{j}^{-1}}=0.

Let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
in
D
starting from
x
with the property that whenever it intersects itself it continues into the component which has the largest boundary length (and is not a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
loop). Fix
b_{1}>0
and let
M_{j}
be the number of downward jumps of size at least
\epsilon_{j}^{b_{1}}
made by the boundary length process for the target component. By Poisson concentration, we have for any
b_{2}>0
that
\mathbf{P}[M_{j}\leq\epsilon_{j}^{b_{2}-4b_{1}/\kappa}]\to 0
as
j\to\infty
faster than any power of
\epsilon_{j}
. Suppose we are working on the event that
M_{j}\geq\epsilon_{j}^{b_{2}-4b_{1}/\kappa}
. Let
(\mathcal{D}_{k})
be the sequence of quantum surfaces described by the components of
D\setminus\eta
which correspond to these downward jumps and let
\ell_{k}
be the quantum length of
\partial\mathcal{D}_{k}
. Then the
(\mathcal{D}_{k})
are conditionally independent given the
(\ell_{k})
and the conditional law of
\mathcal{D}_{k}
given
\ell_{k}
is
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\ell_{k}}
. Moreover, we have that the loops of
\Gamma
contained in
\mathcal{D}_{k}
are a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathcal{D}_{k}
. Consequently, it follows from
[
33
, Theorem 1.3]
that there exists
p_{0}>0
so that the probability that the number of loops of
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with quantum length at least
\epsilon_{j}
is at least
\epsilon_{j}^{b_{1}-4/\kappa-1/2}
is at least
p_{0}
. By the conditional independence and binomial concentration, we have on
M_{j}\geq\epsilon_{j}^{b_{2}-4b_{1}/\kappa}
that the number loops in
\mathcal{D}
with quantum length at least
\epsilon_{j}
is at least a constant times
M_{j}\epsilon_{j}^{b_{1}-4/\kappa-1/2}
off an event whose probability tends to
0
as
j\to\infty
faster than any power of
\epsilon_{j}
. Note that the overall exponent of
\epsilon_{j}
in this expression is:

\left(b_{2}-\frac{4b_{1}}{\kappa}\right)+b_{1}-\frac{4}{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}.

In particular, the coefficient of
b_{1}
is negative as
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
. Thus by choosing
b_{2}>0
sufficiently small, we obtain a contradiction to
[
33
, Theorem 1.3]
.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
and
\Gamma
is an independent
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
on
\mathcal{D}
. There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
so that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that the following is true. For every loop
\mathcal{L}
of
\Gamma
with quantum length
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
x\in\mathcal{L}
with
x\in[\log\epsilon,\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}]\times(0,\pi)
we have that
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(\Upsilon\cap B(x,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
.

Proof.

Step 1. Upper bound on number of loops.
Fix
a>4/\kappa-1/2
. Let
E
be the event that there are at least
\epsilon^{-a}
loops of
\Gamma
with boundary length at least
\epsilon
. Lemma
B.4
implies that there exists
b>0
so that
\mathbf{P}[E]=O(\epsilon^{b})
.

Step 2. Lower bound on loop diameter.
Let
F
be the event that
E
holds or there is a loop of
\Gamma
with quantum length at least
\epsilon
which surrounds a region with quantum area at most
\epsilon^{2+\zeta}
. Note that
[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
implies that the probability that sample from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=\epsilon}
has quantum area smaller than
\epsilon^{2+\zeta}
decays to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
faster than any power of
\epsilon
. It therefore follows that
\mathbf{P}[F]=O(\epsilon^{b})
. The Borel-Cantelli lemma thus implies that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
if
\mathcal{L}
is a loop of
\Gamma
with boundary length
\epsilon
then it surrounds a domain with quantum area at least
\epsilon^{2+\zeta}
. Lemma
B.3
implies that there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}>0
so that, by possibly decreasing the value of
\epsilon_{0}>0
, if
S\subseteq\mathscr{S}
is a square with side length
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
then
\mu_{h}(S)\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{UBD}}}
. By combining it therefore follows that there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}>0
so that (possibly decreasing the value of
\epsilon_{0}
) the diameter of any loop
\mathcal{L}
with quantum length at least
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
is at least
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}}
.

Step 3. Exploration.
We now consider a
center exploration
of
\mathcal{D}
. More precisely, we let
\eta
be an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
process in
\mathcal{D}
coupled with
\Gamma
to be a CPI with the property that whenever the trunk hits itself or a loop of
\Gamma
the process always continues into the complementary component (which is not a loop of
\Gamma
) with the largest boundary length. Fix
\epsilon>0
and for each
j
, we let
\tau_{j}
be the
j
th time that the center exploration has an upward jump of size at least
\epsilon
(hence discovers a loop of
\Gamma
with quantum length at least
\epsilon
). Let
\mathcal{L}_{j}
be the loop discovered by the center exploration at time
\tau_{j}
. Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be an
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}}/2
-net of
[\log\epsilon,\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}]\times(0,\pi)
. Then note that
n=O(\epsilon^{-2\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}}\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon})
. In particular, if
x\in\mathcal{L}_{j}
with
x\in[\log\epsilon,\log\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}]\times(0,\pi)
then there exists
1\leq i_{j}\leq n
so that
|x-x_{i_{j}}|\leq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}}/2
. Fix
\alpha>2\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}
, let
m=\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}-\alpha/2}
, and let
y_{1,j},\ldots,y_{m,j}
be points in
B(x_{i_{j}},\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}})\cap\mathcal{L}_{j}
with
|y_{i,j}-y_{k,j}|\geq\epsilon^{\alpha/2}
for
i\neq k
. Fix
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
. Lemma
A.7
implies for each
k
that there exists
z_{k,j}
and
r_{k,j}
with
|y_{k,j}-z_{k,j}|\leq\epsilon^{\alpha}
,
r_{k,j}\geq\epsilon^{\alpha^{\prime}}
,
|y_{k,j}-z_{k,j}|\leq 2r_{k,j}
so that
B(z_{k,j},r_{k,j})
is disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{j}
and not surrounded by it. Let
D_{j}
be the target component of the center exploration at the time
\tau_{j}
. By Lemma
A.7
, decreasing the value of
\epsilon_{0}>0
if necessary we may assume that each
B(z_{k,j},r_{k,j})
is disjoint from
\eta([0,\tau_{j}])
and is not surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
visited by
\eta|_{[0,\tau_{j}]}
. By possibly increasing the value of
\alpha^{\prime}
we may also assume that
B(z_{k,j},r_{k,j})
is contained in a dyadic square
S_{i,j}
so that if
\widetilde{S}_{i,j}
is the square with the same center and twice the side length then
\widetilde{S}_{i,j}
does not intersect
\eta([0,\tau_{j}])
and the distance of
\widetilde{S}_{i,j}
to
\eta([0,\tau_{j}])
is proportional to its diameter. We note by the Beurling estimate the probability that a Brownian motion starting in
B(z_{k,j},\epsilon^{\alpha})
exits
B(z_{k,j},\epsilon^{\alpha/2})
before hitting
\eta([0,\tau_{j}])
is
O(\epsilon^{\alpha/4})
. In particular, the probability that a Brownian motion starting in
B(z_{k,j},\epsilon^{\alpha})
hits
B(z_{\ell,j},\epsilon^{\alpha})
for
\ell\neq k
is
O(\epsilon^{\alpha/4})
.

Let
w_{0},w_{1}
be the two prime ends on
\partial D_{j}
which correspond to the point where the loop
\mathcal{L}_{j}
is attached to the target component boundary of the center exploration at the time
\tau_{j}^{-}
so that
[w_{0},w_{1}]_{\partial D_{j}}^{\bm{\circlearrowleft}}=\mathcal{L}_{j}
. Let
w_{2}
be some other point on
(\partial D_{j})\setminus\mathcal{L}_{j}
. Let
\varphi_{j}\colon D_{j}\to\mathbf{H}
be the unique conformal map which takes
w_{0}
to
-1
,
w_{1}
to
1
, and
w_{2}
to
\infty
. Let
h^{\mathrm{IG}}
be a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with the boundary values given by
-\lambda^{\prime}-\pi\chi
so that the loops of
\Gamma
in
D_{j}
can be viewed as the image under
\varphi_{j}^{-1}
of the
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
, say
\Gamma_{j}
, generated by
h_{j}^{\mathrm{IG}}
. (The boundary data here is different from as described after the statement of Lemma
A.7
because we have taken the root of the
\mathrm{BCLE}^{\bm{\circlearrowright}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(0)
exploration tree to be at
\infty
rather than at
0
.) By the conformal invariance of Brownian motion, we have for
i\neq k
the probability that a Brownian motion starting from any point in
\varphi_{j}(B(z_{i,j},\epsilon^{\alpha}))
hits
\varphi_{j}(B(z_{k,j},\epsilon^{\alpha}))
before hitting
\partial\mathbf{H}
is
O(\epsilon^{\alpha/4})
.

We will now restrict our attention to those
i,j
such that
\widetilde{S}_{i,j}
is contained in
D_{j}
. Let
w_{i,j}=\mathrm{Re}(\varphi_{j}(z_{i,j}))
,
s_{i,j}=2|w_{i,j}-\varphi_{j}(z_{i,j})|
, and
U_{i,j}=B(w_{i,j},s_{i,j})\cap\mathbf{H}
. Then it follows that the probability that a Brownian motion starting in any point in
U_{i,j}
hits
U_{k,j}
for
k\neq i
before exiting
\mathbf{H}
is
O(\epsilon^{\alpha/4})
. We let
\eta_{i,j}
be the flow line of
h_{j}^{\mathrm{IG}}
starting from
w_{i,j}+s_{i,j}/4
with angle
\pi/2
stopped upon first exiting
U_{i,j}
. Then it follows from
[
36
, Lemma 2.5]
that there exists
p\in(0,1)
so that the probability that
\eta_{i,j}
disconnects
\varphi_{j}(B(z_{i,j},r_{i,j}/2))
from
\infty
is at least
p
. The same remains true if we condition on
\eta_{k,j}
for
k\neq i
. Indeed, let
\psi_{i,j}
be the unique conformal transformation from
\mathbf{H}\setminus(\cup_{k\neq i}\eta_{k,j})
to
\mathbf{H}
with
\psi_{i,j}(\varphi_{j}(z_{i,j}))=i
and which fixes
\infty
. Then the boundary data for the field
h_{i,j}^{\mathrm{IG}}=h_{j}^{\mathrm{IG}}\circ\psi_{i,j}^{-1}-\chi\arg(\psi_{i,j}^{-1})^{\prime}
is at most
\lambda-\pi\chi/2
and is at least
-\lambda-\pi\chi
. In particular, the boundary data for
h_{i,j}^{\mathrm{IG}}
is bounded above and below by deterministic constants. Moreover, for all
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small it is equal to
-\lambda-\pi\chi
in a
B(0,100)
. This implies that the law of
\psi_{i,j}(\eta_{i,j})
stopped upon exiting
B(0,50)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of the corresponding flow line if the boundary data were equal to
-\lambda-\pi\chi
everywhere.

We note that
\eta_{i,j}
is equal to the left boundary of the counterflow line of
h_{j}^{\mathrm{IG}}
from
\infty
targeted at
w_{i,j}
(stopped upon exiting
U_{i}
). Let
W_{i,j}
be the component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta_{i,j}
which contains
\varphi_{j}(z_{i})
. On the event
A_{i,j}
that
W_{i,j}
is bounded, it follows that the conditional law of the loops of
\Gamma_{j}
which are contained in
W_{i,j}
is given by that of a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
W_{i,j}
. Let
V_{i,j}=\varphi_{j}^{-1}(W_{i,j})
and let
\psi_{i,j}\colon V_{i,j}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal map with
\psi_{i,j}(z_{i,j})=0
and
\psi_{i,j}^{\prime}(z_{i,j})>0
. Then the image
\Gamma_{i,j}
of the loops of
\Gamma
in
V_{i,j}
under
\psi_{i,j}
is a
{\rm CLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{D}
. By distortion estimates for conformal maps, we have that
\psi_{i,j}(B(z_{i,j},s_{i,j}))
contains
B(0,1/100)
. Suppose that
h_{i,j}
is a zero-boundary GFF on
\mathbf{D}
. For each
\delta\in(0,1)
, we note that
h_{i,j}
restricted to
B(0,\delta)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the corresponding restriction of the field which defines a sample from
\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
parameterized by
\mathbf{D}
. It therefore follows that the quantum measure on the carpet of
\Gamma_{i,j}
is defined. Moreover, for each
p_{1}\in(0,1)
there exists
c>0
so that the probability that the measure of the carpet in
B(0,1/100)
is at least
c
is at least
p_{1}
.

Let
h^{i,j}=h_{i,j}\circ\psi_{i,j}+Q\log|\psi_{i,j}^{\prime}|
. Then
h^{i,j}
is the sum of the zero boundary GFF
h_{i,j}\circ\psi_{i,j}
on
V_{i,j}
and the harmonic function
-Q\log|\psi_{i,j}^{\prime}|
. By distortion estimates for conformal maps and using that
s_{i,j}\geq\epsilon^{\alpha^{\prime}}
, we have that
|\psi_{i,j}^{\prime}|=\Theta(s_{i,j})=O(\epsilon^{-\alpha^{\prime}})
. By writing
\widetilde{h}_{i,j}=h_{i,j}\circ\psi_{i,j}=h^{i,j}-Q\log|\psi_{i,j}^{\prime}|
, we thus see that the following is true. There exists
p>0
so that the probability that the quantum measure on the carpet of
\Gamma
in
B(z_{i,j},s_{i,j})
associated with the zero boundary GFF
\widetilde{h}_{i,j}
on
V_{i,j}
is at least
s_{i,j}^{\alpha Q}\geq\epsilon^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}Q}
is at least
p
. We are now going to use an absolute continuity argument together with Lemma
B.2
in order to transfer this lower bound to obtains a lower bound for the amount of quantum measure in the carpet of
\Gamma
in
B(z_{i,j},s_{i,j})
associated with
h
instead of
\widetilde{h}_{i,j}
.

Let
S_{i,j}
be the dyadic square which contains
B(z_{i,j},s_{i,j})
so that if
\widetilde{S}_{i,j}
is the square with the same center and twice the side length then we have that
\widetilde{S}_{i,j}\cap\eta([0,\tau_{j}])=\emptyset
. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{S_{i,j}}
be as in Lemma
B.2
, let
\widehat{S}_{i,j}
be the square with the same center as
S_{i,j}
and
3/2
times the side length, and let
\phi
be a
C_{0}^{\infty}
function which is
1
on
S_{i,j}
and
0
outside of
\widehat{S}_{i,j}
. Then we have that
h-\phi\mathfrak{h}_{S_{i,j}}
restricted to
S_{i,j}
is equal in distribution to a zero boundary GFF on
\widetilde{S}_{i,j}
restricted to
S_{i,j}
. The Radon-Nikodym derivative between the law of the former with respect to the latter is given by

\mathcal{Z}_{i,j}=\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left((h,-\phi\mathfrak{h}_{S_{i,j}})_{\nabla}-\frac{1}{2}\|\phi\mathfrak{h}_{S_{i,j}}\|_{\nabla}^{2}\right)\,|\,h|_{S_{i,j}},h|_{\widetilde{S}_{i,j}^{c}}\right].

By Lemma
B.2
, we have for all
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small that
\mathcal{Z}_{i,j}^{p}=O(\epsilon^{-q})
. By applying Hölder’s inequality, we thus see that there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
so that the probability that the amount of mass in
V_{i,j}
is at least
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
is at least
\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. By choosing
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
sufficiently large, we thus see that, with overwhelming probability we have that
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(B(x,\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{DIAM}}}))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
.

We iterate the above until the exploration terminates. Upon doing so, we continue perform the center exploration in each of the components which have been discovered (and correspond to downward jumps). By Step 1, all of these explorations will discover
O(\epsilon^{-a})
number of loops with boundary length at least
\epsilon
. Therefore the result follows by performing a union bound.
∎

Lemma B.6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}=(\mathbf{D},h,x,y)\sim\mathsf{QDisk}_{\bm{\gamma}=\gamma}^{\bm{L}=1}
. There exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}>0
so that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
the following is true. Let
(z_{j})
be an i.i.d. sequence chosen from
\mu_{h}
and let
N_{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}}
. Then
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N_{\epsilon}}
forms an
\epsilon
-net of
\mathbf{D}
.

Proof.

We first choose
c>0
sufficiently large so that with
E_{c}=\{\mu_{h}(\mathcal{D})\geq\epsilon^{-c}\}
we have that
\mathbf{P}[E_{c}]=O(\epsilon^{\alpha})
; this is possible by
[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
. Lemma
B.3
implies that there exists
\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}>0
so that there a.s. exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
z\in\mathbf{D}
we have that
\mu_{h}(B(z,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. Suppose that
(z_{j})
is an i.i.d. sequence chosen from
\mu_{h}
. Suppose that
x\in\mathbf{D}
is fixed. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{N_{\epsilon}}\notin B(x,\epsilon),\ E_{c}^{c},\ \epsilon<\epsilon_{0}]\leq(1-\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}-c})^{N_{\epsilon}}\leq\exp(-\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}-c-\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}}).

If we choose
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}>c-\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}
we see that the above probability decays to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
faster than any power of
\epsilon
. The result thus follows by applying the above to the collection of points
(\epsilon\mathbf{Z})^{2}\cap\mathbf{D}
and using a union bound.
∎

We can now give the proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Suppose that we have the setup as described in the statement of the lemma and suppose that
z
is picked from
\mu_{h}
. We will first explain why it suffices to show that for every
a>0
there exists
b>0
(deterministic) and
\epsilon_{0}>0
(random) so that

(B.5)

\mathbf{P}[B(z,\epsilon)\cap\Upsilon\neq\emptyset,\ \mu_{h,\Upsilon}(B(z,\epsilon))\leq\epsilon^{b},\ \epsilon<\epsilon_{0}]=O(\epsilon^{a}).

Assuming (
B.5
), we claim that we can pick
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}>0
large enough so that

(B.6)

\mathbf{P}[B(z,\epsilon)\cap\Upsilon\neq\emptyset,\ x_{j}\notin B(z,\epsilon)\ \forall 1\leq j\leq N_{\epsilon},\ \epsilon<\epsilon_{0}]=O(\epsilon^{a}).

Indeed, this follows since the construction of
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}
in
[
33
]
implies that
\mathbf{E}[\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(\Upsilon)]<\infty
so by Markov’s inequality we have that
\mathbf{P}[\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(\Upsilon)\geq\epsilon^{-a}]=O(\epsilon^{a})
. Fix
\alpha>0
and fix
\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}^{\prime}>0
as in Lemma
B.6
. Let
N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}=\epsilon^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}^{\prime}}
and let
(z_{j})
be an i.i.d. sequence chosen from
\mu_{h}
. Then Lemma
B.6
implies that by possibly decreasing the value of
\epsilon_{0}>0
we a.s. have for all
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
that
\Upsilon\subseteq\cup_{j=1}^{N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}B(z_{j},\epsilon)
. We therefore have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\Upsilon\subseteq\cup_{j=1}^{N_{\epsilon}}B(x_{j},4\epsilon),\ \epsilon<\epsilon_{0}]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}[\cap_{j=1}^{N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}\{B(z_{j},\epsilon)\cap\Upsilon\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow\exists i:x_{i}\in B(z_{j},\epsilon)\},\ \epsilon<\epsilon_{0}]

\displaystyle\geq 1-N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\mathbf{P}[B(z,\epsilon)\cap\Upsilon\neq\emptyset,\ x_{i}\notin B(z,\epsilon)\ \forall 1\leq i\leq N_{\epsilon},\ \epsilon<\epsilon_{0}]

\displaystyle=1-N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}O(\epsilon^{a}),

where in the last step we used (
B.6
). By choosing
a=\alpha+\alpha_{\mathrm{NET}}^{\prime}
, this gives us the statement we want to prove, up to a redefinition of
\epsilon
.

Fix
c>0
. By Lemma
B.4
there exists
d>0
so that the number of loops of
\Gamma
with quantum length in
[2^{-k-1},2^{-k}]
is
O(2^{(4/\kappa+1/2+c)k})
of an event with probability
O(2^{-dk})
. By
[
1
, Theorem 1.2]
, the expected quantum area surrounded by such a loop is
O(2^{-2k})
. Therefore the quantum area surrounded by all such loops of
\Gamma
is
O(2^{(c+d+4/\kappa-3/2)k})
off an event with probability
O(2^{-dk})
. Note that
4/\kappa-3/2<0
since
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
so we can choose
c,d>0
sufficiently small so that
c+d+4/\kappa-3/2<0
. It therefore follows that the quantum area surrounded by loops of
\Gamma
with quantum length at most
2^{-k}
is also
O(2^{(c+d+4/\kappa-3/2)k})
off an event with probability
O(2^{-dk})
. By taking
k=\lfloor\log\epsilon^{-1}\rfloor
, we see that the quantum area surrounded of those loops of
\Gamma
with quantum length at most
\epsilon
is
O(\epsilon^{c+d+3/2-4/\kappa})
off an event with probability
O(\epsilon^{d})
. As
\mu_{h}(\Upsilon)=0
a.s., each
z_{j}
is a.s. surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
. The above discussion implies that there exists
b>0
so that the probability that any one of the
z_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
is surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
with quantum length which is at most
\epsilon^{b}
is
O(\epsilon^{a})
. Lemma
B.5
implies we have (possibly decreasing
\epsilon_{0}>0
) that if
B(z_{j},\epsilon)\cap\Upsilon\neq\emptyset
and
z_{j}
is not surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
with quantum length at most
\epsilon^{b}
then
\mu_{h,\Upsilon}(B(z_{j},2\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{\alpha_{\mathrm{LBD}}}
. Combining proves (
B.5
), which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Lévy process estimates

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}=(\mathbf{H},h,0,\infty)
is a quantum half-plane and
\eta
is an independent
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}^{0}(\kappa-6)
on
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
which is subsequently parameterized by the quantum natural time of its trunk. For each
t\geq 0
, we let
L_{t}
(resp.
R_{t}
) denote the change in the quantum length of the left (resp. right) side of the outer boundary of
\eta([0,t])
. In
[
33
, Section 4]
, it is shown that
L_{t},R_{t}
are independent
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy processes with the same law. Moreover, if we write the Lévy measure of
L_{t}
(equivalently,
R_{t}
) as
a_{+}|s|^{-4/\kappa-1}{\bf 1}_{s\geq 0}ds+a_{-}|s|^{-4/\kappa-1}{\bf 1}_{s<0}ds
then we have that the ratio
u=a_{+}/a_{-}
of the intensities of the upward to downward jumps is given by

u=-\cos(4\pi/\kappa).

Recall that
\beta=(a_{+}-a_{-})/(a_{+}+a_{-})
. Assuming that we have normalized the process so that
a_{+}+a_{-}=1
, the relationship between
u
and
\beta
is thus given by

u=\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}\quad\text{hence}\quad\beta=\frac{-1+u}{1+u}.

Therefore

(C.1)

\beta=-(\cot(2\pi/\kappa))^{2}=-(\cot(\pi\kappa^{\prime}/8))^{2}.

Recall from
[
4
, Chapter VIII]
that the positivity parameter
P
of an
\alpha
-stable Lévy process as a function of
\beta
is given by

P=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi\alpha}\arctan(\beta\tan(\pi\alpha/2)).

In our case,
\alpha=4/\kappa=\kappa^{\prime}/4
. Plugging in the value of
\beta
from (
C.1
) and this value of
\alpha
gives

P=1-\frac{\kappa}{8}.

Fix
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
. Let
X^{1},X^{2}
be i.i.d.
4/\kappa
-stable Lévy processes
X^{1},X^{2}
with positivity parameter
P
and starting from
0
(so that
(X^{1},X^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(L,R)
from above). Let
I_{t}^{j}=\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}X_{s}^{j}
and
S_{t}^{j}=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}X_{s}^{j}
for
j=1,2
, respectively, be the running infimum and supremum of
X^{j}
. We let
\tau^{j}=\inf\{t\geq 1:X_{t}^{j}=I_{t}^{j}\}
for
j=1,2
and
\tau=\tau^{1}\wedge\tau^{2}
.

Lemma C.1
.

We have that
\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq x]\asymp x^{-\kappa/8}
and
\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq x,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]\asymp x^{-\kappa/8}
as
x\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{X}
be an independent copy of
X^{1}
and let
\widetilde{I}
be its running infimum. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq x+1\,|\,I_{1}^{1},X_{1}^{1}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}[\widetilde{I}_{x}\geq I_{1}^{1}-X_{1}^{1}]=\mathbf{P}[\widetilde{I}_{1}\geq x^{-\kappa/4}(I_{1}^{1}-X_{1}^{1})]

\displaystyle\asymp x^{-\kappa/8}(X_{1}^{1}-I_{1}^{1})^{1/2},

where in the last line we applied
[
4
, Chapter VIII, Proposition 2]
. Taking expectations of both sides and using that
|X_{1}^{1}-I_{1}^{1}|
has a finite mean hence a finite
1/2
-moment, we see that
\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq x]\asymp x^{-\kappa/8}
. This proves the first assertion of the lemma. The same argument gives the second assertion except we take a conditional expectation of
X_{1}^{1}
given
I_{1}^{1}\geq-1
.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
C.1
, we have that

(C.2)

\mathbf{P}[\tau\geq x]=\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq x]\mathbf{P}[\tau^{2}\geq x]\asymp x^{-\kappa/4}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}[\tau\geq x,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]\asymp x^{-\kappa/4}\quad\text{as}\quad x\to\infty.

Fix
M\geq 1
.

Lemma C.2
.

There exists a constant
c>0
so that
-\mathbf{E}[I_{\tau\wedge M}]\geq c(\log M)
.

Before we give the proof of Lemma
C.2
, we first need to collect the following lemma.

Lemma C.3
.

There exists a constant
c>0
so that the following is true. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
let
\sigma_{k}^{1}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}^{1}\geq k^{\kappa/4}\}
. Then

\mathbf{E}[X_{\sigma_{k}^{1}}^{1}\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]\leq ck^{\kappa/4}.

Proof.

Fix
\delta>0
. For each
j\geq 0
, we let
S_{j}^{\delta,1}=\sup_{t\in[j\delta,(j+1)\delta]}X_{t}^{1}
. Fix
u\geq k^{\kappa/4}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[X_{\sigma_{k}^{1}}^{1}\geq u\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u,\sigma_{k}^{1}\geq j\delta\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u,\sigma_{k}^{1}\geq j\delta,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}{\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u,\sigma_{k}^{1}\in[j\delta,(j+1)\delta],\tau^{1}\geq j\delta]}{\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}

(C.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u\,|\,\sigma_{k}^{1}\in[j\delta,(j+1)\delta],\tau^{1}\geq j\delta]\mathbf{P}[\sigma_{k}^{1}\in[j\delta,(j+1)\delta],\tau^{1}\geq j\delta]}{\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}.

We note for
v<k^{\kappa/4}
that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u\,|\,\sigma_{k}^{1}\in[j\delta,(j+1)\delta],\tau^{1}\geq j\delta,X_{j\delta}^{1}=v]=\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u\,|\,S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq k^{\kappa/4},\sigma_{k}^{1}\geq j\delta,\tau^{1}\geq j\delta,X_{j\delta}^{1}=v]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u\,|\,S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq k^{\kappa/4},X_{j\delta}^{1}=v]=\frac{\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq u\,|\,X_{j\delta}^{1}=v]}{\mathbf{P}[S_{j}^{\delta,1}\geq k^{\kappa/4}\,|\,X_{j\delta}^{1}=v]}.

By
[
4
, Chapter VIII, Proposition 4]
, the ratio above converges to
(u-v)^{-4/\kappa}/(k^{\kappa/4}-v)^{-4/\kappa}
as
\delta\to 0
. Assume that
u\geq 2k^{4/\kappa}
. Then we have for a constant
c>0
that

\left(\frac{u-v}{k^{\kappa/4}-v}\right)^{-4/\kappa}=u^{-4/\kappa}\left(\frac{k^{\kappa/4}-v}{1-v/u}\right)^{4/\kappa}\leq cku^{-4/\kappa}.

Altogether, taking a limit as
\delta\to 0
and inserting these bounds into (
C.3
) implies for a constant
c>0
that

\mathbf{P}[X_{\sigma_{k}^{1}}^{1}\geq u\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]\leq cku^{-4/\kappa}\quad\text{for}\quad u\geq 2k^{\kappa/4}.

Therefore for constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[X_{\sigma_{k}^{1}}^{1}\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]

\displaystyle\leq 2k^{\kappa/4}+\mathbf{E}[X_{\sigma_{k}^{1}}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\{X_{\sigma_{k}^{1}}^{1}\geq 2k^{\kappa/4}\}}\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]

\displaystyle\leq 2k^{\kappa/4}+c_{1}k\int_{2k^{\kappa/4}}^{\infty}u^{-4/\kappa}du\leq c_{2}k^{\kappa/4}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
C.2
.

We first claim that

(C.4)

\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4}\,|\,\tau\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad R\to\infty\quad\text{uniformly in}\quad k.

To see this, fix
R\geq 2
. For each
k
, let
\sigma_{k}^{1}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}^{1}\geq k^{\kappa/4}\}
be as in Lemma
C.3
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4}\,|\,\tau\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4}\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]\quad\text{($X^{1},X^{2}$ independent)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4},\tau^{1}\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]}{\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4},\tau^{1}\geq k]}{\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k]}\quad\text{(by Lemma~\ref{lem:tauj_tail})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4},\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}{\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}

(C.5)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4}\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}{\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]}.

We note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]=\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}].

It is easy to see that
\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq k\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]
is bounded from below by a positive constant for
k\in\mathbf{N}
. Consequently, (
C.5
) is bounded from above by a constant times
\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\geq Rk^{\kappa/4}\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{k}^{1}]
which tends to
0
as
R\to\infty
uniformly in
k
by Markov’s inequality and Lemma
C.3
. This proves (
C.4
).

Assume that we have chosen
R\geq 2
sufficiently large so that

(C.6)

\mathbf{P}[X_{k}^{1}\leq Rk^{\kappa/4}\,|\,\tau\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]\geq 1/2\quad\text{for all}\quad k.

Let
E_{k}^{1}
be the event that
X^{1}|_{[k,k+1]}
makes a downward jump of size at least
(R+1)k^{\kappa/4}
. Since the number of such jumps that
X^{1}|_{[k,k+1]}
makes is distributed as a Poisson random variable with mean proportional to
k^{-1}
, we have that

(C.7)

\mathbf{P}[E_{k}^{1}\,|\,X_{k}^{1}\leq Rk^{\kappa/4},\tau\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]=\mathbf{P}[E_{k}^{1}]\gtrsim k^{-1}.

It therefore follows that there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
so that

\displaystyle-\mathbf{E}[I_{\tau\wedge M}]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=1}^{M}k^{\kappa/4}\mathbf{P}[E_{k}^{1},\ \tau\geq k,X_{k}^{1}\leq Rk^{\kappa/4},I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=1}^{M}k^{\kappa/4}\times c_{1}k^{-1}\mathbf{P}[\tau\geq k,I_{1}^{1}\geq-1]\quad\text{(by~\eqref{eqn:x1alphakbound}, \eqref{eqn:ek1givlbd})}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=1}^{M}c_{2}k^{-1}\quad\text{(by~\eqref{eqn:tautail})}

\displaystyle\geq c_{3}(\log M).

This proves the result.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

We have that

\mathbf{E}[(X_{M}^{1}-I_{M}^{1}){\bf 1}_{\tau\geq M}]=O(1)
.

Proof.

On the event
\tau\geq M
, we have that
I_{M}^{1}=I_{1}^{1}
. Fix
p\in(1,4/\kappa)
and let
q>1
be such that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. Since
|I_{1}^{1}|
has a finite
p
th moment
[
4
, Chapter VIII, Proposition 4]
, we have for a constant
c>0
that

(C.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[-I_{M}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\tau\geq M}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}[-I_{1}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\tau\geq M}]\leq\mathbf{E}[|I_{1}^{1}|^{p}]^{1/p}\mathbf{P}[\tau\geq M]^{1/q}\leq cM^{-q\kappa/4}=O(1).

Note that we used (
C.2
) in the second inequality.

Let
\sigma_{M}^{1}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}^{1}\geq M^{\kappa/4}\}
. By the independence of
X^{1},X^{2}
, we have that

(C.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[X_{M}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\tau\geq M}({\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M}+{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}>M})]

\displaystyle\asymp M^{-\kappa/8}\mathbf{E}[X_{M}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\tau^{1}\geq M}({\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M}+{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}>M})]

For each
t\geq 0
let
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1}=\sigma(X_{s}^{1}:s\leq t)
. We moreover have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[X_{M}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\tau^{1}\geq M}({\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M}+{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}>M})]

\displaystyle\leq M^{\kappa/4}\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq M]+\mathbf{E}[X_{M}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M\leq\tau^{1}}]

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{\kappa/8}+\mathbf{E}[X_{M}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M\leq\tau^{1}}]\quad\text{(by Lemma~\ref{lem:tauj_tail})}

\displaystyle\leq M^{\kappa/8}+\mathbf{E}[|X_{M}^{1}-X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}|{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M\leq\tau^{1}}]+\mathbf{E}[X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M\leq\tau^{1}}]

(C.10)

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{\kappa/8}+\mathbf{E}[|X_{M}^{1}-X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}|{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M\leq\tau^{1}}]\quad\text{(by Lemmas~\ref{lem:tauj_tail}, \ref{lem:levy_moment_estimate})}.

We emphasize that in the last line we used that
\mathbf{E}[X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M\leq\tau^{1}}]\leq\mathbf{E}[X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}\,|\,\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq\tau^{1}]\mathbf{P}[\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq\tau^{1}]
and
\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq M\,|\,\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{M}^{1}]
is positive uniformly in
M
so that
\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq\sigma_{M}^{1}]\lesssim\mathbf{P}[\tau^{1}\geq M]\asymp M^{-\kappa/8}
. We further have that (
C.10
) is bounded from above by

\displaystyle M^{\kappa/8}+\mathbf{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq M}|X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}+t}^{1}-X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}|{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq M\leq\tau^{1}}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{\kappa/8}+\mathbf{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq M}|X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}+t}^{1}-X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}|{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq\tau^{1}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{\kappa/8}+\mathbf{E}[\mathbf{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq M}|X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}+t}^{1}-X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}]{\bf 1}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq\tau^{1}}]

(C.11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M^{\kappa/8}+\mathbf{E}[S_{M}^{1}]\mathbf{P}[\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq\tau^{1}].

In the final inequality, we used that
\mathbf{E}[\sup_{0\leq t\leq M}|X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}+t}^{1}-X_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{M}^{1}}^{1}]=\mathbf{E}[S_{M}^{1}]
. As we explained just above,
\mathbf{P}[\sigma_{M}^{1}\leq\tau^{1}]\lesssim M^{-\kappa/8}
and it follows from
[
4
, Chapter VII, Corollary 2]
that
\mathbf{E}[S_{M}^{1}]=M^{\kappa/4}\mathbf{E}[S_{1}^{1}]\asymp M^{\kappa/4}
. Combining this with (
C.9
) and (
C.11
) implies that
\mathbf{E}[X_{M}^{1}{\bf 1}_{\tau\geq M}]=O(1)
which, when combined with (
C.8
), proves the result.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

Fix
\alpha\in(1,2)
and suppose that
X
is an
\alpha
-stable Lévy process with upward (and possibly downward) jumps. There exists a constant
c>0
so that the following is true. For each
n\in\mathbf{N}
, let
S_{n}
be the sum of the largest
n
upward jumps made by
X|_{[0,1]}
. Then

\mathbf{E}[S_{n}]\leq cn^{1-1/\alpha}.

Proof.

Step 1: Setup.
Fix
k\in\mathbf{Z}
. Recall that the number
N_{k}
of upward jumps made by
X|_{[0,1]}
of size in
[e^{k},e^{k+1}]
is distributed as a Poisson random variable with mean
m_{k}
proportional to
e^{-\alpha k}
.

Let
k_{0}\in\mathbf{Z}
be such that
k\geq k_{0}
if and only if
m_{k}\leq 1/2
. Let
k_{n}
be the largest value of
k\in\mathbf{Z}
so that
m_{k}\geq 2n
. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
, let
E_{k}=\{N_{k}<n\}
. Then if we set

\displaystyle S^{1}=\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}N_{k},\quad S^{2}=\sum_{k=k_{n}}^{k_{0}-1}N_{k},\quad\text{and}\quad S^{3}=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{k_{n}-1}N_{j}{\bf 1}_{E_{j+1}},

we have that
S_{n}\leq S^{1}+S^{2}+S^{3}
.

Step 2: Bound for
\mathbf{E}[S^{1}]
.
As
\alpha>1
,

(C.12)

\mathbf{E}[S^{1}]\leq\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}e^{k+1}m_{k}\asymp\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}e^{(1-\alpha)k}<\infty.

Step 3: Bound for
\mathbf{E}[S^{2}]
.
Note that
k_{n}=-\alpha^{-1}\log n+O(1)
. We thus have that

(C.13)

\mathbf{E}[S^{2}]\leq\sum_{k=k_{n}}^{k_{0}-1}e^{k+1}m_{k}\asymp\sum_{k=k_{n}}^{k_{0}-1}e^{(1-\alpha)k}\asymp n^{1-1/\alpha}.

Step 4: Bound for
\mathbf{E}[S^{3}]
.
Since
k\leq k_{n}
implies that
m_{k}\geq 2n
, by Poisson concentration there exists a constant
c>0
so that

(C.14)

\mathbf{P}[E_{k}]\leq\exp(-cm_{k})\quad\text{for all}\quad k\leq k_{n}.

We thus have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[S^{3}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=-\infty}^{k_{n}-1}e^{k}\mathbf{E}[N_{k}]\mathbf{P}[E_{k+1}]\quad\text{(independence of $N_{k}$ and $E_{k+1}$)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=-\infty}^{k_{n}-1}e^{k}m_{k}e^{-cm_{k}}\quad\text{(by~\eqref{eqn:nksmall})}

(C.15)

\displaystyle<\infty.

The result thus follows by combining (
C.12
), (
C.13
), and (
C.15
).
∎
